L16: ## 1 Recommendations/Risk Benefit Assessment
L17: Following assessment of the clinical data, it is the opinion of this reviewer that incobotulinumtoxinA (NT 201, Xeomin®) is effective for the treatment of Benign Essential Blepharospasm (BEB) when the solubilized powder is used for periorbital intramuscular injection. It has a side effect profile consistent with its class (botulinum neurotoxins or BoNT) and its overall risk to benefit ratio is therapeutically acceptable.
L18: ## 1.1 Recommendation on Regulatory Action
L19: My review of the clinical data finds sufficient evidence to support incobotulinumtoxinA's use in the treatment of Benign Essential Blepharospasm in patients previously successfully treated with onabotulinumtoxinA (BOTOX®). In this submission, the basis of approval is a single double blind, placebo controlled efficacy trial of 20 weeks duration in BEB using a well-accepted design. The dose given was that previously used in the patient's last two BOTOX® treatment sessions, up to 50 units per eye. The primary endpoint was the change from baseline of the severity subscale of the Jankovic Rating Scale for blepharospasm (JRS-S) at six weeks following a single treatment session. While this is the first time that this metric is used to support efficacy in blepharospasm, consistent support come from key secondary measures and a long-term open label extension trial with repeated treatment sessions.
L20: At 6 weeks following treatment in the blinded placebo controlled trial, this review finds that NT 201 was superior to placebo injection in the periorbital muscles (p &lt; 0.0004). The JRS-S score improved (moved negatively) by -0.84 points (95%CI -1.09, -0.58), for the active treatment arm compared to placebo which worsened (moved positively) by 0.21 points (95%CI -0.17, 0.58). In this trial, waning of the therapeutic effect as determined by the need for retreatment occurred after a median of 70 days (95% CI 49, 77). To put this change into clinical perspective, the level of functional disability as measured by a patient reported outcome, the Blepharospasm Disability Index (BDSI) also improved significantly in the active treatment arm (p &lt; 0.0023). The BDSI improved (moved negatively) by -0.48 points (95%CI -0.65, -0.30), compared to the placebo arm which worsened (moved positively) by 0.15 points (95%CI -0.23, 0.26). Secondary measures which included clinical global impressions by the patient and blinded investigator all moved consistently in the direction favoring the active treatment group.
L21: To fulfill entry criteria, the subjects in this trial had had previous successful treatment with BOTOX®. As a result, it is unknown what the magnitude of drug effect might be in a treatment naïve population with blepharospasm. There is no clinical *a priori* evidence to suggest that the response in these two populations would differ greatly. However, potentially poor responders would have been excluded from this trial and drug naïve patients may respond in more vigorous fashion to treatment initiation.
L22: Because the clinical phenomenon of blepharospasm is often found accompanied by other movement disorder phenomena (one third of the subjects in this confirmatory trial had other craniofacial dystonia), it is recommended that an indication be granted for the treatment of blepharospasm as a symptom in addition to, or in place of, the syndrome of BEB.
L23: A double blinded, active control non-inferiority trial compared incobotulinumtoxinA to onabotulinumtoxinA (BOTOX®), (b) (4)
L24: This is due to methodological shortcomings including inadequate justification for the non-inferiority margin and lack of assay sensitivity in the trial.
L25: ## 1.2 Risk Benefit Assessment
L26: After discussion with the DNP safety reviewer, it appears that no new or unexpected adverse events were discovered in the course of the development program for this BoNT. The benefit resulting from treatment combined with a low occurrence rate of severe adverse events or treatment emergent events of a serious nature suggests that incobotulinumtoxinA will be a useful addition to the class of BoNT agents. However, review of the safety data suggests that there is the same possibility of spread of toxin effect, i.e.: unintended paralytic effects in both contiguous and remote parts of the body, even with the lower doses used in the treatment of blepharospasm.
L27: ## 1.3 Recommendations for Postmarket Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies
L28: Because of the risk for unintended treatment effects outside of the targeted muscle, it is proposed that the REMS assigned to other members of the class of botulinum toxins (all serotypes) should also apply to incobotulinumtoxinA. Following discussion with the safety reviewer, no additional REMS requirements appear indicated at this time and may follow closely the evaluation plans and risk mitigation strategies currently in effect for the three marketed BoNT products in the US. This includes a Medication Guide and a Communication Plan (Dear Healthcare Provider letter).
L29: ## 1.4 Recommendations for Postmarket Requirements and Commitments
L30: It is proposed that the sponsor will provide evidence of effectiveness of the REMS Communication Plan and change the plan as necessary to ensure the communication of potential risks to patients and healthcare providers. Although the Sponsor proposes the first assessment at (b) (4) a timetable currently in use with other BoNT suggests assessments at 18 month, 3 years and 7 years following launch. In particular this will include reports of treatment emergent adverse events and errors in use.
L31: Because the syndrome of blepharospasm is found exclusively in adults, no Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA) -based requirement is proposed. After review, a full
L32: waiver was granted by the PREA Subcommittee of the Pediatric Review Committee (PeRC) on February 24, 2010.
L33: ## 2 Introduction and Regulatory Background
L34: Blepharospasm is a focal dystonia characterized by involuntary contraction of the periorbital orbicularis oculi muscles, causing involuntary forced eye closure.
L35: The pathophysiology of this disorder (and the dystonias in general) is not well understood. While this family of movement disorders is generally thought to have their origin in dysfunctional basal ganglia circuits, this has not been proven. Functional MRI and PET studies of the brain reveal numerous cortical and subcortical regions of dysfunction (Hallett et al, Neurology 71: 1275-1282, 2008). A meta-analysis of 83 published series of over 5000 dystonia patients shows the mean age of onset of blepharospasm was 56 years of age. It does not appear to exist in the pediatric population. Family studies suggest that there is likely a genetic relationship among all the focal dystonias (O'Riordan, et al Neurology 63, 1423-1426, 2004).
L36: Prior to the medical use of botulinum neurotoxin, there was no effective medical or surgical treatment of this disorder. In 1989, BOTOX® (then named Oculinum) received FDA approval for blepharospasm, including benign essential blepharospasm and / or VII cranial nerve disorders in patients 12 years or older. (The pediatric safety data came from the strabismus population studied.)
L37: ## 2.1 Product Information
L38: NT 201, the Sponsor's designation of this biological during its development, is a botulinum type A neurotoxin. Its USAN and trade names are incobotulinumtoxinA and Xeomin®, respectively.
L39: NT 201 is a single chain polypeptide of 1296 amino acids, weighing approximately 150 kDa. It is synthesized from a (b) (4) strain of *Clostridium Botulinum*, its bacterial source in nature. The native toxin is part of protein complex with a molecular weight of approximately 900kDa. (b) (4)
L40: The drug product is supplied as a lyophilized powder for reconstitution with commercially available 0.9% physiological saline. Each vial of powder contains 50 or 100 units with strength equal to the LD50 in a mouse assay. Sucrose and human serum albumin are excipients.
L41: Its intended use in this BLA is in adults who suffer with benign essential blepharospasm. The Sponsor proposed labeling indicating intramuscular injection of no more than (b) (4) units per eye per treatment session into the periorbital muscles. Interval between treatment sessions was not specified. (b) (4)
L42: (b) (4)
L43: (b) (4)
L44: ## 2.2 Tables of Currently Available Treatments for Proposed Indications
L45: In the US, three Injectable botulinum neurotoxin (BoNT) products are available. Even though only one is approved for use in blepharospasm, all BoNTs are listed due to the extensive off label use these products enjoy.

L48: ## 2.3 Availability of Proposed Active Ingredient in the United States
L49: This version of botulinum toxin A is not currently marketed in the United States but is marketed elsewhere. Merz Pharmaceuticals GmbH is the license holder for NT 201, marketed under the trade name Xeomin®. It was first approved in Germany in May 31, 2005 and the EU in October 2007. It is now approved in 13 EU countries as well as Argentina, Canada, Korea, Mexico, Russia and Uruguay.
L50: It is approved for the treatment of blepharospasm, cervical dystonia (spasmodic torticollis), post stroke spasticity of the upper limb, and glabellar frown lines. For this last indication, it is marketed under the trade name Bocouture®.
L51: The sixth PSUR covering the period up to July 1, 2009 has been submitted. The post marketing experience with Xeomin® is reviewed further in **Section 8**.
L52: ## 2.4 Important Safety Issues With Consideration to Related Drugs
L53: Because of the potential for serious toxicity, the class of botulinum toxin agents is subject to special scrutiny. Two specific issues are of concern:
L54: The first is the potential for the spread of toxicity to both contiguous and distant tissues, with a potentially fatal outcome.
L55: The second is that biological proteins inevitably arouse some immunological response in the host. In humans, this response produces blocking antibodies which may be associated with the development of treatment resistant symptoms in some patients.
L56: These are considered further in **Section 7.3.5 Submission Specific Safety Concerns**.
L57: ## 2.5 Summary of Presubmission Regulatory Activity Related to Submission
L58: A variety of regulatory interactions between the division and the Sponsor leading to this submission have taken place over the years.
L59: - IND 12,821 for the treatment of cervical dystonia was submitted on December 1, 2005 and was released from clinical hold on June 29, 2006.
- BB-IND 100,163 was submitted May 3, 2006 for the indication of blepharospasm and was also allowed to proceed on June 29, 2006.
L60: (b) (4)
L61: Specific regulatory opinions related to areas other than the indication of blepharospasm are not covered here and the reader is referred to the primary reviews in these other disciplines.
L62: ## 2.5.1 Pre-IND / End of Phase II Meeting – September 14, 2004
L63: A Type B meeting was held to discuss the pre Phase III state of the Xeomin® development program, which had performed outside the US. The Sponsor submitted a pre-meeting package of progress to date and 14 questions on the topics of preclinical, clinical, and drug quality. The aim of the meeting was to obtain agreement on the acceptability of the clinical development plan. An FDA memorandum dated October 12, 2004 documents the Division's responses. The Division indicated that the proposed nonclinical program was adequate to support the Phase III trial and the BLA submission, subject to review.
L64: The Sponsor had conducted a Phase 3 trial for blepharospasm in Europe in support of the BLA. The FDA noted that the Sponsor would need to establish "substantial evidence" of effectiveness in order to support a marketing application in the U.S., which can be interpreted to mean more than one adequate and well-controlled investigation that provides "independent substantiation" of experimental results. The Division pointed out the requirements for, and shortcomings of, non-inferiority trials. Because a reliable effect size can not be established due to robust and unpredictable placebo response, and the lack of adequate historical data concerning the active comparator, this type of trial is not considered confirmatory. The Sponsor acknowledged their understanding of this problem.
L65: The Division also indicated that "dose ranging information is valuable for writing an adequate label to guide initial dosing of toxin-naive patients [with blepharospasm]. If a dose-ranging design without a placebo group is being contemplated, then the Sponsor should bear in mind that it is important to evaluate a broad enough range of doses such that differences can be demonstrated across the range of doses examined. That is, the lowest dose evaluated should have low efficacy compared to the higher doses, to permit statistical comparisons to succeed."
L66: The Division also indicated that clinical issues (some of these above) which may have been discussed in the context of cervical dystonia, apply equally to blepharospasm. The supporting clinical studies and proposed pivotal protocols were relying primarily on subjects that had responded to BOTOX® therapy. "Reliance on a select responder population generally does not adequately support a claim of safety and efficacy in a more heterogeneous clinical population of patients with [blepharospasm]." The Agency noted the Sponsor's concern regarding limiting the placebo period in order to minimize subject dropouts and allow for early re-injection of patients. The Agency concurred that an extensive placebo period may be problematic for patients. "However, it is highly desirable to develop adequate placebo- controlled safety data for NT-201 (which is not available from the previous clinical investigations) and to measure the NT-201 duration of effect in [blepharospasm], if possible. For these reasons, the Agency strongly recommends extending the placebo period [REDACTED] (b) (4) in the second study synopsis, preferably on the order of 6-8 weeks. FDA explained that the sponsor would need to establish evidence of effectiveness in blepharospasm
L67: specifically and that studying a BOTOX®-naive population (to the extent possible), would be important.”
L68: (b) (4)
L69: The Agency recommends that you discuss your assay method with our Office of Biological Products to determine if there is a more efficient two-step assay (e.g., ELISA + Mouse Hemi diaphragm study) that will allow you to evaluate all clinical samples for immunogenic responses to NT-201. Having a more complete immunologic profile will be important for writing an adequate label for your product.”
L70: Specific ECG cardiac and pulmonary evaluations were not considered to be required unless particular concerns arose. However it was emphasized that there should be specific and frequent evaluation for adverse events related to collateral spread of NT201 in these and other areas of adjacent muscles: dyspnea, respiratory infections, dysphagia, speech problems, facial weakness, general body weakness, and so forth. Adverse events were to be followed until they stabilize or resolve. They were to be elicited for up to 4 weeks following the last injection.
L71: Given that blepharospasm is a chronic condition, the Agency wished to see evidence of retreatment efficacy sustained over time, approaching the requirements listed in ICH recommendations.
L72: Further comments were made about the Statistical Analysis Plan, and the need for strict definition of analysis groups, methods, and assumptions. Use of an independent DSMB was strongly recommended.
L73: ## 2.5.2 Release from Clinical Hold - June 29, 2006
L74: The IND for study of blepharospasm (as well as cervical dystonia) had been put on clinical hold because of a lack of non-clinical information needed to support the human clinical trials. This information was submitted and discussed with the Division and the clinical hold ended on June 29, 2006.
L75: The following non-hold comments were directed to the Sponsor at that time with a request for comments (verbatim):
L76: 1. Your protocol does not specify a standardized method for eliciting adverse event reports. In the absence of a standardized approach, ascertainment of adverse events is likely to be substantially incomplete and may vary substantially among centers, confounding the interpretation of the study results. We recommend that you revise the protocol to specify a standardized method of eliciting adverse event reports. This standardized method should include the following:
L77: - an open-ended question to elicit reports of unanticipated adverse events
- a set of questions directed at adverse events of predetermined concern (including common adverse events such as ptosis, diplopia, and xerophthalmia)
- directed questioning for gastrointestinal adverse events
L78: 2. Your protocol does not provide details of the methods for administering and scoring the Jankovic Rating Scale (JRS) severity subscore, the primary outcome variable. The absence of appropriate, detailed, standardized testing procedures may increase the risk of high variability among centers and biased results, confounding the interpretation of the study results. Also, in the absence of specified testing procedures, we are not able to assess the appropriateness of your procedures for administering and scoring the JRS. Therefore, we recommend that you develop detailed procedures for administering the JRS and submit those procedures for review.
L79: 3. Your protocol specifies a "recommended order" for assessment of the Blepharospasm Disability Index (BSDI) and JRS during clinic visits. Investigators may interpret a "recommended order" as implying substantial latitude, resulting in high variability in the order of the assessments. Such variability may confound the study results. To decrease the risk of such variability, we recommend that you revise the protocol to specify a standard order that you expect all investigators to follow for all outcome assessments.
L80: 4. Some of your patient-reported outcome measures include terms that may be unfamiliar to patients. For example, the JRS contains terms such as 'stimuli' and 'spasm' that should not generally be used in a patient interview (see comment #2 above, regarding the need for detailed, standardized testing procedures) and the BSDI contains terms such as 'involuntary.' Patients may misinterpret these terms, increasing the variability of the study results, and confounding the interpretation of the study results. To decrease the risk of such variability, we recommend that you review the patient-reported outcome indices to ensure that they are at a reading level appropriate for patients, as discussed in the FDA Guidance for Industry, Patient-Reported Outcome Measures: Use in Medical Product Development to Support Labeling Claims (http://www.fda.gov/Cder/guidance/5460dft.pdf).
L81: 5. Your protocol does not include a "responder analysis" for the primary efficacy endpoint. A "responder analysis" can be helpful to assess the clinical importance of a
L82: study result. We recommend that you revise the protocol to include a "responder analysis" as a sensitivity analysis of the primary efficacy endpoint.
L83: ## 2.5.2 Type C Meeting - June 11, 2007
L84: A Type C meeting was held on June 11, 2007. The clinical substance of that meeting related to this indication is taken from the minutes with the questions posed by the Sponsor, followed by the Agency's response and any discussion that took place (edited for brevity but not content):
L85: (b) (4)

L88: ## Meeting Discussion covering Questions 1-4
L89: a) [The Sponsor] agreed to follow the Agency's advice to conduct a study on 75 treatment-naïve blepharospasm patients, in addition to the study of pretreated blepharospasm patients. The treatment-naïve study will consist of a placebo arm and two fixed dose arms. The Division agreed that the treatment-naïve study could be for supportive purposes, and that it need not be powered to show statistical superiority of active drug.
L90: The Division agreed that the two studies in blepharospasm (study of treatment naïve patients and study of pretreated patients) together with a second pivotal study in another indication (cervical dystonia or glabellar lines) appeared capable of fulfilling the requirement for two adequate and well controlled trials necessary for a BLA for blepharospasm.
L91: b) The Sponsor explained the difficulty of recruiting treatment-naïve blepharospasm subjects. Many patients with blepharospasm have been previously treated with botulinum toxin for other indications, such as glabellar lines. The sponsor further noted that it was not unusual for blepharospasm patients to have a history of previous undertreatment with botulinum toxin. The sponsor estimated that in the U.S. only 1,000- to 2,000 patients were diagnosed with blepharospasm each year. The Division agreed that "treatment naïve" patients could be defined as patients that had not been treated with botulinum toxin type A for the indication of blepharospasm for one year. Patients treated for indications other than blepharospasm within one year would still be considered "treatment naïve" in terms of blepharospasm.
L92: The Division expressed concern that the treatment-naïve study population should reflect the type of treatment-naïve patients that would be encountered in clinical practice, including patients with no previous exposure to botulinum toxin. The sponsor estimated that 20% to 30% of those enrolled in the treatment-naïve blepharospasm study would have been treated with botulinum toxin at some time in the past. The sponsor agreed to submit to FDA an explanation of their efforts to enroll treatment-naïve blepharospasm subjects, and their rationale for enrolling patients with some previous exposure to botulinum toxin.

Questions were also directed toward the indication of spasticity, though it should be noted that this indication was not studied under an IND in the US. The questions posed were only indirectly related to the blepharospasm indication. Agreement was reached concerned the content and presentation of summary reports in the eCTD. The requirements for long term exposure were outlined, and the requirements suggested for spasticity and cervical dystonia were also adequate for the blepharospasm indication. Agreement was reached concerning the pooling of adverse events datasets for individual indications as well as the entire trial-exposed population.

L101: ## 2.6 Other Relevant Background Information
L102: This BLA is being evaluated in parallel to a related submission for the treatment of cervical dystonia designated BLA 125360 / 0. A separate safety review encompassing the clinical experience for all indications is being performed by the Division of Neurology Products. This is part of an ongoing assessment of safety related to all botulinum neurotoxin products.
L103: There have been reports of systemic adverse drug reactions remote from the injection site including respiratory compromise and death following the use of botulinum toxins A and B for both FDA-approved and unapproved uses. The most serious reactions occurred mostly in children treated for cerebral palsy-associated limb spasticity. Following review of post-marketing data and case reports, class labeling was changed to reflect the seriousness of these events.
L104: In 2009, Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS) were approved for onabotulinumtoxinA (BOTOX®/Botox Cosmetic, BLA 103000/5189), rimabotulinumtoxinB (Myobloc®, BLA 103846/5120) and abobotulinumtoxinA (Dysport®, BLA 125274/0 and /1). The REMS created a communication plan and revised prescribing information including a Boxed Warning and a Medication Guide highlighting the risk, signs and symptoms of drug effect remote from the site of injection. In addition, established USAN names were revised for each botulinum toxin product to reinforce the understanding that each product has an individual potency and is not interchangeable with any other BoNT product.

Timeliness of the reporting of all adverse events was checked. Both study monitors and investigators underwent appropriate training for their respective tasks. L115: ## 4 Significant Efficacy/Safety Issues Related to Other Review Disciplines
L116: ## 4.1 Chemistry Manufacturing and Controls
L117: The actual drug substance (designated (b) (4) by the sponsor) is synthesized by *Clostridium botulinum* in a fermentation process. Each batch is the product of a (b) (4). It is run on a scale of (b) (4) which produces (b) (4).
L118: of purified drug substance, in a dedicated facility. The sponsor anticipates that (b) (4) will create adequate drug supply.
L119: (b) (4) is manufactured into NT 201 by a (b) (4) process of formulation with sucrose, (b) (4) and human serum albumin before packing into vials for lyophilization.
L120: At the time of completion of this review there are still unresolved issues with regard to CMC process, including cleaning of the production equipment and adequate sealing of drug product filled vials. The reader is referred to the CMC review for specific analysis of the issues related to the manufacture of the drug product and creation of NT 201 in final form.
L121: ## 4.2 Clinical Microbiology
L122: A master cell bank of the clostridia colony is maintained for the creation of working cell banks. A single WCB is used to generate each batch of (b) (4). Microbacterial criteria for purity and testing of the product and its packaging have been established by the sponsor but await verification.
L123: The reader is referred to the Microbiology review for specific analysis of the problems related to the manufacture of the drug product and creation of NT 201 in final form.
L124: ## 4.3 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L125: Nonclinical studies reveal no obvious issues specific to NT 201 that are not related to BoNTs as a class. As expected from a pure cholinergic blocker, *in vitro* hERG channel testing revealed no effect of NT201 at a concentration of 10,000 LDU/mL and no effect on ECG in monkeys.
L126: Most BoNTs, whether free or in protein complexes, do not diffuse from the injection site in any great quantity. While local anatomy and tissue type determines the diffusion capability, a small amount of toxin diffuses into local tissue and then into the blood stream. It has been demonstrated that diffusion could be considerable, e.g.: 5 cm in a rabbit (Borodic, et al: Head and Neck 12: 392-398, 1990). Single fiber EMG has detected electrophysiological changes in the extensor digitalis brevis (EDB) muscle in the foot of subjects who received BoNT injection in the head/neck region (Garner, et al: Mov Dis 8(1) 33-37, 1993).
L127: ## 4.4 Clinical Pharmacology
L128: The nature of botulinum toxins and their inherent toxicity limits the possibilities for studying their pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic profiles in humans.
L129: The Sponsor did perform three Phase I trials in healthy volunteers to compare the action of Xeomin® to onabotulinumtoxinA (BOTOX®), a currently marketed biologic in the US.

L134: ## 4.4.1 Mechanism of Action
L135: Botulinum neurotoxins all act by targeting the same cellular vesicle fusion machinery. The single polypeptide chain is activated endogenously by proteases which cleave the protein into a 100kDa heavy (H) chain and 50 kDa light (L) chain linked by a disulfide bridge. This is the biologically active form of the agent.
L136: The toxicity of BoNT is a result of a multistep mechanism. The neurotoxin binds to the pre-synaptic nerve terminals of cholinergic neurons and enters by receptor-mediated endocytosis. The carboxyl terminal of the H chain binds to the target, while the amino terminal interacts with the L chain, a zinc dependent metalloprotease, and translocates internally into the neuron (Lacy, et al. Nat Struct Biol 5 (10) 898-902, 1998). This zinc-dependent catalytic domain is common to all BoNT serotypes and specifically attacks the vesicle machinery in the neuron necessary to release packets of neurotransmitter into the synaptic cleft. It does this by cleaving one of three different SNARE proteins essential for synaptic vesicle fusion: synaptobrevin, syntaxin, or SNAP-25. This cleavage results in inhibition of acetylcholine secretion, ultimately leading to paralysis. Serotype A has the longest paralyzing effect of the seven immunologically distinct serotypes (Davletov, et al, TINS 28 (8) 446-452, 2005). The cartoon below from the TINS article illustrates these steps from (i) where it hijacks the cellular machinery to enter the cell to (iii) where it blocks fusion of the vesicle for release of neurotransmitter.
L137: ## **Figure 1: BoNT Mechanism of Action**
## **Copyright Material**
L138: This ability to paralyze muscular contraction makes BoNTs useful for the treatment of disorders where small amounts of localized muscular paralysis may aid function. These disorders include the focal dystonias and spasticity, both neurological syndromes in which alterations of posture and tone result from abnormal contraction resulting from muscular dyscontrol by different central nervous system circuits.
L139: ## 4.4.2 Pharmacodynamics
L140: Two Phase I trials compared the action of NT 201 to BOTOX® in healthy volunteers. The first trial (BTC 60201-9901) compared a single intramuscular injection of 4 units of NT 201 and BOTOX® into contralateral extensor digitorum brevis (EDB) muscles on the lateral aspect of the foot in 14 normal men. The outcome parameter was the compound muscle action potential (CMAP) of surface electromyography (EMG). It was measured for time to onset of paralytic effect (CMAP \le 70% of baseline) and the duration of the effect. 50% of subjects responded in a day to either treatment and over the course of observation time the response to either BoNT was not statistically different. The time course for duration of effect was also not distinguishable. This study (and the figure below) has been published: Jost, et al., J Neural Transmission 112: 905-913, 2005.
L141: Figure 2: Percent of EDB CMAP decrement following BoNT injection
L142: ## Copyright Material
L143: The second trial used a similar methodology for a single injection ascending dose trial in 32 healthy men. A single dose of 2, 4, 16 or 32 units of NT 201 and ® were injected intramuscularly into contralateral EDBs. The patients were then observed at intervals over 52 weeks. The effect of paralysis on CMAP at 4 weeks after injection was the outcome parameter, as well as muscle power of the toes. Twelve subjects were randomization failures (6 found before and 6 after unblinding). The nature of these failures is unclear from the sponsors report but analysis was performed on data from 26 subjects.
L144: At Week 4, all dose groups of both BoNT products had significant paralytic effect by the CMAP measure. This difference was larger for the 32 unit vs. 2 unit dose groups for both products, but no other differences were seen. The duration of effect was also not statistically significantly different. This trial has also been published: Wohlfarth, et al., Clin Neuropharmacol 30: 86-94, 2007. This paper is the source of the next two figures. The first illustrates the primary efficacy parameter: decrement in CMAP at 4 weeks and the plot shows median, minimum, maximum and 25% and 75% quartiles. The second figure shows the duration of paretic effect by time (weeks) to return to 80% of baseline EDB CMAP.
L145: **Figure 3: Primary efficacy parameter: decrement in CMAP at 4 weeks**
L146: **Copyright Material**
L147: **Figure 4: Duration of paretic effect; weeks to return to 80% of baseline**
L148: **Copyright Material**
L149: An important element of pharmacodynamic understanding of potential toxicity of Xeomin® has to do with the ability of a botulinum toxin product to have an effect at a distance from the injection site. While objective safety data will come from the adequate and well controlled trials, the Sponsor looked at this potential in a Phase I trial:
L150: *Trial MRZ 60201-0709/1, Double-blind, randomized, single-dose, single-center study with healthy female subjects to evaluate the migration potential of Xeomin® as compared to two other botulinum toxin type A products Vistabel® and Dysport®*
L151: This trial was conducted at a single center in Germany in 2007 – 2008 and enrolled 29 women. They were randomized to receive two separate injections from a panel of three different BoNT A products, randomized 1: 1: 1, so that the final tally consisted of equal numbers of injections of each product. The botulinum toxin A products, NT 201, Vistabel (EU trade name for BOTOX®, onabotulinumtoxinA) and Dysport® (abobotulinumtoxinA) were injected intramuscularly into the forehead and the resulting area of anhydrosis was recorded photographically over time. Each individual was treated with two different medications (one to each side of the forehead), dosed as 5 units each of NT 201 and Vistabel and 12.5 units of Dysport® in equal volumes.
L152: The rationale for the trial was that the botulinum toxin injected intramuscularly spreads into surrounding tissue such as skin. Cholinergic-innervated skin structures i.e.: sweat glands are affected by the toxin. This model makes use of the effect of botulinum toxin on sweat glands in the skin of the forehead by measuring the area of anhydrosis around the injection site. Outcome measurement were made frequently over a 6 month period but the outcome the plan specified was a photographic result of the Minor's starch / iodine sweat test blindly read by a dermatologist and image analysis recorded at 6 weeks and 6 months post injection. 29 subjects were enrolled, 1 subject was discontinued for having missed both evaluation days, and 23 completed the protocol and were evaluable.
L153: It is of interest that the Sponsor reports: "In 6 subjects there were incomplete or implausible (area of anhydrosis was too diffuse so that no clear border could be defined) results in the Minor test on right or left side of the forehead [between days 15 and 29]... As these incomplete or implausible data fell into the time of expected maximum of area of anhydrosis they were regarded as major protocol deviations," and excluded from the Per Protocol Set (PPS) analysis. It is not known whether these were relatively larger or smaller areas.
L154: Maximal area of anhydrosis (mm2) at 6 weeks post injection (Per Protocol Set)

L159: Looking the size of the anhydrotic area for Xeomin® by subject over the timeframe of the entire trial shows that early after injection there is a larger area in general that decreases with time. This is true of the other preparations as well.

L162: A major flaw of the trial is that there is no way of knowing true dose equivalency for these biologics: the data does not exist. There has been some modeling of dose equivalency based on non-clinical data and presumably the chosen doses are based on similar work (Aoki, et al. Eur J Neurol 13 (Suppl 4):10-19, 2006). The reviewer wonders if the excluded subjects were anomalous as the Sponsor thought, or at one end of the diffusion spectrum. It is not possible to draw any firm conclusions about diffusion of BoNT from this trial.
L163: ## 4.4.3 Pharmacokinetics
L164: Kinetic studies of Xeomin® have not been performed. If there is systemic absorption of NT 201 (and BoNT products in general), it is too small to be detected with available assay methods.

L174: ## 5.2 Review Strategy
L175: The review strategy focuses upon the following areas and their supporting trials:
L176: - Does NT 201 act like a botulinum toxin is expected to act in human muscle?Phase I trials reviewed in **Section 4.4 Clinical Pharmacology**, above
- Is NT 201 effective in reversing the symptoms and disability of blepharospasm?Trial MRZ 60201 – 0433/1 Randomized, double blind, placebo controlled confirmatory trial in blepharospasm.
- How durable is the clinical effect of NT 201 upon blepharospasm?Trial MRZ 60201 – 0433/2 One year open label treatment extension to the double blind placebo controlled trial.
- How does NT 201 compare to another botulinum toxin A product which approved for the treatment of blepharospasm?Trial MRZ 60201 – 0003 Randomized, double blinded, active control, non-inferiority trial (NT201 vs. BOTOX®)
L177: *Reviewer's Note: It should be noted, as will become evident as the trial populations are discussed, no BoNT treatment naïve subjects with blepharospasm were enrolled in this development program.*
L178: Because of safety concerns specific to botulinum toxins, and because human experience with NT 201 was derived from three different disease entities with a wide range of dosing regimens (blepharospasm, cervical dystonia and spasticity), this BLA submission is the subject of separate comprehensive safety analysis created within DNP. The reader is referred to that document in lieu of Section 7 in this document.
L179: However, this reviewer does comment on two particular safety issues also related to clinical efficacy, i.e.: evidence for the spread of toxin effect from the injection site past the target muscles and immunogenicity. For this reason, **Section 7.3.5 Submission Specific Primary Safety Concerns** and **Section 7.4.6 Immunogenicity** are retained.
L180: ## 5.3 Discussion of Individual Studies/Clinical Trials
L181: ## 5.3.1 Confirmatory trial of NT 201 for the treatment of blepharospasm (MRZ 60201-0433/1)
L182: ## Trial
L183: Prospective, double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized, multi-center trial with an open-label extension period to investigate the efficacy and safety of NT 201 in the treatment of blepharospasm (MRZ 60201-0433/1)
L184: ## Phase III
L185: ## Purpose
L186: The objective of this trial was to determine the efficacy, safety and tolerability of a single injection of NT 201 compared to placebo when injected intramuscularly in patients with benign essential blepharospasm (BEB). All subjects had had a satisfactory therapeutic response to BOTOX® injection on at least two previous occasions.
L187: This multicenter trial randomly assigned subjects to NT 201 or placebo in a 2:1 ratio. A single injection session was administered. Subjects received up to 50 U of NT 201 per eye, the total administered dose being equivalent to the total dose of BOTOX® given at the last two treatments prior to enrollment. The evaluation period lasted at least 6 weeks but no more than 21 weeks.
L188: A long term, open label extension trial covering further treatment with a maximum of 5 injection sessions for up to 48 weeks, with 20 weeks of observation following the final injection, allowed further safety observations. This is described separately in **Section 5.3.2 Open label repeated injection trial for the treatment of blepharospasm.**
L189: Both trial periods were monitored by a Data Safety Monitoring Board (DSMB).
L190: The primary objective of the study was to measure the relief of blepharospasm after a single session of peri-orbital injections of NT 201 using a maximum dose up to 50 U per eye.
L191: The primary outcome measure used to evaluate this is the change from base line of the Jankovic Rating Scale blepharospasm severity subscore (JRS-S), as assessed by a blinded rater, six weeks following NT 201 administration. Secondary measures include the same JRS-S rating by patient diary, change in Blepharospasm Disability Index (BSDI) six weeks after administration, and the Patient Evaluation of Global Response (PEGR) at the final visit, up to 20 weeks following drug administration.
L192: ## Trial design
L193: The trial, using a prospective, double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized, multi-center design in 34 centers in the US and Canada, began enrolling October 9, 2006 and the last subject completed the blinded portion of the trial on May 28, 2008. Thirty three clinical sites were initiated and 31 sites enrolled patients. The open label extension period is ongoing.
L194: Figure 6: Design of Trial 0433/1 (source: Sponsor)
L195: The trial's protocol and statistical analysis plan presented the following procedures, outcomes and endpoints, and proposed analyses:
L196: ## Clinical Assessment:
L197: Two investigators were used at each site. One investigator was in charge of all aspects of the study except for the Jankovic Rating Scale (JRS) scoring, which was performed by the “blinded independent investigator.” For the sake of consistency, the BSDI had to be administered before the JRS was rated. Investigators who rated the JRS were trained and certified.
L198: ## Primary endpoint:
L199: - **Jankovic Rating Scale (Severity Subscale):** The JRS is a two item, 0-4 rating scale with anchored cut-points for severity of blepharospasm and its frequency. The primary statistical analysis is the change from baseline (Visit 2) to week 6 (Visit 4). The dependent variable in the ANCOVA model is the change from baseline in the JRS Severity subscale score assessed by the blinded independent investigator and the independent variables are treatment, baseline JRS severity subscore, sex, age, dose and trial center.
L200: JRS is determined by interview between the rater and the patient. The patient reports their symptoms for the prior 3 days and judge the average severity and frequency, which is recorded by the rater.
L201: ## Secondary criteria:
L202: - **JRS Severity Subscale Diary:** The median value of the diary (a patient self-rated JRS-S) for the seven days prior to visit will be used in an analysis of covariance. The initial self-assessment is made in the week prior to the baseline injection, with the endpoint value taken from diaries prior to week 6 (Visit 4).
- **Blepharospasm Disability Index:** The BDI is a self-rated scale for the assessment of impairment of specific activities of daily living caused by BEB. The scale consists of 6 items rated 0-4 with anchored cut-points, plus a choice of "not applicable to me." The 6 items are "Driving a vehicle", "Reading", "Watching TV", "Shopping", "Walking" and "Doing everyday activities".
- **Patient Evaluation of Global Response:** The PEGR is a single descriptive and subjective response to a self-rated scale ranging from "complete abolishment of signs and symptoms" (+4) down to "very marked worsening" (-4).
- **Global Assessment of Efficacy:** The investigator performed a single global assessment of the efficacy of study medication at the final visit of the double blinded phase of the trial ("Main Period"). The patient was rated on a 4-point Likert scale, ranging from "very good", "good", "moderate", to "poor".
L203: ## Secondary time-based measurements (duration of effect):
L204: - **Time to Onset of Treatment Effect after Injection (subjective assessment)**
Time to onset of effect was defined in weeks from the day of injection (Day 0). The patient was queried at each visit and telephone contact up to Visit 4 (Week 6, 42 \pm 3 days). If the patient answered yes they were asked what day they noted the effect. An amendment to the Sponsor's SAP added that, for subjects without any reported onset of treatment effect through the trial period, the time to onset of effect will be calculated to the last visit of the trial period and censored.
L205: - Time to Waning of Treatment Effect after Injection (subjective assessment)
Time to waning of effect was similarly defined in weeks from the day of injection. However no inquiry was made unless the patient was one who reported an onset of treatment effect. In this case they were asked about waning of effect at Week 3, at the earliest. An amendment to the Sponsor's SAP added that, for subjects without any reported onset of treatment effect until end of the trial period, the time to waning of effect will be set to zero weeks.
L206: - Duration of Treatment Effect
For all patients who experienced a treatment effect, the duration was defined as the time period from day of injection (Day 0) until the time point for the "agreed upon" day of retreatment. This day (which ends the blinded portion of this trial for that subject) was determined by the evaluation of the JRS severity subscore assessed by the blinded investigator (score must be \ge 2).
L207: If a patient did not experience a treatment effect, the duration of treatment effect is set to 0 days.
L208: ## Safety measurements:
L209: Adverse Events were defined for the purposes of recording, documentation, definition of intensity, action taken and outcome. All patients who had an AE were monitored until it resolved or stabilized. The minimum period of AE related observation was defined as 20 weeks \pm 3 days, or until the next injection of any BoNT. Serious Adverse Events (SAEs) were defined in the protocol using standard criteria. Reports went to the DSMB as well as the Sponsor within 24 h.
L210: Adverse events of special interest included symptoms such as ptosis, abnormal vision, dry mouth, dysphagia, speech problems, dyspnea, respiratory infections, local weakness, facial weakness, and general body weakness which are considered to be signs of local or systemic toxin spread. These symptoms and all other signs and symptoms, which the Investigator considered to indicate toxin spread, were to be recorded as an AE and reported within 7 days to the CRO.
L211: Other safety variables included vital signs, body weight, physical and neurological examination, electrocardiogram, a global assessment of tolerability and laboratory parameters (pregnancy testing, clinical chemistry, hematology and serum for BoNT antibodies).
L212: Blood samples for botulinum neurotoxin antibodies are obtained at the screening visit, Visit 4 (week 6) and the final visit of the double blind period (Visit 5, up to 20 weeks). Sampling extends into the open label period at all injection visits and the final visit of the open label trial. Measurement is done by ELISA and positive samples will be confirmed using the mouse ex vivo hemidiaphragm assay. The assay is unable to discriminate between neutralizing and non-neutralizing antibodies.
L213: ## Key Inclusion Criteria
L214: - Male or female outpatients aged 18 to 80 years (inclusive) previously treated with BOTOX®
- Clinical diagnosis of bilateral BEB
- Jankovic Rating Scale Severity (JRS-S) subscore \ge 2 at baseline
- Source documentation of the last two consecutive injection sessions with BOTOX® and a stable satisfactory therapeutic response directly prior to trial entry.
- At least 10 weeks must have been passed between the last injection with BOTOX® for BEB and baseline.
L215: ## Key Exclusion Criteria
L216: - Atypical variant of BEB caused by inhibition of levator palpebrae muscle
- Myotomy or denervation surgery in the affected muscles (e.g., peripheral denervation and/or spinal cord stimulation)
- The two injections prior to enrollment using more than 50 U BOTOX® per eye
- Hypersensitivity to human serum albumin, sucrose, or Botulinum toxin Type A
- Neuroleptic induced blepharospasm
- Diagnosis of myasthenia gravis, Lambert-Eaton syndrome, amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, or any other significant neuromuscular disease which might interfere with the trial
- Treatment with Botulinum toxins for any indication other than BEB within 4 months prior to baseline and during the trial
- Present or pre-existing blood coagulation disorders including therapeutic or prophylactic anticoagulation
- Severe hearing loss.
L217: ## Concomitant Medication
L218: Any medication not related to the treatment of BEB were allowed and recorded. Anti depressants, opioid analgesics, non-steroidal anti-inflammatory agents, anticholinergics and benzodiazepines were allowed if taken in stable doses for at least 3 months prior to entry into the study and maintained through the evaluation period.
L219: Other drugs active at the neuromuscular junction were to be "avoided": neuroleptics, verapamil, dantrolene, quinine and peripheral muscle relaxants.
L220: The following were contraindicated: botulinum toxin of any other serotype, phenol or alcohol injection in the part of the body under study, intrathecal baclofen, aminoglycoside antibiotics, tetracycline, polymixin, aminoquinolones, and anesthetic agents including locals.

L224: - The subject was to be questioned every 2 weeks, either by phone or at a visit, about the need of an injection.
- The interval between two visits was never to exceed 4 weeks.

L225: The following applies to the optional phase:
L226: ## Treatments and Ancillary Management:
L227: NT201 and placebo were provided as sterile lyophilized powder reconstituted with 0.9% saline and then injected. Sucrose and human albumin were the excipients used in the placebo vial. One unit of NT 210 corresponds to the calculated median lethal dose in mice (LD50 i.p.).
L228: At the baseline visit (Visit 2), each subject received one dose of NT201 or placebo distributed among several injection points in both eyes. Highest dose permitted was 50 Units per eye. The treatment in both groups was to be identical (i.e.: total dose, volume, dilution, injection points per muscle, doses at each site) to what they had received in the previous two botulinum toxin sessions before entering the study. On the CRF volumes were recorded according to the following schema for documentation:
L229: Figure 7: Case Report Form Injection site identification (source: Sponsor)
L230: ## Injection Documentation
L231: Warning symbol: exclamation mark inside a triangle.
L232: Please mark and identify injection points with numbers.
L233: The subject was injected only once in the blinded portion of this trial. Any new injection of NT 201 after this time automatically marked entry into the open label extension trial.
L234: ## Randomization and Controls:
L235: Subjects were randomized to NT-201 versus placebo in a 2 : 1 ratio at Visit 2. Block randomization was used but block size (3) was kept secret. The DSMB held a sealed coded list of randomized subjects.
L236: Because blepharospasm is more prevalent in women, it was expected to have a female preponderance in the study population.
L237: ## GCP:
L238: See also **Section 3.2** above. Merz performed an internal audit of a single clinical site #1019, six subjects enrolled. They found no evidence of noncompliance with GCP.
L239: ## Protocol Amendments:
L240: (The reviewer selected the substantive changes)
L241: ## Amendment 1 (August 1, 2006, prior to the start of the study):
L242: - The site personnel were directed how to ask patients for adverse events. This served to standardize the Adverse Event elicitation.
- The sequence of the assessments, i.e. first the Blepharospasm Disability Index (BSDI) and then the Jankovic Rating Scale (JRS), was defined as “mandatory” instead of “recommended” only. This served to avoid variability in the sequence of these two assessments.
- A further sensitivity analysis on the primary efficacy endpoint was defined in the protocol: “Further sensitivity analyses of the primary efficacy parameter will be based on the comparison of response rates (OR) at Control Visit V4 (Week 6) between the treatment groups. Responders are defined as patients with an improvement of at least 1 point in the JRS severity subscore compared to the Baseline Visit (V2, Day 0)... Comparisons between the two treatment groups will be performed using logistic regression models. The dependent variable is the response to treatment (0 = no, 1 = yes) and the independent variables are identical to the independent variables in the ANCOVA models for the primary efficacy variable. Again, three models will be presented: 1) full model with all adjusting variables, 2) final model with all adjusting variables with p &lt; 0.2 (backward selection), and 3) simple model including the treatment effect only. The same missing replacement strategies as for the primary efficacy variable will be applied to the JRS severity data before responder determination.”
L243: ## Amendment 2 (March 26, 2007, 5 months after the first enrollment):
L244: - The upper limit of age of patients allowed was increased to 80 years of age. The reason was that the blepharospasm population turned out to be on average of higher
L245: age than initially expected, as assessed during screening procedures at several sites.
L246: - The exclusion criteria "severe hardness of hearing" was added to avoid inclusion of elderly patients who are not able to handle the patient telephone diary with interactive voice recognition and response system.
- Patients with other facial dystonia were allowed to be enrolled, provided the symptoms did not interfere with blepharospasm and did not need treatment with other botulinum toxins during the study participation.
- The requirement for stable (i.e.: identical) injection schemes of the two previous injections prior to study entry was changed by adding tolerance ranges of allowed deviations (\pm 10\%). Consequently the wording requesting "identical" or "same" injections was changed to "similar".
L247: ## Method of analysis for efficacy (exposure / response):
L248: The Sponsor defined three populations:
L249: ## - Evaluable for Safety Population [EFS]:
L250: The EFS Population includes all patients who received trial medication.
L251: ## - Intent-To-Treat Population [ITT]:
L252: All randomized patients are included in the analyses of each efficacy outcome. This group of patients, defined as the ITT Population, is the basis for confirmatory testing.
L253: ## - Treated-Per-Protocol Population [TPP]:
L254: The TPP Population is defined as identical to the ITT Population but excluding patients for whom major protocol deviations were identified. This population was intended to be used for sensitivity analyses.
L255: Unless otherwise indicated, this review uses the ITT population for analysis.
L256: Descriptive statistics were derived by the Sponsor for all efficacy outcome variables. The Sponsor indicated that, for the primary efficacy variable,
L257: "the confirmatory analysis of the [Jankovic Rating Scale Severity Score] will be based on the comparison of least square means from an analysis of covariance [ANCOVA] model at Visit 4 (Week 6) between the two treatment groups in the ITT Population. The dependent variable in the ANCOVA model is the change from baseline in the JRS severity subscore assessed by the blinded independent Investigator and the independent variables are treatment, baseline JRS severity subscore, sex, age, dose (cut-off: median dose), and center (or pooled center). In case of missing data for the change from baseline in the JRS severity subscore,
L258: the last available value for the change will be used (LOCF). Type III Sum of Squares will be used for testing procedures."
L259: All tests in the ANCOVA models are two-sided with Type I error of \alpha=0.05.
L260: Similar analyses were performed for the patient diary reporting the JRS severity subscale (for which the median score for at least 3 days prior to Visit 4 at week 6 was used) and the Blepharospasm Disability Index (BSDI).
L261: The Patient Evaluation of Global Response (PEGR) was recorded at the final visit of the trial period. Missing data for this endpoint was given a value of 0 to indicate no effect.
L262: Time to event variables measuring drug response and its duration are analyzed as Kaplan-Meier curves. In general, ambiguous data was treated with a "worst case" scenario to temper any undue optimism with regard to drug effect.
L263: Sample size calculations were made based on previous JRS severity subscale data from trial MRZ 60201 – 0003. From these data a clinically relevant difference for the NT 201 treated group compared to placebo was assumed to be 0.8 points in mean change from baseline in JRS severity subscore. A two group t-test with a two-sided significance level of \alpha = 0.05 would have 90% power to detect such a difference assuming a common standard deviation of 1.0, when the sample size is 52 in the NT 201 group and 26 in the Placebo group (ratio 2:1). Taking into consideration a 30% drop-out rate, the Sponsor planned to randomize 75 patients in the NT 201 group and 37 in the placebo group (total n = 112).
L264: ## Subject Enrollment:
L265: 31 sites in the US and Canada enrolled subjects. The first enrollment occurred on October 9, 2006 and the date of last completion was May 28, 2008.

L270: ## Screen Failures (20):
L271: Most common reasons for screening failures, all of which occurred at the screening visit (Visit 1) were: missing doses from two previous BOTOX® treatments, lack of BOTOX® stable response, surgery in affected eye muscles, previous two BOTOX® injections greater than 50 u per eye, and uncontrolled medical disease.
L272: One subject was randomized (NT 201 group) but did not receive medication; no reason was given. Seven subjects withdrew prematurely (NT 201 = 5; PCB = 2). Reasons included withdrawal of consent, lost to follow up or triggers of withdrawal criteria (see Dropouts, below).

L277: ## Study Results
L278: ## Demographics
L279: Demographic characteristics did not vary within the ITT population. Consistent with the epidemiology of the syndrome, 65% of the subjects were women in both treatment arms. Average age was similar in both groups, about 63 years (range 22 to 79 years). Participants in the trial were white (83 %), Hispanic (8%), Asian (6%) or black (4%), and most were non-smokers (67%).
L280: The median duration of blepharospasm was 84 months, the same in both treatment arms (range 6 - 468 months; mean 105 months). Length of disease after formally diagnosis was 5 years on average.
L281: Baseline severity as quantified by the Jankovic Rating Scale and Blepharospasm Disability Rating Scale revealed no statistical difference between the two treatment arms (the baseline rating is used as covariate in the outcome analysis).
L282: Baseline Ratings in ITT Population (source: Sponsor).

L286: 86% of subjects had a positive medical history at entry into the trial. These were usual and common illnesses and surgeries, and no disparity between the groups was noted. Depression was noted in one NT 201 subject and 3 PCB subjects; depression is known to negatively influence self ratings of illness and related disability.
L287: Eyelid ptosis was noted in equal ratios in both groups (NT 201 = 6; PCB = 3). Ten (13%) NT 201 and 6 (18%) placebo subjects had undergone blepharoplasty or other eyelid surgery. Other dystonia phenomena were noted in a third of subjects, all but 1% in facial, cervical, perioral, or mandibular muscles.
L288: ## Concomitant medications before trial medication:
L289: All subjects by definition had had BOTOX® treatment prior to entry into the trial. The median and range of numbers of injections received prior to entry into the trial were comparable in both arms (NT 201 = 13 (0 – 104); PCB 16 (2-90); *Reviewer's note: the subject receiving "0" injections in the NT 201 group was a major protocol violation*)
L290: ## Concomitant medications during the trial period:
L291: Almost all enrollees were taking concomitant medication (96%) and these were distributed roughly equally between the arms. The most common concomitant medications were vitamins, lipid modifying agents, NSAIDs, osteopenia related treatments, aspirin, ophthalmologicals, proton pump inhibitors, and antihypertensive agents. Neurological agents included anti Parkinson drugs (levodopa preparations and trihexyphenidyl: NT 201 7%, PCB 9%), anticonvulsants (clonazepam, gabapentin, levetiracetam, primidone, and topiramate: NT 201 20%, PCB 27%), muscle relaxants including baclofen (NT 201 3%, PCB 6%), antidepressants (NT 201 20%, PCB 27%), and sedatives (benzodiazepines, barbiturates and other hypnotics: (NT 201 27%, PCB 27%). In the reviewer's opinion, none of these had any effect on the trial's outcome.
L292: ## Compliance with Study Medication
L293: The study medication was injected by the Investigator or their designated staff at each study site. No compliance plan was specified.
L294: ## Dosing Information and Exposure
L295: Various parameters related to the dosing of NT 201 were recorded the course of this study: total dose of NT 201 by eye, number of injections around each eye, total volume of injection (sum of mL around each eye), and number of injections by anatomical site around the eye, as noted in the CRF (see Figure 7 above, page 37).
L296: Injection points that were used in at least 10 subjects in the total ITT population for either eye are shown on Figure 10 below. It should be noted that injection sites in this trial were recorded as areas around the orbit, not individual periocular muscles. Of interest, I reviewed the BOTOX® clinical review of July 12, 1988 (by courtesy of its author) and this is consistent with the method of data collection used for injection site location in the trials that were used to support BOTOX® approval in this indication.
L297: Figure 10: Injection point schema (source: Sponsor)
L298: Because the treatment of blepharospasm by botulinum toxin injection is a highly individualized and specialized clinical practice, the only possible analysis of this trial is the relationship of the total dose of NT 201 given to both eyes at the injection session to the primary outcome variable. (The rating scales used as the primary outcome do not distinguish severity or function of each individual eye alone). Medical practice dictates which muscles are appropriately injected, as it is known which determine the action of forced eye closure.
L299: The median dose of NT 201 in 74 active treatment subjects was 65 units (range 20 to 100 units). The mean dose was 67 units (SD \pm 22 units). There were no important differences between active and control groups nor right vs. left eye.
L300: The subjects in the active arm averaged 6 injections per eye (SD \pm 1.6).
L301: The injection sites and NT 201 doses used in the active treatment arm of this trial are described in the following tables. The Sponsor does not discuss the range of dose for each injection site.
L302: In conclusion, from the information collected, this reviewer finds that it is impossible to specify how much each periocular muscle had been injected and with what range of NT 201 doses.

L307: ## Protocol Deviations and Violations
L308: Protocol deviations were classified as major and minor by the Sponsor prior to breaking the blind and these have been reviewed. The classification was appropriate. Only missing outcome evaluations (or missing visits that resulted in missing assessments) and errors in drug administration were considered major. Seven subjects were affected by major protocol deviations (NT 201 = 4; PCB = 3). Two PCB subjects and one NT 201 subject were included in the study even though they did not require injection i.e.: JRS score was 1 (too low).

L311: Minor protocol deviations were numerous (one or more in each of the 109 randomized subjects) but did not lead to exclusion from analysis. The most common were: noncompliance with the JRS diary in 98 (90%) subjects, non adherence to time schedule of visits 65 (60%) of subjects, and inability to document a stable response to BOTOX® treatment given prior to entry into this trial (18 (17%) subjects). Twice as many minor deviations by percent occurred in the active treatment arm. Most had to do with missing visits and time deviations.
L312: ## Efficacy Results
L313: The population represented in this trial and its demographic features closely models the population previously treated with BoNT which would likely be encountered in clinical practice. As such, conclusions concerning the efficacy of NT 201 in blepharospasm resulting from this trial may be generalized to the target clinic population.
L314: The dosing and exposure experience with NT 201 in the single injection session is given above. However, it is important to note that, in addition to the time based outcomes noted below, there are certain constraints which the protocol imposed. No patient would be re injected before Visit 4 (at 6 weeks) but if needed, that evaluation could be performed simultaneously with Visit 5 which is the termination visit and evaluation. While the performance of Visit 5 would be triggered by the need for new treatment, a patient would otherwise have this visit performed at 20 weeks.
L315: ## Sponsor's Efficacy Analysis
L316: The primary statistical reviewer has evaluated the Sponsor's efficacy analysis and finds, as have I, that the primary and key secondary outcomes demonstrate NT 201 to be effective in treating blepharospasm in this population. While this reviewer makes no specific attempt to replicate the Sponsor's analysis, the calculations below closely corroborate those findings, though this reviewer's interpretation of the findings may differ.
L317: The Sponsor, using analyses pre-specified by the SAP, finds that statistical comparisons demonstrated that the JRS Severity Subscore had a significantly greater reduction from baseline evaluation to Visit 4 in the NT 201 group (negative least squares mean change) than in the Placebo group (positive least squares mean change, i.e., a score increase) and that the score change was also influenced by gender, age, and baseline JRS Severity Subscore.

L321: ## Primary Efficacy Endpoint
L322: **Table 12: Jankovic Rating Scale, Severity Subscale (JRS-S)**
L323: Severity	
0: None
1: Increased blinking present only with external stimuli (e.g. bright light, wind, reading, driving, etc)
2: Mild but spontaneous eyelid fluttering (without actual spasm), definitely noticeable, possibly embarrassing, but not functionally disabling
3: Moderate, very noticeable spasm of eyelids only, mildly incapacitating
4: Severe, incapacitating spasm of eyelids and possibly other facial muscles
L324: ## Jankovic Rating Scale - Severity Subscale:
L325: The JRS Severity Subscale rating performed by the independent blinded rater was available for 108 of 109 subjects at both baseline (Visit 2) and 6 weeks after the injection (Visit 4 at 42 \pm 3 days). One subject was randomized but did not receive drug.
L326: The mean difference from baseline to Visit 4 in the NT 201 arm was -0.84 (95% CI -0.56, -1.11) moving from 3.15 to 2.31 on average. In contrast, the mean difference for the placebo group was 0.21 (95% CI 0.52, -0.11) moving from 2.94 to 3.15 over the same period. There was no statistically significant difference in severity between the arms at baseline.
L327: Figure 11: Trial 0433/1 JRS-S scores at baseline (Visit 2) by arm
L328: Figure 12: Trial 0433/1 JRS-S scores at 6 weeks (Visit 4) by arm
L329: Change from baseline (Visit 4 minus Visit 2) by arm
L330: Figure 13: Trial 0433/1 JRS-S change from baseline (Visit 4 minus Visit 2) by arm
L331: Table 13: Trial 0433/1 Change in JRS-S (Visit 4 – Visit 2)

NT 201	74	-0.83784	0.12855	-1.093	-0.5830
PCB	34	0.20588	0.18964	-0.170	0.5819
L333: Using the baseline JRS-S score as a covariate strengthens the differences between the arms (p &lt; 0.0004) and the reviewer thinks it is appropriate. By regression analysis, there is a clear relationship between the baseline severity and the improvement one is likely to see as a treatment outcome in the active drug arm. Of interest, there is a similar relationship in the placebo arm, though not as strong in this smaller sample (p &lt; 0.0426). This could be explained by regression to the mean following multiple observations of the trial cohort or simply a poor quality metric. While the difference is statistically significant, it is quite small (0.21 points on the JRS-S) and clinically meaningless.
L334: Stepwise regression control reveals that total dose of NT201 does not add any predictive strength to the model and doesn't contribute to the significance of the treatment effect.
L335: Figure 14: Trial 0433/1 Regression plot of NT 201 arm (F Ratio 6.0954, p &lt; 0.0159)
L336: The results of multivariable analysis with the baseline JRS Severity Score as covariate reveals that there is a statistically significant improvement after treatment with NT201 (p &lt; 0.001).
L337: The reviewer's analysis closely corroborates the Sponsor's findings.
L338: The JRS Frequency Subscale, while not a primary or secondary outcome showed similar movement from baseline evaluation.

L356: ## Blepharospasm Disability Index:
L357: The BSDI is a self-rated scale for the assessment of impairment of specific activities of daily living caused by BEB. The scale consists of 6 items rated 0-4 with anchored cut-points, plus a choice of "not applicable to me." The 6 items are "Driving a vehicle", "Reading", "Watching TV", "Shopping", "Walking" and "Doing everyday activities". Higher scores denote increasing severity of disability.
L358: Analysis of covariance using the baseline BSDI score as the covariate revealed that there was statistically significant improvement in the active treatment group (p &lt; 0.0023).

L368: Because this rating occurred at the last visit of the study, it is the Observed Case population as opposed to LOCF. Note that the Sponsor reversed the usual direction of the scale, i.e.: negative in the protocol designated worsening.)
