L390: ## Other Secondary Efficacy Analysis: Duration of Effect
L391: The Sponsor developed two subjective patient reported time related measures: time to onset of treatment effect after injection and time to waning of treatment effect after injection.
L392: As the table notes below, there is a difference in the number of subjects who report a perceived onset of treatment related effect. The duration to waning of effect was only ascertained only within the group that initially reported an onset.
L393: Table 23: Trial 0433/1 Patient reported onset and waning of treatment effect
L394: Onset to treatment effects	YES	NO	Time to onset (range)
NT 201	55 (77%)	17 (23%)	6.2 days (0 - 30)
PCB	13 (38%)	21 (62%)	6.0 days (0 - 35)
Waning of treatment effects*	Reported	Time to waning (range)
NT 201	53 of 55	6.5 weeks (1 - 15)
PCB	11 of 13	5.2 weeks (0-13)
* only considered if onset was also reported by the individual subject
L395: The reviewer thought that this was a somewhat selective and uninformative method for determining duration of effect. In its place, I looked at a usual clinical scenario where both the physician and patient agree that it is time for a reinjection for control of blepharospasm. This time point (denoted Visit 5) was pre-specified in the protocol to signal the end of participation in the double blind portion of the trial.
L396: Visit 5, the final assessment, occurred when the patient and investigator agreed that the next injection session of NT 201 was due. This meant that the patient met pre-specified criteria for worsening by the JRS-S. The mean difference from baseline to Visit 5 in the NT 201 arm was 0.14 (95% CI 0.32, -0.05) moving from 3.15 to 3.01 on average. In the placebo group, the mean difference in JRS-S during this epoch was -0.26 (95% CI -0.002, -0.53) moving from 2.94 to 3.21 over the same period. The treated group was in essence approaching their baseline clinical state. For this reason, the interval in days from the injection visit to Visit 5 yields a valuable estimated duration of effect and of time to the need for the next injection (a point agreed upon by the patient and clinician).
L397: The NT 201 treated group went a mean of 69 days (95% CI: 63, 76) while the placebo arm required reinjection after a mean of 54 days (95% CI: 45, 64), ANOVA p &lt; 0.012. (4 subjects missing data: NT 201 3, PCB 1)
L398: Figure 22: Trial 0433/1 Days from injection session to retreatment
L399: The number of subjects who required injection at the earliest allowable time (Visit 4 at 42 \pm 3 days) indicates that as expected, more placebo subjects left the study sooner than those in the active treatment arm. But it also suggests that a considerable number of subjects treated with drug were inadequately controlled. The following histograms looked at the elapsed time in weeks between Visit 4, the earliest reinjection point and Visit 5, the termination visit. For example, where elapsed time in weeks is 0, Visits 4 and 5 were performed at the same time. These illustrate that while 76% of PCB arm left the trial at Visit 4 (6 weeks), so did 40 % of the active arm. By week 10, 96% of the active treated arm had left the trial and needed retreatment (percent of subjects listed above histogram bars below).
L400: **Figure 23: Trial 0433/1 Elapsed time from Week 4 to termination visit (top: NT 201; bottom: Placebo)**
L401: As demonstrated by survival analysis below, this difference between the arms is significant. This graph illustrates the percent of subjects "surviving" (i.e.: having continuing effectiveness of the injections).
L402: Table 24: Trial 0433/1 Days after randomization to loss of effect (quartiles)
L403: Group	Median Time	Lower 95%	Upper 95%	25% Failures	75% Failures
NT 201	70	49	77	43	86
PCB	43	42	45	42	49
Combined	49	44	70	42	84
L404: Test	Chi Square	DF	Probability > Chi Square
Log-Rank	5.5288	1	0.0187
Wilcoxon	12.0085	1	0.0005
L405: Figure 24: Trial 0433/1 Elapsed time to failure after treatment (survival model)
L406: The question is raised whether there is a relationship between those who terminated early in the trial and a treatment factor such as dose of NT 201 or disease factor such as severity of illness.
L407: The Cox proportional hazards model takes into account covariates which might modify the length of drug effect in either group. In this case however, neither total dose (both eyes) of NT 201 nor baseline JRS Severity score affected the outcome. This was also replicated using a linear regression model for least squares. Limiting the analysis to "best responders" by various criteria also did not reveal any dose response relationship.
L408: Figure 25: Trial 0433/1 Elapsed time to failure: lack of covariate effect in regression model by leverage plot (top: total dose NT 201; bottom: baseline JRS-S score)
L409: ## Clinical Meaningfulness
L410: The question is posed whether the improvement in blepharospasm outcome measures noted in this trial's evaluations are internally consistent and clinically meaningful. I looked at this in two ways.
L411: As noted above, there is agreement between the patients' assessment of their state on the JRS-S subscale with that of the blinded rated.
L412: Furthermore, the patient's self evaluation of their improvement (PEGR) is closely related to how long they were able to last from the injection session (Visit 2) until the final evaluation (Visit 5) which marked the need for the next treatment by injection and the end of the double blind portion of the trial. (One outlier (a placebo patient who did well for 139 days) was excluded from this linear regression analysis.)
L413: Figure 26: Trial 0433/1 Relationship of PEGR to Duration of Effectiveness
L414: The equation for the linear fit is PEGR = -3.1 + 0.06 \cdot V2V5INT, and for instance, one sees that a treatment lasting 90 days would be perceived as "2" (moderate improvement).
L415: Analysis of the relationship of the PEGR and the change of the blinded investigator's rating of the JRS Severity Subscore from baseline to Visit 4 reveals a similar relationship.
L416: Figure 27: Trial 0433/1 Relationship of PEGR to JRS-S change from baseline
L417: The equation for the linear fit is PEGR = 0.2 - 0.9 \cdot V4minusV2. By this model, an improvement of 1 point would be perceived as "1" (slight improvement), and a change of 2 points on the JRS-S would be perceived as "2" (moderate improvement). (The mean change from baseline in JRS-S was -0.84 points in the treated arm.) The peer-reviewed literature suggests that this is at the bottom of the range of what has been determined to be a clinically important difference (Jankovic, et al, Mov Dis, 24 (3) 407-413, 2008). The interval (days) from injection to injection appears to be a more robust measure of patient satisfaction and this is borne out in multivariable modeling.
L418: It should be noted that the PEGR is an ordinal scale and the residuals from this least squares method are not normally distributed (goodness of fit by Shapiro Wilk W test). For this reason, a non parametric analysis would be more appropriate to model these relationships. However, the consistency and linearity of this parametric model makes it useful and sufficient for this sort of post hoc exploration.
L419: ## **Safety Analysis**
L420: Because of safety concerns specific to botulinum toxins, and because human experience with NT 201 was derived from three different disease entities with a wide range of dosing regimens (blepharospasm, cervical dystonia and spasticity), this BLA submission was the subject of separate comprehensive analysis of safety performed within the Division of Neurology Products. The reader is referred to that document.
L421: This reviewer's recommendation concerning the risk to benefit ratio and the use of NT 201 in blepharospasm is based upon that safety analysis and the efficacy analysis presented above.
L422: ## **5.3.2 Open label repeated injection trial for the treatment of blepharospasm (MRZ 60201-0433/2)**
L423: ## **Trial:**
L424: Prospective, double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized, multi-center trial with an open-label extension period to investigate the efficacy and safety of NT 201 in the treatment of blepharospasm (MRZ 60201-0433/2)
L425: ## **Phase: III**
L426: ## **Purpose:**
L427: The purpose of this open label extension to the primary confirmatory trial for the indication of blepharospasm was to provide additional data concerning the efficacy of NT 201 in continuing use, as well as long term exposure and safety data. This extension provided for up to 5 additional open label injection periods following participation in the double blind portion of this trial. However, the Sponsor provided no statistical analyses of efficacy or maintenance of effect. Exposure data was received in the 120 day safety update.
L428: The reviewer's analysis is based upon Information provided gleaned from the trial summary provided for the double blind portion of the trial. Data requests were made by the reviewer for efficacy data and these were received after the date for the 120 day safety data update submission (see Appendix 9.4). If one estimates the allowed 5 additional injection periods at intervals of 3 months each, based on the completion of the blinded trial, the extension period should have ended by the end of 2009.
L429: ## Trial design
L430: After the blinded trial (i.e., after the termination Visit 5) the patient entered the open label period.
L431: Up to 11 visits (five injection sessions, five control visits, and one termination visit) took place. Assessment by rating scales took place at the time of injection and at the "control visit" 42 \pm 3 days after the injection visit.
L432: Patients attended the visits for injections as required. The patient was eligible to receive a new injection if the investigator agreed with the need (based on the JRS severity subscore). The minimum interval between two injections was to be at least six weeks. If the patient had a need for an injection, but did not meet the trial criteria above, the patient was not to be injected and the visit was to be documented as an optional visit. The next visit was then scheduled within 4 weeks. Optional visits were also to be performed for safety reasons.
L433: The Termination Visit was performed no later than 20 weeks after the last injection during the open label period or when the patient received a new injection with any botulinum toxin other than study medication.
L434: ## Key Endpoints:
L435: - Duration of effect at all visits: interval between two injections sessions for patients with treatment effect.
- Global assessment of efficacy by investigator
- Change in the investigator rated JRS severity and frequency sub scores and in the JRS sum score at all visits (except Visit 6*)
- Change in the patient diary rated JRS severity and frequency sub scores and in the JRS sum score at all visits of the first treatment interval (except Visit 6*), median score of previous 7 days)
- AUC, T_{max}, and E_{max} for change in the JRS severity and frequency sub scores and in the JRS sum score (as assessed by patient diary) after the first injection session in the open label period
- Change in Blepharospasm Disability Index at all visits (except Visit 6*)
- Patient Evaluation of Global Response at all injection visits (except Visit 6*) and the Trial Termination Visit
L436: (* Visit 6 is the first injection session of the open label extension and the termination visit of the double blind portion of the trial)
L437: ## **Safety Measurements:**
L438: - Adverse events
- Clinical chemistry and hematology
- Global assessment of tolerability by Investigator
- Antibodies against Botulinum neurotoxin Type A
- Physical and neurological examinations
L439: ## **Inclusion**
L440: Completion of the double blinded portion of the trial
Negative pregnancy test before each injection
L441: ## **Exclusion**
L442: The patient receives any other botulinum toxin
L443: ## **Concomitant Medication**
L444: No limitation

L448: ## Treatments and other ancillary management
L449: Administered with the same technique as the double blind portion of the trial, a maximum of 5 injection sessions of NT 201 was allowed. Dose adjustment up to 50 U per eye was permitted depending on the effect achieved with the previous injection.

L480: ## Reviewer's Aim
L481: The reviewer's analysis of this open label trial extension is focused upon determining that the patient's response to continued treatment is sustained. To this end, the following parameters are analyzed:
L482: - Dose and exposure: For those subjects who continue receiving open treatment, there should be no evidence for an increased in dose requirement.
- Magnitude and duration of drug effect: The robustness of efficacy as measured by JRS-S, BDSI and PEGR should approximate that found in the double blind portion of the trial. Duration of effect is demonstrated by absence of shortening of the interval between injection sessions.
L483: ## Dose and Exposure
L484: As seen above, half the subjects (56 of 102) received 5 treatments, the maximum possible under the protocol. Of the remainder, 25 received 4 injections and 21 received 3 or less.
L485: An analysis of matched pairs of data for individual subjects, uncorrected for multiple comparisons, revealed no significant change over the course of the entire trial, i.e.: there was no significant increase or decrease in dose of NT 201 injected from the start of the trial.
L486: For example, compared below are the first open label injection (n = 102) with the fourth (n=81) and fifth injection (n=56), which are not statistically different.
L487: **Table 29: Trial 0433/2 Injected dose of NT 201 by treatment session**

L494: There are two ways to look at the OL JRS Severity subscore. First, would be to see if there is waning of drug effect within an individual over multiple injection periods. The second would be to see whether differences in efficacy resulted in subjects wishing to take fewer injections in the open label period.
L495: As is evident from the table above, neither of these appear to be the case. Multivariable analysis using the baseline JRS-S subscore as the covariant for each respective period reveals that there are no statistically significant intra-individual or intra-period differences of severity. The one exception is for the group receiving 3 injections in their final injection period (JRSS: -0.4, CI -1.2, 0.5). This is likely the result of multiple
L496: uncorrected comparisons; the n is small and there is no obvious explanation for this single finding.
L497: Also of reassuring interest is that the magnitude of effect of NT 201 upon the JRS-S subscore is very consistent with that seen in the double blind portion of the trial (for the Trial 0433/1 arm receiving active drug : JRSS: -0.84, CI -1.1, 0.6).

L510: ## Duration of effect
L511: In order to look at duration of effect, the reviewer adopted a naturalistic approach as used in the double blind portion of the trial. The interval analyzed was the time from injection to the time when the patient and investigator agreed that criteria were met for the next injection session. Due to missing data, intervals were only calculated for subjects receiving 3, 4 or 5 open label treatments with NT 201. In these 87 patients, 364 injection treatments were administered. The mean interval between treatments was 82 days (95% CI 80, 85) range 39 to 238 days.
L512: In examining what determined the number of injections a subject received in open label follow-up, it was apparent that the duration of effect was the major determinant of this.

L515: This would indicate that the number of injections received in the extension trial may be related to the length of time that it was perceived to be successful, and that time ran out in the extension trial, curtailing treatment availability for patients. Multiplying the interval (mean number of days) from the second to third injection by the number of injections the group received in the trial equals 366, 376, and 385 days, respectively.
L516: ## Dose Received
L517: Finally, it was possible that the duration of treatment effectiveness was related to the dose administered. This appears not to be the case. For example, the chart below of mean total NT 201 dose for both eyes demonstrates that dose appears to have no relationship to length of effectiveness, and in fact, long intervals were associated with smaller doses in some groups. One possible explanation is that there are individual differences in the sensitivity of blepharospasm to treatment with BoNT and that some subjects have the biologic capacity to go further with less.

L520: ## Discussion of findings and conclusion
L521: The exploratory post hoc analyses presented above suggest that there is sustained effectiveness of NT 201 in the treatment of blepharospasm in this open label extension trial. The magnitude of effect in the investigator and patient based rating scales are of similar magnitude to those seen in the double blind portion of the trial. There was a wide range of duration of effectiveness of treatment among individuals and this appears not to be related to the dose administered but rather individual differences in biologic response to therapy or other unmeasured factor outside the scope of this trial.
L522: ## 5.3.3 Supportive trial of NT 201 compared to BOTOX® in blepharospasm (MRZ 60201-0003)
L523: ## **Trial:**
L524: Safety and efficacy of NT 201 (highly purified Botulinum Neurotoxin A) compared to BOTOX® (purified Botulinum Neurotoxin A-complex) in blepharospasm.
L525: **Phase:** III
L526: ## **Purpose:**
L527: The Sponsor's stated objective for this trial was to demonstrate non-inferiority in safety and efficacy of NT 201 compared to BOTOX® in patients with blepharospasm.
L528: ## **Trial design**
L529: This trial was a randomized double-blind parallel active-controlled multicenter trial performed in 42 centers in Europe and Israel. It began enrollment in March 2001 and was completed January 2002. A total of 304 patients were enrolled. All subjects had had a stable therapeutic response to BOTOX® injection on at least two previous occasions. The primary outcome was the change from baseline in the Jankovic Rating Scale from the time of injection to 3 weeks later. Secondary measures included a patient assessment of efficacy and measures of the duration of clinical response.
L530: During the baseline visit (day 0), each eligible patient was randomized to NT 201 or BOTOX®. At this visit, the trial medication was administered in randomized, double-blind fashion and the patients were observed for a period of up to 16 weeks after injection session. Each patient was injected with a dosage of up to 35 units of NT 201 or BOTOX® per eye, equivalent to the previous two injection sessions directly prior to trial entry and according to the randomization.
L531: A "control" visit for evaluation of efficacy was performed at day 21 \pm 1. From this visit on, if the patient felt the need for a new injection session, they were to return at any time up to day 112 for an optional visit. If both the physician and the patient agreed upon the necessity of a new injection session after the control visit (day 21), all assessments required for the final visit were performed and the trial was terminated for this patient.
L532: ## **Primary endpoint:**
L533: Jankovic Rating Scale is the sum of the ratings for two single items judging severity and functional impairment of blepharospasm.
L534: ## Secondary criteria:
L535: The Function Scale for Patients with Blepharospasm is an earlier version of the Blepharospasm Disability Index used in Trial 0433/1 above. It is almost identical the BSDL. This self rating scale assesses the impairment for specific activities of daily living caused by blepharospasm. The scale includes 6 items (0-4 points per item): "Driving a vehicle", "Reading", "Watching TV", "Shopping", "Getting about on foot" and "Doing everyday activities"
L536: Patient Evaluation of Global Response is a 9 point scale for self rating the blepharospasm from "complete abolishment" to "very marked worsening".
L537: Duration of Treatment is defined as the interval between the day of the injection session and the date when both the patient and the physician have agreed upon the necessity of a new injection. The trial ended at 112 days. Amendment 1 to the trial also added estimations by the patient as to time to onset and waning of treatment effect.
L538: ## Safety endpoints:
L539: These include physical examination, vital signs, clinical chemistry and hematology, antibodies against botulinum toxin A,
L540: ## **Inclusion**
L541: - Male or female outpatients between 18 and 75
- A clinical diagnosis of blepharospasm with a need for injection
- Patients with documented stable therapeutic response to BOTOX® as a result of the last two previous injection sessions directly prior to trial entry
- (Stability was defined as injecting at least two times the same injection points, administering the same doses and volumes in the same interval resulting in the same satisfactory therapeutic response as evaluated by the investigator and assessed by the patient)
- Source documentation of the last two injection sessions prior to trial entry
- Patients must be on a stable dose of other medications (if any) used for focal dystonia treatment (e. g. anticholinergics and benzodiazepines) for 3 months prior to and throughout the trial
- Negative pregnancy test at entry
L542: ## Exclusion
L543: - No neurotoxin treatment other than BOTOX®.
- Atypical syndrome
- Hypersensitivity to human serum albumin
- Will need anesthesia during the trial
L544: ## Concomitant Medication
L545: Medications for other conditions were allowed. Psychotropic medications were allowed if the subject was on a stable dose for 3 month prior to entry into the trial. Drugs affecting the neuromuscular junction were prohibited.
L546: ## Study Visits (Checklist)
L547: This version of the study checklist is from the original protocol. Amendment 1 added a global assessment by the investigator at the end of the trial.

L550: ## Treatments and other ancillary management
L551: Injection points and the dose of botulinum toxin (specified as volume in the protocol) were chosen on the basis of the patient's previous BOTOX® experience. BOTOX® and NT 201 were considered equipotent unit for unit.
L552: ## Randomization and Controls:
L553: Balanced block randomization was used in a 1:1 ratio.
L554: ## GCP:
L555: The trial was performed in accordance with GCP, was in compliance with the Declaration of Helsinki and operated according to requirements of local IECs. Copies of IEC approvals were provided as well as sample informed consent.
L556: The trial sites were monitored and the Sponsor's quality assurance unit audited 13 clinical sites. Two of the sites were audited twice for reasons unknown.
L557: ## Protocol Amendments
L558: ## Amendment 1 (February 20, 2001):
L559: Prior to the start of the trial, this amendment refined the use of the outcome parameters, added duration measures for the treatment effect, and added a global assessment by the investigator. Most importantly, the statistical analysis plan was changed with regard to the non-inferiority margin. These changes are incorporated into the Sponsor's Statistical Analysis Plan, discussed below.
L560: ## Amendment 2 (July 10, 2001):
L561: This amendment prolonged the enrollment period due to slower than expected recruitment.
L562: ## Trial populations / Patient disposition
L563: The safety population was considered everyone who was randomized. The Intent to Treat (ITT) population is everyone who was randomized and received medication.
L564: Figure 32: Trial 0003 Subject disposition (source: Sponsor)
L565: Data Source: Table 14.1.1.1.1, Table 14.1.1.3.1
L566: ## Method for determining the outcome of efficacy analysis (statistical analysis plan):
L567: The SAP from the original protocol was only finalized and signed on August 20, 2002 well after the start of the trial. There is a statement affirming that "the statistical analysis plan was finalized after data base closure and before unblinding." It does not differ substantively from that proposed in the protocol and amendments.
L568: The method for replacing missing data was use of LOCF.
L569: JRS total score, the primary efficacy parameter, was to be analyzed using an analysis of covariance model with baseline, treatment, baseline*treatment and total dose. The interaction is taken as fixed effect, and the baseline value and total dose as covariate. The baseline value is defined as the value at the baseline visit. If there is a significant baseline treatment interaction, an analysis of variance model was to be used instead. The treatments would be considered clinically equivalent if the mean difference between treatments (NT 201 - BOTOX®) is less than \Delta. The therapeutically "irrelevant difference" (a term taken by the reviewer to mean non-inferiority margin) was defined as 1 point based on the clinically supported suggestion by physicians derived from an internal expert meeting and from results of a field study conducted by the Sponsor. The hypothesis of NT 201 being the same or better than BOTOX® was to be tested by comparing the 95% upper confidence limit for the adjusted treatment group means (NT 201 - BOTOX®) with \Delta, the value of clinical irrelevance. If this upper limit is less than \Delta,
L570: then the Sponsor believes the treatments may be declared equivalent. If this upper limit is less than 0, then NT 201 may be declared superior to BOTOX®. In situations where the basic model assumptions are violated, a suitable transformation or a non-parametric procedure would be used.
L571: The sample size calculation was based on the assumption that the irrelevant difference of the two treatments for the change of the Jankovic rating scale is \Delta = 1 point. From previous trials the standard deviation of the change was estimated to be 2.0 by the Sponsor. Cohort size was calculated using a significance level of 2.5% and a power of 90%.
L572: This, however, was adjusted by Amendment 1. At that time the irrelevant difference was changed to \Delta = 0.8 point to maintain less power, with \alpha = 0.025 (1 sided) and with 1 - \beta = 0.85. The Sponsor did not provide a rationale for justification of the non-inferiority margin or change in power. The effect size of BOTOX® was not calculated or estimated from review of published trials and there is no discussion of why such a clinical margin would be acceptable.
L573: Only the primary efficacy variable (Jankovic Rating Scale) was tested as confirmatory by the Sponsor. "All other statistical tests will be interpreted in an exploratory sense," (SAP, p 10, Version 20.08.2002). Exploratory survival analyses were used to look at time based effects.
L574: Also of note, it is only in the trial's final report that it is indicated that the Treatment Per Protocol (TPP, NT 201 n = 129; BOTOX® n = 127), not the ITT cohort, was used by the Sponsor to analyze the primary efficacy variable.
L575: The SAP strategy for missing data in the primary outcome was as follows: For the confirmatory analysis, only observed cases were used and those subjects with missing date were dropped. For the ITT analysis, baseline evaluations were used for the follow-up evaluation (LOCF), but no imputation was made for the one subject with missing baseline data. For secondary outcomes, no imputations were made and only the TPP population was used.
L576: ## Study Results
L577: ## *Reviewer's note:*
L578: *In the submission's proposed label (version 3/1/2010) the Sponsor makes the following statement in Section 2.3. General Dosage and Administration:*
L579: (b) (4)
L580: *This is restated in Section 14, Clinical Studies (14.2 Blepharospasm). As a result this reviewer has focused upon the primary efficacy variable which was used to support this claim (JRS total score) and the secondary*
L581: *outcomes which provide some comparability to the confirmatory study in Section 5.3.1 (JRS severity subscore).*
L582: ## Demographics and Concomitant Medications
L583: In the ITT population, 73% were women, commensurate with the syndrome. Only four subjects were not white. They had comparable medical histories and concomitant medication use. Mean age was 63. Subjects had 6 to 7 years of blepharospasm prior to entry into the trial. They had had on average 15 to 16 treatments with botulinum toxin before enrolling. There were no significant differences in demography between the arms.
L584: Upon entry into this trial they had comparable passage of time since the previous BOTOX® treatment (13-14 weeks). The subjects had comparable perceptions of how long that pervious treatment had lasted (10 weeks).
L585: The two arms had comparable severity of blepharospasm at the randomization visit:

L588: ## Compliance with Study Medication
L589: The study medication was injected by the clinical investigator at each study site. No compliance plan was specified.
L590: ## Dosing Information and Exposure
L591: One unit of NT 201 corresponds to the calculated median lethal intra peritoneal dose (LD_{50}) in mice. Each vial contained 100 units, approximately 600 picograms (Sponsor's range 400-800 pg).
L592: The injection scheme followed that of usual clinical practice with regard to target sites and dose injected. Volume of NT 201 injected solution was recorded on the CRF by area, not dose in units or by muscle injected.

L603: ## Protocol Deviations and Violations
L604: Protocol violations were equally distributed between the treatment arms. If subjects had been randomized they were allowed to continue in the trial. Half the subjects had minor violations and 15% of subjects had major violations. Major violations included time window violations, violations of exclusion criteria, errors in injected dose and lack of safety examination and missing evaluations of outcome variables.
L605: ## Efficacy Results
L606: ## Primary Efficacy Endpoint
L607: ## Jankovic Rating Scale –Total Score
L608: The analysis dataset provided listings for 303 of 304 enrolled subjects (NT 201 n = 148; BOTOX® n = 155). One subject had been randomized but not treated. Outcome evaluations were missing on ten patients (NT 201 n = 4; BOTOX® n = 6). Three subjects in this group also had no injection dose recorded (all were in the BOTOX® arm). This efficacy analysis used the available data on the rest of the subjects.
L609: Both arms were treated with virtually identical dose distributions (t-test, p = 0.585): NT 201: mean 41 units (95% CI 38, 43); BOTOX® mean 42 units (95% CI 39, 44).
L610: Using the baseline value of the total JRS score as a covariate in multivariable analysis, there is no difference in effect between the treatment arms (p = 0.362):

L623: ## Comparability of JRS-S to placebo controlled Trial MRZ 60201-0433/1
L624: Even though the prespecified primary outcome in this active comparator trial was the total JRS score, the JRS Severity subscale data makes it possible to evaluate the comparability of the effect size in this trial to that of the double blind placebo controlled confirmatory Trial MRZ 60201-0433/1. The patient populations in these two trials were comparable with regard to gender, race, age, BMI, and disease duration. As noted below in Table 44 they were judged to have different baseline severity.
L625: Change from baseline of the JRS severity subscore at 6 weeks was the primary outcome in that trial, but a rating was also performed at 3 weeks making a comparison to this active control trial possible for the purpose of looking at effect size of NT 201 and making an assessment of assay sensitivity for this trial.
L626: By analysis of covariance using the baseline severity subscale score as the covariate, the change in JRS-S from baseline at 21\pm 3 days in Trial 0433/1 was significantly different from placebo, as expected (p &lt; 0.0043):
L627: **Table 42: Trial 0433/1 JRS-S change from baseline at 3 weeks**
L628: Level	Mean	Std Error	Lower 95%	Upper 95%
NT 201	-1.0558	0.13272	-1.319	-0.7927

L636: It is of interest that, as shown in the analysis of the placebo controlled trial above, more severe disease at baseline was related to greater change from baseline following treatment. In this trial, NT201 also had a smaller effect when compared to placebo, in a population more severely affected by blepharospasm. This suggests that the rating scales were applied in different fashion in the two trials. Without a placebo arm in the active comparator trial it is difficult to know which trial represents greater estimation of drug effect, but interpretation would favor the smaller effect size as blindly measured against placebo. In support of this is the Duration of Treatment Effect analysis which follows below.

L654: ## Duration of Treatment Effect
L655: Duration of Treatment was defined in the protocol as the interval (days) between the day of the injection session and the date when both the patient and the physician have agreed upon the necessity of a new injection. The trial ended at 112 days, and the date of the final visit (often past day 112) was the number used if this point had not yet been reached. There was no difference between the arms but fully 25 percent of subjects in both arms had duration of treatment less than 3 months.
L656: Table 50: Trial 0003 vs. Trial 0433/1 Duration of treatment effect for NT 201 treated arms
L657: Level	Number	Mean	Std Error	Lower 95%	Upper 95%
BOTOX	151	98	2.2	93	102
NT 201	146	98	2.3	94	103

L664: Patient reported Waning of Effect was added by protocol Amendment 1. This time-based outcome was the patient's own evaluation of when the treatment's effect wore off. The interval from the injection session to this point was recorded in weeks by the investigator.

L668: The patient reported length of effect for NT 201 in this active comparator trial was 11 weeks on average. This is very consistent with the 77 day reported in the placebo controlled trial. The investigator guided assessment of duration of effect, when both the investigator and patient "agreed" the next treatment was needed, appears to show a bias towards an optimistic assessment of duration of effect for both active agents.
L669: ## Exploration of dose and responses
L670: No dose response relationship was found in this trial.
L671: ## Outcome of Safety Analysis
L672: The reader is referred to the separate clinical safety review being performed for this BLA submission.
L673: ## Discussion of findings and conclusion
L674: As a non-inferiority design, this trial has deficiencies that affect its interpretation.
L675: It is difficult to estimate from prior experience what the effect size of the primary outcome variable should be. No discussion was presented regarding the rationale for the size of the non-inferiority margin, how much effect one was willing to give up (which changed from 95 to 90% by Amendment 1) and the historical constancy of the effect. In fact, the JRS has not been previously used in an adequately controlled and blinded trial of blepharospasm. The difference in effect size of the JRS for the NT 201 arms in the placebo controlled and active controlled trial graphically demonstrates the difficulty this poses.
L676: Even if the margin had been adequately prespecified, the population in this trial is not the same as other populations used in blepharospasm trials which have led to market approval. A precondition to entry in this trial was an adequate and stable response to a marketed BoNT A product, which is, in effect, enrichment. This parceling of the real world population of subjects with blepharospasm would eliminate those with small drug effect size, treatment resistant blepharospasm, and patients who respond well for very long intervals of time. All these factors preselect a population which resulted in the response to treatment pattern elicited in these this trial. We have no data as to the effect of NT 201 in a population of treatment-naïve subjects with blepharospasm, ultimately the population of subjects who will most often be offered this agent.
L677: While the subject's estimation of the duration of treatment effect was consistent in both this and the placebo controlled trial, the investigators' assessment of the duration of treatment effect in the active control trial appears considerably more favorable, and not consistent with the experience of neurological clinics specialized in the treatment of blepharospasm. (It would have been even longer had the interval not been limited by the ending of the trial period at 112 days). There are few controlled trials which look at the duration of efficacy of botulinum toxin preparations but it is generally considered that clinical efficacy falls off after 60 days and 10 weeks is the average duration of action (Simpson, et al. Neurology 70: 1699-1706, 2008).
L678: The source of the discrepancy in the investigators' assessment is not clear. Certainly, knowing that every subject in the trial was treated with an active BoNT product would bias the clinicians towards an optimistic assessment of the treatment they administer. Training for assessing the primary outcome did occur. Given that this trial was performed in Europe and Israel, whilst the confirmatory trial was performed in North America raises the possibility of cultural effects or practice differences among the investigators.
L679: Both the investigators' rated improvement of severity and the patients' assessment of satisfaction are more favorable in this trial compared to the placebo controlled trial. A placebo arm in the active comparator trial would have established the ability of the trial to sensitively detect the magnitude of drug effect (i.e. establish assay sensitivity).
L680: Failing that, the consistency of the patient reported outcome for time to the waning of treatment effect seems to offer the only support as to the comparability of these two treatment trials.
L681: ## 6 Review of Efficacy
L682: ## Efficacy Summary
L683: The proposed indication for NT 201 is the treatment of benign essential blepharospasm.
L684: In this application the treatment of blepharospasm was studied in a population of adult subjects who had previously responded in a favorable and stable fashion to BOTOX®, another BoNT Type A product approved for blepharospasm.
L685: Three Phase III trials were performed: a double blind trial which demonstrated superiority to placebo in a single treatment session (NT 201, n = 75; PCB, n = 34), an open label extension of that trial which demonstrated the maintenance of drug effect over 1 year (n = 102), and a blinded active control trial using a single treatment session which was intended as a non-inferiority comparison trial to BOTOX® (NT 201, n = 144; PCB, n = 149).
L686: All three trials used the Jankovic Rating Scale (JRS) which is composed of a severity and a frequency subscale. The non-inferiority trial used the total JRS score while the other two trials used only the severity subscale (JRS-S) for the primary outcome. Secondary measures included the Blepharospasm Disability Index (BSDI) and global ratings of improvement by the investigator and patient. Multivariable analyses were performed using the baseline measure as a covariate.
L687: The selected treatment dose for NT 201 in each subject was the same as that previously used for the BOTOX® product prior to entering the trial and give in the same periorbital sites. Dose ranging studies, per se, were not performed as the source and manufacture of the drug product and Phase I data suggested that its physiological effect should approximate that of BOTOX®. A maximum of 50 units per eye was administered in the development program. The average total dose delivered to both eyes for the double blind and the open label extension trial was 67 units, with no side to side difference.
L688: In the placebo controlled trial at 6 weeks following treatment, NT 201 was superior to placebo injection in the periorbital muscles (p &lt; 0.0004). The JRS-S score improved (moved negatively) by -0.84 points (95%CI -1.09, -0.58), compared to placebo which worsened (moved positively) by 0.21 points (95%CI -0.17, 0.58).
L689: The level of functional disability at six weeks following treatment as measured by the BSDI also improved significantly in the active treatment arm (p &lt; 0.0023). The BSDI improved (moved negatively) by -0.48 points (95%CI -0.65, -0.30), compared to the placebo arm which worsened (moved positively) by 0.15 points (95%CI -0.23, 0.26).
L690: Other secondary measures, of which there were many, moved consistently in the direction favoring the active treatment group.
L691: The duration of treatment effect was assessed in both the double blind and open label trials. In the double blind trial waning of effect was best determined by the interval from treatment until meeting the predetermined criteria for retreatment. The time to the need for retreatment was a median of 70 days (95% CI 49, 77).
L692: In the open label trial which lasted over a year, the number of injections the subject received was determined by how long the treatment effect was adequate. As a result, 89% of subjects received either 4 or 5 treatment sessions. The average interval for 87 evaluable subjects receiving 364 treatments in the open label trial was 82 days (95% CI 80, 85; range 39 to 238 days).
L693: The active control trial yielded little additional useful information. As indicated in the trial review in **Section 5.3.3**, the reviewer had concern about the validity of the results of that trial due to deficiencies in its design.
L694: ## 6.1 Indication
L695: The treatment of benign essential blepharospasm is the proposed indication for NT 201. This chronic syndrome occurs exclusively in adults beginning on average in the fifth or sixth decade of life. Disability is the result of uncontrollable forced eye closure. While this is a clinical syndrome, blepharospasm, may occur by itself or in combination with other movement disorder phenomena such as other dystonias and Parkinsonism. It can be expected that NT 201 will have considerable off label use just as the other BoNT preparations do.
L696: ## 6.1.1 Methods
L697: The confirmatory double blind trial compared a single treatment of NT 201 to placebo. The enrolled cohort was randomized 2:1, active: placebo. The range of doses given is summarized in this table:
L698: **Table 53: Trial 0433/1 Range of doses administered (source: Sponsor)**

Source: Table 14.3.1.1
L700: The average total dose delivered to both eyes in the double blinded trial was 67 units. In addition, doses in the open label extension did not change over the course of the trial and the mean total dose for both eyes remained 67 units.
L701: The effect of treatment was rated by a blinded investigator at six weeks following treatment. The patient was followed after that to a maximum of 20 weeks. At the time where the patient and investigator agreed that retreatment was needed and the patient met predetermined criteria for worsening, they ended the trial. The patient was then offered treatment in the open label extension trial.
L702: The severity subscale of the Jankovic Rating Scale was the primary efficacy measure. This is a single item, 0-4 rating scale, with anchored cut-points for the severity of blepharospasm. The planned statistical analysis was the change from baseline (Visit 2) to week 6 (Visit 4). The dependent variable in the ANCOVA model is the change from baseline in the JRS Severity subscale score assessed by the blinded independent investigator and the independent variables are treatment, baseline JRS severity subscore, sex, age, dose and trial center. The trial was powered to demonstrate a one point change from baseline, thought to represent a clinically meaningful change by the Sponsor.
L703: The most important secondary criteria were: duration of treatment effect, change from baseline of the JRS-S from the patient's diary and the blepharospasm disability index (BSDI), the patient evaluation of global response (PEGR) and the investigator's global assessment of efficacy.
L704: ## 6.1.2 Demographics
L705: In the double blind trial, 109 patients were randomized and 108 received medication (NT 201, n = 75; PCB, n = 34). Seven patients withdrew leaving 70 NT 201 and 32 placebo subjects completing the trial. All 102 subjects entered the open label extension of this trial which demonstrated the maintenance of drug effect over 1 year. The blinded active control non-inferiority trial which is not discussed further in this section randomized 293 patients for a single treatment session (NT 201, n = 144; BOTOX®, n = 149).
L706: Demographic characteristics did not vary between treatment arms. Consistent with the epidemiology of the syndrome, 65% of the subjects were women. Average age was 63 years (range 22 to 79 years). Participants in the trial were white (83 %), Hispanic (8%), Asian (6%) or black (4%), and most were non-smokers (67%). Medical illness and concomitant medications were as expected for a cohort of this age.
L707: The median duration of blepharospasm was 84 months, the same in both treatment arms (range 6 - 468 months; mean 105 months). The length of disease after formally diagnosis was 5 years on average. A third of subjects had other dystonic phenomena, all but 1% in the contiguous segments of face, neck, mouth, and mandible.
L708: ## 6.1.3 Subject Disposition
L709: Of the 109 subjects randomized and receiving medication. One dropped out and never received medication. The rest completed the trial. Seven (NT 201 = 4; PCB = 3) were subject to major protocol violations (assessments out of window, errors in medication administration) but were evaluated in the ITT population.
L710: ## 6.1.4 Analysis of Primary Endpoint(s)
L711: Jankovic Rating Scale (Severity Subscale):
L712: The mean difference from baseline to week 6 (Visit 4) in the NT 201 arm was -0.84 (95% CI -0.56, -1.11) moving from 3.15 to 2.31 on average. In contrast, the mean difference for the placebo group was 0.21 (95% CI 0.52, -0.11) moving from 2.94 to 3.15 over the same period. There was no statistically significant difference in baseline severity between the arms.

The initial self-assessment is made in the week prior to the baseline injection, with the endpoint value taken from diaries prior to week 6 (Visit 4). Multivariable analysis of the diary self-rating, using baseline measures as the covariate and LOCF for missing data reveals that there was a significant difference between the treatment groups (p &lt; 0.005):

L719: Blepharospasm Disability Index: The BSDI is a self-rated scale for the assessment of impairment of specific activities of daily living caused by BEB. The scale consists of 6 items rated 0-4 with anchored cut-points, plus a choice of "not applicable to me." The 6 items are "Driving a vehicle", "Reading", "Watching TV", "Shopping", "Walking" and "Doing everyday activities". Baseline disability scores were equivalent between the arms: NT 201: 1.60 (95% CI: 1.41, 1.78) PCB: 1.37 (95% CI 1.09, 1.64). Analysis of covariance using the baseline BSDI score as the covariate revealed that there was statistically significant improvement in the active treatment group (p &lt; 0.0023).

L728: ## 6.1.6 Other Endpoints
L729: The duration of treatment effect was defined as the time period from day of injection (Day 0) until the time point for the “agreed upon” day of retreatment. This day (which ends the blinded portion of this trial for that subject) was determined by the evaluation of the JRS severity subscore assessed by the blinded investigator (score must be \ge 2). The duration of treatment effect was assessed in both the double blind and open label trials.
L730: In the double blind trial waning of effect and the need for retreatment was after a median of 70 days (95% CI 49, 77). In the open label trial which lasted over a year, the number of injections the subject received was determined by how long the treatment effect was adequate. As a result, 89% of subjects received either 4 or 5 treatment sessions. The average interval for 87 evaluable subjects receiving 364 treatments in the open label trial was 82 days (95% CI 80, 85) range 39 to 238 days.
L731: The JRS Frequency Subscale, while not a primary or secondary outcome showed similar movement from baseline evaluation. The results of multivariable analysis with the baseline JRS Frequency Score as covariate reveals that there is a statistically
L732: significant improvement (change from baseline) after treatment with NT201 (p &lt; 0.0137). The mean difference from baseline to week 6 (Visit 4) in the NT 201 arm was -0.67 (95% CI -0.91, -0.43). In contrast, the mean difference for the placebo group was 0.15 (95% CI -0.49, 0.20). There was no statistically significant difference in baseline severity between the arms NT 201: 2.75 (95% CI 2.55, 2.94) PCB 2.82 (95% CI 2.53, 3.12).
L733: ## 6.1.7 Subpopulations
L734: The effects of gender and age upon outcome of treatment with NT 201 were investigated by the Sponsor. Age (above and below 60) had no effect in the group receiving active treatment. In the placebo group lower age was associated with slight worsening of symptoms over the evaluation period: a gain of 0.43 points on the JRS-S vs. 0.05 points in older subjects. Women achieved higher mean score reductions in response to treatment than male subjects: (-1.02 vs. -0.46 points). It is to be emphasized that this exploration is retrospective on a small number of subjects and should be viewed in that light. The low number of non-white subjects precludes any conclusion on the effect of race.
L735: ## 6.1.8 Analysis of Clinical Information Relevant to Dosing Recommendations
L736: The Sponsor wishes to demonstrate that the effective dose of NT 201 is roughly equivalent to that of a population that has previously responded to BOTOX® treatment for blepharospasm. It is not clear, however, how a BoNT naïve population may respond. For this reason, prudence suggests a lower starting dose, 25 units per eye, with individual injections sites initially receiving 1.25 - 2.5 units, and increasing to a maximum recommended dose as clinically needed and tolerated. The Sponsor suggests (b) units per eye as a maximum dose.
L737: Single dose exposure to higher doses (45 to 50 units per eye) of NT 201 is considerable. In Trial 0003, the active control trial, the maximum dose was limited by protocol to no more than 35 units per eye (70 u total dose).
L738: In Trial 0433/1, the double blind trial, 16 subjects in the NT 201 arm received 90 units total dose or greater. In Trial 0433/2, the open label extension, 5 additional subjects from the placebo arm subjects received 90 units total dose or greater. This brings the total to 21 unique individuals who received 90 u total dose or greater (i.e.: 45 u /eye). If one takes into account the multiple treatment sessions in the double blind and open label extension trials, as opposed to individuals, then there were 114 treatment sessions at 90 u total dose or greater.
L739: However, because NT 201 is intended as a chronic treatment, the chronic exposures in the clinical trials bear careful examination.
L740: Using the datasets for Trials 0433/1 and 0433/2, the intervals between sequentially administered treatments (numbers of days from one injection to the next injection) were summed. The interval of exposure to active drug in the double blind trial was carried over into the open label extension as these patients were rolled over without interruption in treatments.
L741: This summed exposure for each subject was converted to weeks to get total duration of exposure. The rounding was downward in direction, e.g. 7 weeks and 6 days was considered 7 weeks. If a subject was lost to follow-up and had no final visit for that treatment session, that injection dose and its interval were dropped from the exposure data set.
L742: Subjects with at least 24 weeks and 48 weeks continuous observation were taken to be supportive of exposure to the lowest dose in that interval. I looked at all combinations to see if a given dose exposure might be supported at some middle interval over the course of a subject's treatment. For example one subject received 4 injections of 100, 100, 60, and 55 units at intervals over a year. As a result this patient was supportive of 100 units at 6 months exposure, but also 50 units at 48 weeks exposure. (The doses are expressed as deciles, e.g.: a dose between 50 and 59 units supported a 50 unit dose).
L743: Table 59 NT 201 Cumulative dose exposure at 24 and 48 weeks

L745: This then would suggest that there is insufficient exposure data to support the Sponsor's proposed (b) (4) maximum total dose and indicates that 70 units (35 units per eye) may be more appropriate.
L746: The clinical literature suggests that frequency of usage may have a long term effect upon efficacy. More frequent treatment (intervals less than every 10 weeks) may contribute to the formation of blocking antibodies. Because drug concentrations were not measured it is impossible to propose a response relationship. There does not appear to be one related to injected dose. Subjects taking concomitant medications which affect the neuromuscular junction were excluded from trials so specific drug-drug interactions are unknown. It is apparent that the degree of improvement obtained by treatment with NT 201 may also be related to the baseline severity of disease.
L747: ## 6.1.9 Discussion of Persistence of Efficacy and/or Tolerance Effects
L748: The open label extension trial demonstrates by three criteria that tachyphylaxis to drug effect did not occur that over the time of the trial: there was no need for increasing doses of NT 201 over time, there was no loss of duration of treatment effect, and there was no loss of effect as measured by the JRS-S.
L749: ## 6.1.10 Additional Efficacy Issues/Analyses
L750: The clinical reviewer, in looking at the magnitude and direction of change in the primary and secondary outcome measures, has found a credible relationship between scales rated by the investigators and patients rated outcomes of efficacy. On the basis of the concordance and the quantifiable relationship between investigator and patient derived measures, it is fair to state that the magnitude of improvement as assessed by the JRS-S is clinically meaningful to the patient.
L751: ## 7 Review of Safety
L752: ## Safety Summary
L753: Because of safety concerns specific to botulinum toxins, and because human experience with NT 201 was derived from three different disease entities with a wide range of dosing regimens (blepharospasm, cervical dystonia and spasticity), this BLA submission was the subject of separate comprehensive DNP safety analysis. The reader is referred to that document.
L754: After discussion with the safety reviewer, it appears that no new or unexpected adverse events were discovered in the course of the development program for NT 201. However, review of the safety data suggests that there is the same possibility of unintended paralytic effects in both contiguous and remote parts of the body as is seen in other BoNTs.
L755: Two safety subsections are retained here for discussion: the issues of spread of toxin after injection (7.3.5) and immunogenicity (7.4.6)
L756: ## 7.3.5 Submission Specific Primary Safety Concerns
L757: ## Spread of neurotoxin effect to non-target tissues:
L758: The potential for spread of botulinum toxin into tissues adjacent to the target muscles or more remotely via tissue planes or even the blood stream is a source of concern for this class of agents. Because Xeomin® is a smaller biologic by virtue of its size, it is cogent to raise the concern that, precisely because of the nature of the preparation, greater diffusion is possible.
L759: The Sponsor's Periodic Safety Update Report No. 6, covering the period up to July 1, 2009, reports 90 cases of potential spread from their spontaneous reporting system. Using a list of sentinel terms (including, e.g. dysphagia, aspiration, ptosis, muscle weakness) that signal adverse events of special interest, 3 cases considered serious were found. One was ptosis in association with a cosmetic use in the glabellar region. One case with respiratory failure and one case with dyspnea were associated with off label use. Two additional cases of speech disorder were considered to be evidence of systemic or local toxin spread, documenting that this phenomenon occurs with NT 201 just as with the other BoNTs.
L760: Only Trial 0433/1 lends an opportunity to judge spread in an NT 201 treated group as compared to placebo. The table below looks at reported AEs by Preferred Term selected for the potential that it could reflect spread, either local or distant.
L761: **Table 60: Trial 0433/1 Subjects reporting AE of special interest**
L762: Individuals reporting the AE
(Preferred Term)	NT 201 (n=75)	Placebo (n=34)
N	%	N	%
Facial paresis	2	2.7	0	0.0
Dry mouth	12	16.0	2	5.9
Dysphagia	4	5.3	2	5.9
Dyspnea	4	5.3	1	2.9
Muscular weakness	1	1.3	2	5.9
Asthenia	3	4.0	0	0.0
L763: Dry mouth, presumably a result of local diffusion, appears to exist more commonly in the active arm. Other local (facial weakness) and more distant phenomena (dysphagia, dyspnea, and generalized weakness) are present but appear less commonly in the NT 201 treated arm. The open label extension corroborates these findings but they must be considered preliminary information from a very small sample. L766: Nevertheless, it suggests that NT 201 is not immune from the AEs observed for the class of BoNT agents.
L767: A paucity of AE reports from the Trial 0003 NT 201 vs. BOTOX® comparison trial suggests that events were poorly captured. For example, in 297 BoNT treated subjects, there was only one report of dry mouth which suggests poor “assay sensitivity” for the collection of AEs in this trial. In Trial 0433/1 a specific checklist of potential side effects was employed while in Trial 0003 the CRF contained only one general question as to whether anything new had occurred health-wise.
L768: ## 7.4.6 Immunogenicity
L769: Because botulinum toxin products are biological proteins which are chronically administered on an intermittent schedule, they are obvious sources of activation for the human immune system. Blocking antibodies have been described but their relationship to the loss of clinical efficacy is not clear cut. (b) (4)
L770: An early series, investigating 559 BoNT type A treated patients between 1984 and 1992, found 24 (4.3%) with antibodies by mouse assay. Some had had only one or two injection sessions. Eight of 76 patients with follow-up (10.5%) developed resistance to drug effect. As a rule, they had had been treated with larger doses and had injections at closer intervals, especially booster injections 2 to 3 weeks after the initial session (Greene, et al. Mov Dis 9(2): 213-217, 1994).
L771: However, well-documented cases of antibodies developing after a single injection or with low dose (as in the treatment of glabellar lines) exist.
L772: Another study (by far the largest in the literature) looked at the correlation between the presence of neutralizing antibodies and non-responsiveness to treatment (Lange, et al.
L773: Clin Neuropharm 32(4)213-218, 2009). Serum samples were taken from 503 patients with a variety of diagnoses treated by BoNT-A (Dysport® or BOTOX®). The selected subjects were secondary nonresponders, i.e.: initially responded but then lost the responsiveness to therapy. Fewer than half of the subjects (n = 224, 44.5%) had neutralizing antibodies. The authors concluded that non-responsiveness is not well correlated with the presence of antibodies and that non responsiveness may not reliably be used as a surrogate for the presence of antibodies. They confirmed that the presence of antibodies in these non-responders was associated with higher doses and shorter intervals between injections (for example, only 6 of 38 subjects (16%) with blepharospasm, but 175 of 362 subjects (48%) with cervical dystonia).
L774: The authors found the presence of antibodies to be unrelated to the commercial preparation used, but found that cumulative doses higher than 6000 units were associated with a higher proportion of patients with antibodies. Of note, the mean duration of therapy until secondary nonresponsive was 41 months (Dysport®) and 45 months (BOTOX®). This also suggests that formulation differences among BoNT agents may not be relevant to the formation of antibodies or the development of non-responsiveness.
L775: An alternative plausible explanation for the loss of efficacy to BoNT products could be that actual muscle fiber damage results from the use of toxin. This has been documented by biopsy in humans (Schroeder, et al. Mov Dis 24 (10)1494-1503, 2009).
L776: In the NT 201 development program, antibodies were investigated (Goschel, et al Exp Neurol 147:96-102, 1997) and the Sponsor argues that antibodies were present in all non-responders, stating that non-responders who were not antibody positive subsequently responded to retreatment. However, in this paper, no study design specifications or clinical data are presented. The Sponsor demonstrated antibody development to high doses of BOTOX® but not NT 201 after biweekly injections for 12 weeks in rabbits. In a 33 week study comparing Dysport®, BOTOX® and NT 201, one animal did develop antibodies in the NT 201 group.
L777: In Trial 0433/1 (placebo controlled) and Trial 0003 (active control with BOTOX®), the Sponsor screened antibodies by ELISA and confirmed with the mouse hemidiaphragm bioassay. Antibody titers &gt; 3.58 mU/mL and hemidiaphragm paralysis times &gt; 64 minutes were considered positive. In these two trials, 2 of 222 patients (1%) had antibodies at baseline (2 others were "borderline"). In Trial 0433/1 two subjects developed transient "borderline" antibodies which were not present at the final visit. In Trial 0003, one NT 201 subject became positive and another "borderline" at the final visit. In Trial 0003, two subjects developed antibodies in the BOTOX® arm; they had had 21 and 31 previous injection sessions, respectively. There were no clinical phenomena which could be attributed to this development. The Sponsor's tally from all trials in the development program for all indications reveals that 0.7% of 1048 NT 201 patients and 0.8% of 354 BOTOX® patients developed anti BoNT antibodies (ISS, p. 84).
L778: Results of antibody screening in the 52 week open label follow-up to Trial 0433/1 have not been reported.
L779: The most recent Periodic Safety Update Report (PSUR No. 6, submitted August 2009) reports 36 cases of ineffective or no response to treatment. The report also states "It has not been investigated whether secondary non-response due to the development of antibodies is less frequent under Xeomin® therapy than under treatment with conventional preparations containing the Botulinum toxin type A complex."
L780: The Sponsor proposes the following for the label:
L781: (b) (4)
L782: (b) (4)
L783: This reviewer thinks that none of this is warranted.

L787: 201. Cumulative exposure in clinical trials and since the first marketed use of Xeomin® on May 31, 2005 is estimated at (b) (4) vials and about (b) (4) patients.

L802: ## Suggested label changes:
L803: A preliminary review of the label (version of March 1, 2010) by this reviewer prompts the following preliminary suggestions for change by label section:
L804: ## 1.2 Benign Essential Blepharospasm - suggested change to Blepharospasm
L805: Because the clinical phenomenon of blepharospasm is often found accompanied by other movement disorder phenomena (one third of the subjects in this trial had other craniofacial dystonia), it is recommended that an indication be granted for the treatment of blepharospasm as a symptom in addition to the syndrome of BEB. This makes the indication consistent with the BOTOX® label in which a similar population was studied.
L806: (From the BOTOX® label: **1.4 Blepharospasm and Strabismus** BOTOX is indicated for the treatment of strabismus and blepharospasm associated with dystonia, including benign essential blepharospasm or VII nerve disorders in patients 12 years of age and above.)
L807: NT 201 was tested in subjects who had had previous successful treatment with BOTOX®. It is reasonable to view this as enrichment of the study population. As a result, it is unknown what the magnitude of drug effect might be in a treatment naïve population with blepharospasm. However, there is no clinical *a priori* evidence to suggest that the response in these two populations would differ greatly. The potential exceptions would be for a subgroup of poor responders, who would have been excluded from this trial, and treatment naïve subjects who could have a more robust response to initial treatment. A conservative approach erring on the side of safety would be to indicate NT 201 only for blepharospasm patients previously treated with BOTOX®.
L808: ## 2.2 Benign Essential Blepharospasm
L809: Trials defined the areas of injection topically in diagrams, not by anatomical names of location. It may be clearer to illustrate the sites of injection, the frequency and the dose used, using the illustration like that in the study report (see Figure 10 and Table 8 in this review).
L810: ## 2.3 General Dosage and Administration Information
L811: This reviewer suggests that the claim of [REDACTED] (b) (4) [REDACTED] is not substantiated. The cited reference, Roggenkämper, et al, 2006, is a published report of Trial 003 reviewed above in Section 5.5.3 of this review.
L812: Dose and exposure data suggests a maximum dose of 35 units NT 201 per eye.
L813: ## 2.3.2 Administration
L814: No data was submitted to support the statement that [REDACTED] (b) (4) [REDACTED] I am concerned that employing this strategy in blepharospasm would likely lead to more unintended treatment emergent adverse effect because of the small size of the targeted muscles.

I suspect it is written this way because the literature suggests that a change of one point in the JRS-S was the prespecified margin for a clinically significant difference.

L825: ## 9.4 Dataset Requests to Sponsor
L826: The datasets from the confirmatory blepharospasm trial and its open label follow-up extension trial were uninterpretable as submitted. The datasets originally submitted electronically to the eCTD did not adhere to a standard structure i.e.: they were neither CDISC SDTM nor ADaM compliant and they lacked a XML backbone or a useable key to interpretation of the variable domains. For this reason a request for restructuring of the eCTD analysis datasets were made at the time of acceptance of the submission for filing. This request (which follows) was answered promptly and satisfactorily allowing the review to proceed.

L870: ## **Calculation for the outcome for the primary efficacy outcome:**
L871: Using the LOCF method for replacement of missing values, the mean (SD) change in the JRS Severity subscore from the Baseline Visit to Visit 4 (Week 6) in the ITT population was -0.83 (1.18) points in the NT 201 group and 0.21 (0.91) points in the Placebo group. JRS Severity subscore values decreased in the NT 201 group from 3.12 points at baseline to 2.29 points at Visit 4 and increased in the Placebo group from 2.94 points to 3.15 points. Adjusted treatment difference: 1.0 (95% CI= [-1.4, 0.5]), p&lt;0.001 for simple model with no covariates beyond baseline JRSS score.

L875: ## **Addendum to:**
L876: ## **5.3.3 Supportive trial of NT 201 compared to BOTOX® in blepharospasm (MRZ 60201-0003)**
L877: ## **Efficacy Results (on page 90)**
L878: ## **Calculation for the outcome of the primary efficacy outcome and its relationship to the prespecified non-inferiority margin:**
L879: As noted above in the statistical analysis plan, the non-inferiority margin was defined (after Amendment 1) as irrelevant difference \Delta = 0.8 points with \alpha = 0.025 (1 sided) and with 1 - \beta = 0.85.
L880: **As is evident in the Sponsor's Table 11 below, the change in JRS sum score from baseline in the two treatment arms have substantially overlapping confidence intervals and fall within the parameters set by the sponsor. Failing to achieve a significant difference between the arms allows rejection of the null hypothesis that the two responses are different and fulfills the prespecified non-inferiority conditions.**
L881: The change in the JRS sum score at the control visit was entered as the dependent variable in an analysis of covariance, with JRS sum score at baseline, treatment group, total dose, sex, age, number of injections since diagnosis of blepharospasm, pooled country, and treatment*pooled country as the independent variables. The results from the full ANCOVA model are presented in the Sponsor's Test Table 11 below:
L882: **Text Table 11 Full ANCOVA Model of the Change from Baseline in JRS Sum Score to the Control Visit (TPP)**
L883: Treatment	LSMean	95% Confidence Interval	p-value
Lower Limit	Upper Limit
NT 201	-2.779725	-3.208636	-2.350814	<0.0001
BTXCo	-2.528504	-2.963093	-2.093915	<0.0001
NT 201-BTXCo	-0.251221	-0.719848	0.217407	0.2920
L884: Data source: Table 14.2.1.1.3.1
L885: **The final ANCOVA model (Sponsor's Text Table 12, below) included only those variables having an influence on the primary efficacy variable of p&lt;0.2. These covariates were: baseline mean JRS sum score (F value=27.66, p&lt;0.0001), pooled country (F value=3.62, p=0.007), and dose (F value=6.81, p=0.01). From this model, the least squares mean decrease in the JRS sum score from baseline was -2.90 in the NT 201 group and -2.67 in the BOTOX group. Both values represent a significant change from mean baseline values (p&lt;0.0001), indicating**
L886: that the two treatments effectively improved blepharospasm symptoms as assessed by the JRS, while still fulfilling the non-inferiority margin.

L890: ## Review and Evaluation of Clinical Data
## Safety Team Leader Memorandum
L891: **BLA:** 1235360 and 125360 (Supplement 1)
**Drug:** Botulinum Toxin (Xeomin)
**Indication:** Cervical Dystonia and Blepharospasm
**Sponsor:** Merz
**Submission Date:** 7/2/09
**Review Date:** 7/2/10
**Reviewer:** Sally Usdin Yasuda, Safety Team Leader
Neurology Drug Products, HFD-120
L892: ## 1. Background
L893: Xeomin (NT 201; incobotulinumtoxinA) is a purified formulation of Botulinum neurotoxin Type A that according to the sponsor is free from complexing proteins. According to the sponsor, [REDACTED] (b) (4) is the active substance in NT 201. NT 201 includes the excipients sucrose and albumin [REDACTED] (b) (4)
L894: [REDACTED] respectively, according to the Sponsor. The mechanism of action is thought to be due to blockade of acetylcholine release at cholinergic nerve terminals. The original BLA for Xeomin was submitted for [REDACTED] (b) indications: cervical dystonia, blepharospasm, [REDACTED] (b) (4)
L895: [REDACTED] However, the safety review includes the safety data from the spasticity [REDACTED] (b) (4) Clinical studies conducted to evaluate the use of Xeomin to treat glabellar frown lines and crow's feet contribute to the safety review.
L896: FDA and the sponsor developed a list of Adverse Events of Special Interest (AESIs) based on adverse events observed with previously developed botulinum toxin products, and these were a focus of the safety review. These included AEs such as dysphagia, speech problems, facial weakness, general body weakness, and dyspnea or other respiratory problems associated with spread of toxin effect.
L897: This memorandum primarily summarizes the findings of Dr. Lisa Jones' primary safety review of the Xeomin BLA. Please refer to Dr. Jones' review for more detail.
L898: ## 2. Summary of Findings from the Safety Review
L899: ## 2.1 Sources of Data
L900: The clinical data evaluated in the safety review are from studies submitted as part of the BLA, and include studies in patients treated for cervical dystonia (CD), benign essential blepharospasm (BEB), and spasticity (SP) that were included in
L901: ## Safety Team Leader Memo
## BLA 125360
L902: the Sponsor's pooled database, as well as glabellar frown lines (GFL), and crow's feet (CF). Dr. Jones includes analyses of pooled studies in order to adequately capture all deaths and serious adverse events (SAEs) and to increase the number of patients for subgroup analyses. However, due to limitations of pooling, she has also considered the safety populations for cervical dystonia, blepharospasm, and spasticity separately. There were 6 placebo-controlled trials included in the ISS as well as 3 active control studies using Botox as the active control and there was also 1 controlled study with different dilutions of NT201 that contributed to the pooled safety database. In addition, Studies 0520/1 and 0527 were placebo controlled studies in Glabellar Frown Lines, (GFL) and Study 0617 was a placebo controlled study in Crow's feet (CF) that Dr. Jones reviewed for deaths and SAEs. Dr. Jones has also considered the postmarketing experience in her review. The placebo and active controlled studies in BEB, CD and SP are shown below, as extracted from Dr. Jones' review.

L910: NT201 is approved in Argentina, Canada, Mexico, Uruguay, and several European countries for the indications of blepharospasm and cervical dystonia, in Canada for post-stroke upper limb spasticity, and in Argentina also for a variety of indications including strabismus and temporary improvement of glabellar frown lines.
L911: **Exposure** - ICH guidelines recommend that 1500 patients should be treated overall in a drug development program, 300-600 for 6 months, and 100 for at least 1 year at the dose or dose range believed to be efficacious. During the pre-BLA period, FDA discussed appropriate exposure guidelines with Merz (b) (4)
L912: For BEB and for CD, there were no explicit guidelines for exposure stated in pre-BLA minutes, although it was stated that 85 patients with cervical dystonia will have been treated for one year at 240 units. In total, 1313 subjects received NT201 in the pooled studies. This included 431 for CD and 222 for BEB, as well as the subjects exposed at the higher doses given for spasticity.
L913: For **BEB**, the proposed initial dose is up to (b) (4) per eye, and the recommended total Xeomin dose is up to (b) (4) per eye. In Study 003 (n=148), the dose was a single IM injection of \le 35 U per eye. In study 0433/1 (Main period) patients received a single dose of up to 50 U per eye and in 0433/2 (OLEX) patients received up to 5 IM injections of up to 50 U per eye at least 6 weeks apart over 48 weeks. In 0433 (n=106 for Main period and OLEX), 54 patients received 5 or 6 injections over 24 to 48 weeks, according to Table 11 in Dr Jones' review. While the total exposures may give an indication of overall safety, particularly
L914: when including higher doses used in spasticity, I note that for specific doses in blepharospasm there were few patients with long-term exposure to a doses of 50 U/eye (100 U for both eyes total) for blepharospasm. As extracted from Dr.

L917: In a 6/17/10 response to an FDA information request, the Sponsor has also performed this analysis for the data from the combined NDA filing and 120 day safety update. Excluding subjects with dosing intervals greater than 12 weeks, the Sponsor reports that only 2 subjects had 4 or more doses of 75 to \le 99 U of Xeomin over 48 weeks and 4 subjects had 4 or more doses of 50 to \le 74 U of Xeomin over 48 weeks. Thus, the exposure data for doses greater than 50 or 70 units is sparse.
