L921: I have examined the analysis dataset OLEFCD.xpt from 0408/2 and have found that 71 total subjects had a dose of 240 U, the maximum dose in that study. Of those, 38 subjects had 4 or 5 doses of 240 U, confirming the results in the Sponsor's table, above. The Sponsor's 6/17/10 response to the FDA's information request regarding exposure shows that 8 subjects had 4 or more injections (every 12 weeks or less) of 240 U or more over 48 weeks, and 4 subjects had 4 or more injections of 120 to ≤ 239 U (every 12 weeks or less) over 48 weeks. The largest experience for injections every 12 weeks or less was for subjects who had 3 or more injections over 6 months (n=36 for doses of 240 U or more, and n=31 for doses of 120 to ≤ 239 U).
L922: *Capture of Adverse Events* - Dr. Jones has evaluated the methods used to elicit adverse events and finds that they were adequate but not ideal. The majority of studies, with the exception of BEB study 0433, appeared not to have used checklists to ask specifically about known adverse events associated with botulinum toxin. She is concerned that some patients may not mention certain adverse events associated with botulinum toxin to investigators believing that because this is already a known event it does not need to be tracked further. I agree with Dr. Jones' recommendation to use checklists to more adequately capture adverse events in future studies.
L923: ## 2.3 Significant Safety Findings
L924: ## 2.3.1 Deaths
L925: Dr. Jones notes that there were 6 deaths in the Xeomin development program for all indications. Two were in placebo treated patients (1 in whom the cause of death was myocardial infarction and 1 in whom the cause of death was intracranial hematoma), 1 was in a Botox-treated patient who died of metastatic colon cancer diagnosed within months of entering the study, and 3 were in patients treated with NT 201. The deaths in patients on NT 201 all occurred in the spasticity studies during the OLEX period and are described as follows.
L926: Subject 1054 was a 72 y.o. male with a history of multiple cardiovascular risk factors (CAD, hypertension, prior aortic aneurysm, former smoker), with a history of CVA prior to the study and a second CVA 1 week prior to death, who died in his sleep 136 days after the last dose of NT 201; there was no autopsy. I agree with Dr. Jones given the patient's past medical history, recent CVA, and the
L927: event occurring approximately 19 weeks after the last dose of NT 201 (outside of the expected duration of effect of NT201), it seems unlikely related to NT201.
L928: Subject 1058 was a 67 y.o. male who died as a consequence of a “severe cardiac arrest” 44 days after an injection of 400 U of NT 201. He had a past medical history significant for hypertension, CVA 2.5 years prior, and was a former smoker. Dr. Jones notes that laboratory and diagnostic tests were not done and an autopsy was not performed. I agree with Dr. Jones’ assessment that the cause of death is unknown.
L929: Subject 1169 was a 71 y.o. male who died of a severe CVA (according to communication from the subject’s wife to the investigator) 21 days after his last dose of NT201. He had a history of prior CVA and hypertension and was a former smoker. There was no post-mortem examination.
L930: I agree with Dr. Jones’ assessment that there does not appear to be a pattern clearly suggesting that the deaths were drug-related.
L931: ## 2.3.2 Nonfatal Serious Adverse Events
L932: *Overview of SAEs in the pooled safety population (including non-neurologic indications)* – Dr. Jones notes that 2.9% (43/1476) of subjects who received NT 201 in the pooled and ongoing studies had a SAE compared to 5/356 (1.4%) of subjects who received placebo and 12/471 (2.5%) who received Botox. In the pooled studies, the System Organ Class (SOC) groupings with the most SAEs after exposure to NT 201 (n=1313) were Nervous system disorders (0.6%), Gastrointestinal disorders (0.5%) and Infections and Infestations (0.5%). Dr. Jones further considers the SAEs by indication as discussed below.
L933: No subjects exposed to NT 201 experienced SAEs of hepatic failure, hepatitis, rash, Stevens Johnson syndrome, Toxic epidermal necrolysis, angioedema, rhabdomyolysis, or aplastic anemia.
L934: SAEs in BEB – There were no SAEs in the Phase 2 study 0703. There was only 1 SAE in the placebo controlled BEB trials, a case of dyspnea in a patient who received placebo. In the OLEX trial, there were 16 SAEs that included one case each of pancreatitis, dyspnea, convulsion, and bronchitis. No SAEs occurred in more than one patient. The case of dyspnea occurred in a 78 y.o. man in the setting of congestive heart failure 4 months after the last dose of NT 201. I agree with Dr. Jones that this does not appear to be a spread of toxin effect after administration of Xeomin. The SAE of convulsion (tonic-clonic seizure, followed by a grand mal seizure after arrival at the hospital) occurred in a 22 y.o. female (subject 2174) approximately 12 weeks following administration of 20 U NT 201; the subject was treated with Ativan and oxygen and was released from the hospital 2 days after the event. This event does not seem likely to be related to NT 201.
L935: In BEB study 003, 3/148 subjects (2%) in the NT 201 study and 5/152 (3.3%) in the Botox group experienced SAEs. The 3 SAEs in the NT201 group were abdominal pain, enterocolitis, and hemorrhagic diverticulum. Dr. Jones notes that in placebo-controlled studies in all indications, there was only 1 gastrointestinal SAE.
L936: SAEs in CD - In the Main Period of Study 408/1, 4 subjects in the NT 201 group (2.5%) experienced SAEs compared with no subject in the placebo group. These were appendicitis, asthma exacerbation, COPD exacerbation, and staphylococcal infection. The case of COPD exacerbation occurred in a 58 y.o. male (subject #1128) with concomitant diseases that included COPD/asthma treated with albuterol and with fluticasone/salmeterol inhalers, intermittent bronchitis, and Parkinson's disease. The subject received a single 240 U dose of NT 201 IM on 06-Mar-2007. On (b) (6), the subject presented to the ER complaining of progressive shortness of breath and cough over the previous 4 days, with occasional sputum production and one episode of blood-streaked sputum. The subject was hospitalized and treated with medications including IV Solu-Medrol, Levaquin, albuterol/Atrovent nebulizer, and Advair Discus. The event was considered resolved on (b) (6). It is difficult to know how Xeomin may have contributed to the event. Dyspnea has been reported in patients treated for cervical dystonia with Botox, Dysport, and Myobloc, according to the approved labeling for those drugs.
L937: In the CD OLEX study 0408/2, Dr. Jones reports that 11 subjects experienced 16 SAEs. Dr. Jones notes that the SAEs generally occurred in only 1 patient and no pattern was apparent. She notes 1 case of respiratory failure that occurred following surgery for possible intra-abdominal bleeding, 6 weeks after the dose of NT 201. I agree with Dr. Jones that the event appeared related to the surgery, rather than the NT 201 administration. There were 2 cases of pancreatitis, one that occurred in the setting of calculi blockage of the gall bladder and common bile duct approximately 3 months after the dose of NT 201, and one that occurred approximately 1 month after the last dose of NT 201 in a patient with high alcohol consumption (12 cans of beer per day over the past 3 months). In the other CD studies, there were 30 SAEs in 23 subjects, 17 of whom had received NT 201 and 6 had received Botox.
L938: Overall, in the CD studies, Dr. Jones notes that the SAEs which occurred in more than 1 patient were neoplasm (7 patients), myocardial infarction (2 patients1), transient ischemic attacks/CVA (2 patients on Botox; 1 on NT 201 that was a 50
L939: 1 Subject 1007 was a 66 y.o. male with a history of smoking; MI 25 days after last dose; occlusion of left coronary artery was diagnosed and stent was placed; other treatment included aspirin, bisoprolol, ramipril, simvastatin. Subject 1020 was a 65 y.o. male with a history of diabetes mellitus and hyperlipidemia; event occurred approximately 10 weeks after last dose. Dr. Jones noted 3 patients with myocardial infarction in her review. However, further discussion by email on 6/24/10 clarified that subject 1007 was counted twice in her review.
L940: y.o. male with a history of hypertension, angina, diabetes, and depression, who had a concussion following a fall after mild positional vertigo) and pancreatitis (2 patients). Dr. Jones notes that with the exception of pancreatitis, these are generally prevalent events in the population.2 Human carcinogenicity is discussed in section 2.3.13 of this memo.
L941: **SAEs in Spasticity** – In the placebo controlled spasticity study 0410/1 Main period, 4/73 subjects (5.5%) in the NT 201 group and 1/75 (1.3%) subjects in the placebo group experienced SAEs. In the NT 201 group these included bronchitis chronic; cellulitis; epilepsy, paraparesis, status epilepticus; and hypertension, vertigo. Intracranial hematoma occurred in a placebo treated patient. In the OLEX period of 0410/2, 26/145 subjects (17.9%) experienced SAEs. This included 1 patient with inadequate control of diabetes mellitus 13 weeks after the last dose of NT 201 in a patient with a history of diabetes. There did not appear to be a pattern to the events in the OLEX period. In the other spasticity studies, a total of 17 subjects experienced a total of 23 SAEs. Dr. Jones finds that SAEs in the spasticity development program were typical of an older patient population with a history of a prior CVA (including myocardial infarction, CVA/TIA, and pulmonary issues). She notes 6 cases of “epilepsy” and one of “convulsion” among the spasticity SAEs. Dr. Jones notes that CVA can cause seizures. She confirmed that 3 of these 7 cases had a prior history of epilepsy or “epileptic aura” and 1 patient was diagnosed with epilepsy 3 months subsequent to the event; 1 patient had a seizure approximately 4 months after the last dose (and after recent discontinuation of clobazam); 2 patients had no prior history of epilepsy or seizure (1 in which the event occurred approximately 12 weeks after the last dose of NT 201 and 1 that occurred 7 days after the dose in the OLEX). Dr. Jones also reports no cases of epilepsy in CD or in controlled studies for BEB. She considers the epilepsy/seizures as likely not related to NT 201 treatment. I agree that it is difficult to attribute these events to NT 201. I note that the postmarketing section of the Botox label states, “New onset or recurrent seizures have also been reported, typically in patients who are predisposed to experiencing these events. The exact relationship of these events to the botulinum toxin injection has not been established”.
L942: **SAEs – anaphylaxis** - Dr. Jones notes that there has been 1 post-marketing report of anaphylaxis following Xeomin administration. This occurred within 3 to 5 minutes after injection of Xeomin 50 U for glabellar lines in a patient who had had 5 prior treatments with Botox. The symptoms were characterized by palpitations, faintness, and nausea lasting for 36 hours. There was no further information on treatment of this event.
L943: In summary, the review of SAEs does not demonstrate a particular pattern in any indication or overall.
L944: ## 2.3.3 Dropouts and/or Discontinuations
L945: 2 In the CD studies, NT 201-treated patients were 18-75 y.o. (mean 53 y.o.).
L946: In the pooled controlled studies in the NT 201 development program, 6/1313 subjects (0.5%) in the NT 201 treatment group discontinued due to a treatment emergent adverse event (TEAE) compared with 1/396 (0.3%) Botox-treated subjects and 1/346 (0.3%) placebo-treated subjects. In the NT 201 group, these events included muscular weakness (2 subjects), dizziness, neck pain, musculoskeletal pain, nausea, and neoplasm (1 subject each) in the CD controlled studies and paraparesis and TIA (1 subject each) in the spasticity controlled studies according to Dr. Jones' review. The event leading to dropout in the placebo group was intracranial hematoma and the event in the Botox-treated patient was ankle fracture. According to Dr. Jones' review, there were no discontinuations due to adverse events in the BEB studies.
L947: In placebo controlled CD study 0408/1 the 3 NT 201-treated subjects that withdrew due to a TEAE included 2 of the 72-treatment naïve subjects (2.8%) and 1 of the 87 pre-treated subjects (1.2%). Two subjects were in the 240 U group (2/81, 2.5%) and 1 was in the 120 U group (1/78, 1.3%).
L948: In addition to the dropouts in the controlled studies for Spasticity, there were 7 NT 201-treated patients that discontinued OLEX study 0410/2 because of a TEAE. Two were due to death and have been previously discussed in this memo; the other 5 were injection site pain, dysphagia, CVA, hypokinesia, and colon cancer. Dr. Jones reviewed the narrative for the dysphagia event, and found it to be lacking in detail. Upon request, the sponsor provided more information on this subject on 6/8/10. The subject had a past medical history significant for ischemic cerebrovascular accident. He received a 310 U dose of NT201 on (b) (6) in the OLEX and experienced an episode of dysphagia 9 days later. The event resolved on an unknown date. On (b) (6), 1 day after the last injection with NT 201 (325 U) the subject developed a second TEAE of dysphagia, and the event resolved on an unspecified date. Study drug was withdrawn consequently.
L949: I agree with Dr. Jones that there does not appear to be a pattern among the patients discontinuing due to adverse events.
L950: No subjects taking NT 201 discontinued for hepatic failure, hepatitis, Stevens Johnson syndrome, toxic epidermal necrolysis, angioedema, anaphylaxis, rhabdomyolysis, pancytopenia, or aplastic anemia, based on a search of the database by Dr Jones.
L951: ## 2.3.4 Significant Adverse Events
L952: Adverse events of special interest identified prior to the submission of the BLA included spread of toxin effect, hyperglycemia, and elevation of alkaline phosphatase. Dr. Jones has reviewed these adverse events of interest. I will summarize her findings here. Please refer to her review for a more detailed discussion.
L953: *Spread of Toxin Effect* – Events related to spread of toxin effect are referred to as adverse events of special interest (AEI) in Dr. Jones' review. The sponsor was requested to monitor for AESIs according to a list based on such events provided by FDA. In placebo-controlled studies, according to Dr. Jones' review, Merz reported that the overall incidence of AESIs in the NT 201 treatment group was higher than in the placebo group. At the PT level in the pooled indications, the only AEI that occurred with a greater incidence in the NT 201 group compared with placebo with a difference of \ge 2\% was dysphagia (28/701 NT 201 subjects, 4% vs 5/346 placebo subjects, 1.5%).
L954: In the BEB study 0433/1 Main period, 32/74 subjects (43.2%) in the NT 201 group and 9/34 (29.5%) in the placebo group experienced AESIs. In the NT 201 group, the most common were eyelid ptosis (18.9%), dry mouth (16.2%), vision blurred (5.4%), and dyspnea (5.4%). The doses for the cases of dry mouth ranged from 32.5 – 100 U and occurred within 2-4 weeks of administration. I agree with Dr. Jones that the time course of dry mouth suggests a causal association. The dyspnea occurred at doses of 50-90 U. Three occurred within 3 to 6 weeks, and 1 occurred approximately 13 weeks after the dose. In the placebo group the most common AESIs were eyelid ptosis (8.8%), vision blurred, dysphagia, and muscular weakness (each in 5.9%). Dr. Jones notes that the occurrence of eyelid ptosis in the placebo group suggests that this may be due to some extent to the injection or related to patient expectations. Dr. Jones states that with respect to the occurrence of AESIs, similar findings were observed when the OLEX data were combined with the 433/1 data.
L955: In the Main period of 0408/1 in CD, the overall incidence of AESIs was 23.3% for NT 201 treated subjects (37/159) compared to 4.1% in placebo treated subjects (3/74). In pooled controlled CD studies, the results were similar (16% for NT 201 vs 4.1% in placebo treated subjects). For NT 201 treated subjects, the most common of these events were dysphagia (12.3%) and muscular weakness (4.2%). All other AESIs occurred in &lt; 1% of NT 201 treated subjects. The Sponsor notes that the incidence of AESIs appears to decrease in NT 201 treated subjects with moderate to severe disease at baseline compared to those with mild disease, and hypothesizes that this is due to more hypertrophic muscles and larger tissue mass in the more severe disease, presenting a greater barrier to diffusion. Dr. Jones finds this a reasonable hypothesis, as do I. Of note, the Botox label states that “Patients with smaller neck muscle mass and patients who require bilateral injections into the sternocleidomastoid muscle have been reported to be at greater risk for dysphagia”, supporting this hypothesis. In addition, according to the sponsor, incidence of AESIs with NT 201 was higher in patients who were treatment naïve at baseline (in 0408/1 it was 23% for toxin-naïve patients vs 14% for pretreated patients according to table 3.5.10.2.1.1.1 in the ISS). The sponsor believes that this was most likely due to enrollment bias in that subjects with prior botulinum toxin experience enrolled in the study are those
L956: with no adverse experience in previous treatment. I agree with Dr. Jones that this seems like a reasonable hypothesis.
L957: In Spasticity Study 0410/1 Main period, 1/73 (1.4%) subjects in the NT 201 group reported AESIs (constipation and paraparesis) compared to 1/75 (1.3%) in the placebo group (dysphagia). However, when the controlled studies (0410/1 and 0607) were pooled, 6/257 (2.3%) subjects in the NT 201 treatment group had AESIs. For NT 201-treated subjects, the most common of these events was muscular weakness (3 subjects 1.1%). All other AESIs (dysphagia, constipation, dysarthria, and paraparesis) occurred in only 1 subject. In the 0410/2 OLEX Period, 9 subjects experienced AESIs (muscular weakness, hemiparesis, dysphagia, dry mouth).
L958: The occurrence of dry mouth and dyspnea in the BEB program would appear to be due to spread of toxin effect beyond the immediate area of injection. There appears to be little evidence of spread in the CD program or in the spasticity program. I agree with Dr. Jones' assessment that the lack of a signal for systemic spread in the NT 201 development program should not be interpreted as a confirmation that systemic spread of toxin effect will not occur.
L959: **Hyperglycemia** – Based on a small increase in glucose noted in the Dysport label, FDA requested that the sponsor perform an analysis of glucose within the NT 201 development program. Dr. Jones has reviewed the Sponsor's data and agrees with the Sponsor that in the pooled data, there was a slight increase of 1.1 (1.38 mmol/L) with NT 201 treatment to primary endpoint assessment in the &gt; 360 U treatment group in Spasticity, but that this did not persist when change from baseline to final visit was analyzed, and that a larger increase was observed in the placebo group. In BEB, Dr. Jones finds that glucose levels over the course of the study and between NT 201 and placebo groups were “essentially equivalent”. There were 2 patients who had a high “clinically significant” serum glucose value in 0433/1 Main Period and 0433/2 OLEX period. One patient (2011) who had no previous history of hyperglycemia or diabetes mellitus had glucose values at screening and before the second injection that were within normal limits (68-110 mg/dl), but had elevated levels of &gt; 140 mg/dl before the 3rd and 5th injections and at the final visit. However, as any effect of NT201 would be expected to have worn off prior to the next injection, it does not seem that NT 201 would have contributed. Patient 2129 was noted to have hyperglycemia at screening with a serum glucose value of 126, and levels of 173 and 126 mg/dl before the 2nd injection and last observation visit, respectively. In CD there were no clinically important mean changes or shifts from baseline to final visit in NT 201-treated patients according to Dr. Jones. No clinically meaningful difference was seen when 0 408/1 and 0408/2 were analyzed together. There were 2 treatment-emergent cases of abnormal serum glucose, 1 with a high serum glucose value of 7.5 mmol/L (normal range 3.8 to 6.1 mmol/L) before injection 4, but within normal limits at screening and before the 5th injection, and 1 patient with a low serum glucose at the last study visit. In
L960: Spasticity, 1 subject had an SAE of hyperglycemia 13 weeks after the last dose, as noted under the SAE section, and 6 subjects with normal serum glucose values at the first injection (5 of whom had a pre-existing history of diabetes) experienced abnormalities in serum glucose values while receiving NT 201. Overall, the mean glucose values compared from initial study visit to primary endpoint visit and final visit with the controlled trials for NT 201, placebo, and Botox were similar, according to Dr. Jones' review. Data to support a clear role for Xeomin in causing hyperglycemia is lacking.
L961: Alkaline Phosphatase – The Sponsor was asked to perform a variety of analyses to assess alkaline phosphatase levels in the development program. Dr. Jones has reviewed the data for all indications and finds no trend toward an increase in alkaline phosphatase. However, she notes that testing was performed in the controlled trials at baseline and at the final visit, not at the primary endpoint visit. The analysis is not useful in evaluating effects on alkaline phosphatase.
L962: ## 2.3.5 Common Adverse Events
L963: Overall, in the pooled, placebo controlled trials, 368/701 (52.5%) NT 201 treated patients and 140/346 (40.5%) placebo treated patients experienced treatment emergent adverse events. Among the most common adverse events (\ge 2\% and greater than placebo) in the controlled trials were sinusitis (2% vs 1.5%), headache (10% vs 7%), neck pain (2.5% vs 0.9%), muscular weakness (2% vs 0.9%), dysphagia (4% vs 1.5%), dry mouth (2% vs 0.3%), eyelid ptosis (2% vs 0.9%), and dry eye (2% vs 1%), according to Section 7.4.1.2 of Dr. Jones' review (Sponsor table 5.3.5.3-15). These AEs are common within the botulinum toxin class in general. As Dr. Jones notes, a table of common adverse events is poorly suited to pooling indications because the AEs are indication specific to some extent.
L964: In BEB, 41.4% of NT 201 treated patients, 70.3% of NT 201 treated patients in placebo-controlled studies, and 61.8% of placebo treated patients experienced TEAEs. After the first injection, 66% of patients in study 0433 Main experienced TEAEs. However, in the OLEX period, approximately 39-52% experienced TEAEs, with the largest percentage at Injection 3, and remaining relatively constant from Injection 4 through injection 6. The adverse events occurring in \ge 3\% of NT 201 treated subjects and greater than placebo were eyelid ptosis, dry eye, dry mouth, diarrhea, headache, visual disturbance, dyspnea, nasopharyngitis, respiratory tract infection, vision impaired, asthenia, and asthenia. When compared by dose (placebo vs 10-40 U NT201 vs &gt; 40 U NT 201), several adverse events in 0433/1 appeared to be dose related including eyelid ptosis, visual impairment, and diarrhea. The most common AEs in the open label studies were eyelid ptosis, dry eye, dry mouth, and visual disturbance.
L965: In CD in the placebo controlled studies, the adverse events occurring in \ge 3\% of NT 201 treated subjects and greater than placebo were neck pain, muscular weakness, musculoskeletal pain, and musculoskeletal stiffness, dysphagia,
L966: nausea, and sinusitis. TEAEs in the extension period were similar to those in the MAIN period.
L967: In the Spasticity placebo controlled study, the AEs in the NT 201 group with the largest increase compared to placebo treated patients were headache, epilepsy, and hyperglycemia. The 2 cases of epilepsy had a history of epilepsy prior to entering the study. In the OLEX study, the most frequent adverse events were muscle spasticity, depression, upper respiratory tract infection, and muscular weakness.
L968: ## 2.3.6 Laboratory findings
L969: *Chemistry and Hematology* – I note that the placebo controlled studies in BEB and CD (0433/1 and 0408/1) did not measure laboratory parameters at the time of the primary endpoint, although the active control trials 0003 and 0013 and the placebo controlled spasticity trial 0410 did. In the pooled controlled studies, 41/131 NT treated subjects (3.1%) and 14/346 subjects (4%) in the placebo group had treatment-emergent, clinically significant (CS) abnormalities of biochemistry parameters. In the NT 201 group these were elevated glucose, total cholesterol, LDL cholesterol, GGT, AST, ALT, urea, bilirubin, alkaline phosphatase, and creatinine, and low HDL, potassium, and sodium. However, there were no clinically meaningful shifts or mean changes from baseline in most biochemistry parameters. For hematology studies fewer patients in the NT 201 group than in the placebo group experienced clinically significant laboratory abnormalities (7/1313 NT 201 subjects (0.5%) and 4/346 placebo subjects (1.2%) in the pooled controlled studies. Abnormalities in the NT 201 group were low hemoglobin, erythrocytes, platelets, hematocrit, relative reticulocytes, and elevated eosinophils. These were all detected at the final visit of the respective study, which in every study occurred weeks after receipt of a single dose.
L970: In the pooled BEB studies, Dr. Jones found that hematology values and clinical chemistry parameters were similar for treatment group and from screening to final visit in Study 0443/1 Main period. However, Dr. Jones notes the small number of patients on which these results are based (73 in the NT 201 group and 34 in the placebo group).
L971: In the pooled CD studies and in the pooled spasticity studies, Dr. Jones agrees with the Sponsor's findings that there were no clinically meaningful shifts or mean changes from baseline in NT 201-treated patients for hematology data or for biochemistry laboratory testing, including glucose. I also agree.
L972: The data that are available do not suggest meaningful changes in laboratory findings.
L973: ## 2.3.7 Vital Signs
L974: Dr. Jones reports that although BEB showed a slight decrease in heart rate from baseline to final visit for NT 201 (mean 71.9 vs 70.2 bpm) that was not seen in
L975: placebo (mean 70.9 vs 71.3 bpm), this was not replicated in other indications. Other vital signs were not different compared to placebo or over time. Vital signs were performed prior to injection during the study visit, and therefore are not likely to represent an effect of the drug.
L976: ## 2.3.8 Electrocardiograms
L977: According to Dr. Jones' review, [REDACTED] in human blood had no effect on hERG tail currents. The sponsor has extrapolated the results, at concentrations of [REDACTED] that exceed achievable concentrations in humans, to [REDACTED]. In male Cynomolgus monkeys, a single IM dose of 16 LUD/kg resulted in a decrease in heart rate 2-3 hours after injection and induced an increase in QTcF of less than 20 ms. As noted in Dr. Jones' review, according to Dr. Barbara Wilcox, a dose of 16 LDU/kg represents 4 times the maximum human dose for CD and 9.4X the maximum human dose for BEB.
L978: In the type B Meeting on September 14, 2004, the FDA stated that specific ECG and cardiac evaluations are generally not required for most biologics. In a 6/8/10 submission in response to an information request, the Sponsor states that in the placebo controlled trials in BEB, CD and spasticity, ECGs were assessed at baseline only. Serial ECGs were performed, however, in studies 0003 (BEB) and 0013 (CD). The sponsor has presented a table of ECG parameters at baseline, primary endpoint visit, and final visit for the pooled controlled studies and has found no clinically meaningful changes in the mean parameters. This does not seem valuable in evaluating effects of drug on the ECG for studies with various doses and very different populations.
L979: No clinically significant changes between baseline and post-baseline visits were observed in ECG parameters when serial ECGs were collected. As shown in ISS table 5.3.5.3-27 there was a mean decrease in QTcF of -3 msec for NT201 and of -1.2 msec for Botox from baseline to primary endpoint visit in the BEB study, and a change of 0.6 msec for NT 201 vs a change of -2.7 for Botox in the CD study. The sponsor states that changes of 60 ms were not present for the QTcF interval, based on the assessments of by a cardiologist associated with a CRO. These results were inadvertently not included in the original submission of the BLA but were included in a 6/16/10 response from the sponsor regarding the ECG assessments. I note that according to the response to an additional information request, the sponsor states that ECGs were printed for 10 seconds at 50 mms/s recording speed, and that no supine position was necessary before measurement. Measurement of intervals was to be done from 3 (possibly consecutive) normal heart complexes in Lead II, and in the case of non-measurable lead II tracings, all tracings of a subject were to be measured in an alternative lead (V5). Given the inconsistencies in the measurement of the ECGs (e.g. not necessarily in supine position without the patient at rest, not measuring consecutive beats), it is difficult to know the reliability of any measurements made in these studies. However, based on these results and on the adverse event profile of NT 201, drug-induced ECG changes do not appear to be
L980: associated with NT201, consistent with the adverse event profiles of the other members of this class of drugs.
L981: An additional ECG assessment was performed if an adverse event of the cardiovascular system occurred. This occurred in 1 patient in study 0410 (spasticity) in which the sponsor states in the 6/8/10 submission that a further ECG assessment was performed at final visit after a syncope.
L982: ## 2.3.9 Special Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
L983: Merz conducted several studies to examine systemic diffusion of NT 201. Study 0709 CSR evaluated migration potential of Xeomin (5 U in 0.125 ml) as compared to Vistabel (5 U in 0.125 ml) and Dysport (12.5 U in 0.125 ml) after IM injection into the forehead in healthy female volunteers with the area of anhidrosis the primary endpoint. Study 0113 was a double-blind study to evaluate dose-response profile of NT 201 in human extensor digitorum brevis (EDB) muscles and comparison of systemic diffusion of different Botulinum toxin Type A products in healthy male volunteers. It evaluated surface electroneurography of EDB on the upper surface of the foot, abductor hallucis (AH), and abductor digiti quinti (ADQ) muscles. The study compared dose of 2, 4, 16, or 32 U of NT 201 and the same doses of Botox. Neither study used clinically relevant doses in clinically relevant locations for the purposes of evaluating potential for spread for the indications of CD or BEB.
L984: ## 2.3.10 Immunogenicity
L985: Dr. Jones reports that during clinical development of NT 201, antibody assessments were performed using an Enzyme-Linked Immunosorbent Assay (ELISA) or Fluorescence ImmunoAssay for antibodies (FIA-AB) for screening, and positive samples were subsequently tested using the mouse ex vivo hemidiaphragm assay (HDA) for neutralizing antibodies. In the completed studies of NT 201, a total of 12 of 1080 subjects (1.1%) who were antibody negative at baseline developed neutralizing antibodies to botulinum toxin during the course of their study. Each of these 12 subjects had been treated with another botulinum toxin prior to exposure to NT 201. Antibodies to botulinum toxin did not develop in treatment naïve subjects after exposure to NT 201. Dr. Jones notes that the development of antibodies was similar in the NT 201 and Botox groups (0.7% vs 0.8%, respectively).
L986: Four NT 201-treated subjects in the BEB studies developed borderline values of antibodies to botulinum toxin. This included 2 subjects in Study 0003 (and in both cases antibodies were not present at the final visit) and 2 subjects in 0433/1 Main period (1 of whom became positive at the final visit and the other who was "borderline" at the final visit) according to Dr. Jones' review. Four subjects in CD study 0408/1 Main period developed antibodies during participation in the study. Four additional subjects in 0408/2 Extension Period developed antibodies to botulinum toxin. One of these subjects had positive FIA at all assessments,
L987: positive HDA before injection 3, and negative HDA at the last study visit. A second subject had positive FIA and negative HDA at baseline, and positive (borderline) at the 6th injection. A third subject had negative FIA and HDA at baseline and positive (borderline) HDA at the 4th injection visit. No patients in the spasticity studies were reported to develop neutralizing antibodies to botulinum toxin. None of the subjects who developed antibodies experienced TEAEs suggestive of immune-mediated events. According to Dr. Jones' review, the sponsor stated that most of the subjects who developed neutralizing antibodies showed a therapeutic response to treatment. I agree with Dr. Jones that a strong signal for immunogenicity has not been demonstrated, (b) (4)
L988: ## 2.3.11 Other Safety Explorations
L989: ***AE risk by Study Location*** – Dr. Jones examined the risk of serious and common adverse events in several North American studies and non-north American studies for BEB and for CD (all of the spasticity studies were conducted outside of North America). In BEB, she finds a higher incidence of AEs in North American Study 0433/1 compared to Non-North American Study 0003 and this was true for both NT 201 and for placebo, and particularly for ptosis (19% vs 6% for NT 201 for 0433/1 vs 0003) and upper respiratory tract infection (1.4% vs 0.7% for NT 201 for 0433/1 vs 0003). However, I note that the dose in Study 0003 was limited to \le 35 U per eye. Similarly, for CD, the overall AEs were greater for both NT 201 and for placebo in North American Study 0408/1 that used single doses of 120U or 240 U vs Non-North American Study 0013 that used a single dose of 70-300 U. For muscular weakness for NT 201 this was 6.4% vs 1.7% for 0408/1 vs 0013 and a smaller difference was observed for dysphagia. I agree that this should be considered when writing labeling, although I note that cross study comparisons are difficult to interpret for a variety of reasons including the differences in dose in this case.
L990: ***Dose Dependency for Adverse Events*** – Dr. Jones has considered dose and total number of injections with respect to dose dependency. For BEB, Dr. Ken Bergmann has provided the figure at right showing adverse events by dose in the placebo-controlled period of 0433/1. As Dr. Jones notes, the number of events is small, making it difficult to characterize well the dose response relationships. However, ptosis has a pattern suggesting a possible dose response relationship (9% in placebo, 15% for 10-40 U, and 20% for &gt;40 U). I also note that dyspnea was reported in 2.9% for placebo, none for 10-40 U, and 6.6% for &gt; 40 U. For doses &gt; 40 units I have further explored this event that could reflect spread of toxin effect. The 4 cases of dyspnea on NT
L991: 201 in that study had doses of 50 Units, 55.5 Units, 80 Units, or 90 Units.
L992: Dry mouth could potentially be considered a distant spread of toxin effect in the case of administration for blepharospasm. There did not appear to be an excess of dry mouth after doses of &gt; 70 U (total in both eyes) compared to lower doses, although the numbers of events were small. Narratives were available from the sponsor for 11 cases. For all but one of those cases, the event occurred within 1 to 6 weeks after the injections. Doses ranged from 32.5 to 100 U. Two of those subjects had a history of dry mouth that worsened after administration. One subject was taking chlorpheniramine concomitantly. It is difficult to know the role of Xeomin in these events. Of note, one of those subjects also had right upper lip droop that began 1 day after administration of Xeomin.
L993: Dr. Jones also assessed common adverse events by number of injections, but does not find a clear trend, noting that when stratified over 6 injections the number of patients in each subgroup is small. Given the limited exposure, as discussed in Section 2.1 of this memo, and a possible dose-response relationship for some adverse events, the appropriate upper limit of dosing should be considered.
L994: For CD, common TEAEs that demonstrated a higher risk in the 240 U group compared to the 120 U group were neck pain (15% vs 7%), muscle weakness (11% vs 7%), and dysphagia (18% vs 13%). In CD, Dr. Jones notes that the risk of adverse events decreases as the number of injections increase. I agree with her hypothesis that this may be due to “survivor bias”. As I have noted in Section 2.1, there is limited exposure for multiple doses of 240 U. The appropriate upper limit of dosing should be considered, depending on whether additional benefit is observed at higher doses.
L995: For spasticity, risk of adverse events generally increase with higher doses, although there were no strong dose response patterns, likely due to small numbers of adverse events.
L996: ## 2.3.12 Drug Interactions
L997: Dr. Jones has reviewed drug-demographic interactions. She reports that there were too few non-Caucasian subjects to analyze adverse event data by race.3 In addition, she reports that due to small numbers of patients in the subgroups, no demographic factor was demonstrated to be a modifier of (adverse) effect. Dr. Jones also does not find a clinically meaningful pattern of adverse events based on disease severity for any indication.
L998: Dr. Jones evaluated the relationship between adverse events with a status of “pre-treated” and “treatment naïve”. Three of the 222 patients in the pooled BEB
L999: 3 In BEB, 91.9% in the pooled controlled studies were Caucasian; in 0433/1 78.4% in the NT 201 group and 91.2% in the placebo group were Caucasian. In CD 97.2% in the pooled controlled studies were Caucasian; in 0408/1, 93.1% of NT 201 treated subjects and 87.8% of placebo subjects were Caucasian.
L1000: studies were botulinum toxin naïve, and therefore no assessment based on this status could be performed. For CD, Dr. Jones reports that although pre-treated patients in the CD indication seem to be over-represented among patients who experienced adverse events, the number of patients is small and the comparison not controlled for dose or disease severity.
L1001: The Sponsor did not perform drug-drug interaction studies.
L1002: ## 2.3.13 Human Carcinogenicity
L1003: According to the integrated summary of safety, in the pooled, placebo-controlled trials, treatment-emergent adverse events in the "Neoplasms benign, malignant and unspecified" SOC occurred in 5 of 701 subjects (0.7%) in the NT 201 group, compared with 4 of 346 subjects (1.2%) in the placebo group. The events that occurred in the NT 201 group were melanocytic naevus, dysplastic naevus syndrome, haemangioma, lentigo maligna stage unspecified, and skin cancer (1 subject, 0.1%, each). The events in the placebo group were melanocytic naevus (2 subjects, 0.6%), basal cell carcinoma and skin papilloma (1 subject each 0.3%). Dr. Jones notes that the cancers occurring in the placebo controlled trials were primarily skin cancers or precancerous lesions, the most common form of cancer observed in the general population.
L1004: ## 2.3.14 Human Reproduction and Pregnancy Data
L1005: There were 3 pregnancies in patients taking NT 201 in the database: 1 in CD study 0408/2, 1 in CD study 0013, and 1 in GFL study 0520/2. The subject in study 0013 had an elective abortion "for personal reasons", according to Dr. Jones' review.
L1006: The subject in study 0408/2 had a single dose of NT 201 (120 U) in the main period of the study, followed by 3 injections in the Extension study. The last injection was 11/16/07. The patient stopped taking birth control pills between the last week of November and the first week of December, 2007. She had her last menstrual period on [REDACTED] (b) (6). On [REDACTED] (b) (6) she gave birth at 37 weeks gestation to a healthy boy.
L1007: The subject in 0520/2 received the first dose of NT 201 (20 U) in the Main Period on 11/20/06 and the most recent dose on 7/9/07. At visit 10 of the Main Period (8/30/07) her pregnancy test was positive. A healthy baby was born on [REDACTED] (b) (6).
L1008: I agree with Dr. Jones that the number of pregnancies was too small to make conclusions about the effect of prenatal NT 2201 exposure. [REDACTED] (b) (4)
L1009: [REDACTED] Dr. Jones recommends that patients known to be pregnant be advised not to receive NT 201, given that the indications for NT 201
L1010: use are not-life threatening. I agree with that recommendation, unless the consideration between the patient and her physician conclude that the benefits outweigh the potential for this unknown risk.
L1011: ## 2.3.15 Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal and Rebound
L1012: According to the ISS, there is no clinical experience with NT 201 overdose. The Sponsor has included in the proposed label some information about excessive doses of botulinum toxin type A in general. I recommend that the class language from Botox and from Dysport be used in the overdose section (section 10) of the Xeomin label. This should include the language regarding the availability of antitoxin.
L1013: With respect to abuse potential, the Sponsor states that there are no reports of drug abuse with Botulinum toxin type A preparations. Dr. Jones agrees with the Sponsor that based on post-marketing results of other botulinum toxins and the apparent lack of similarity between botulinum toxin actions and known abuse mechanisms, there is little evidence of an abuse potential for NT 201. I agree. I also agree with her suggestion that withdrawal seems unlikely.
L1014: ## 2.3.16 Postmarketing Experience
L1015: I agree with Dr. Jones' assessment that the majority of events observed in the postmarketing experience are those expected with the use of a botulinum toxin product.
L1016: ## 2.3.17 Summary of Significant Safety Concerns:
L1017: Dr. Jones has evaluated the safety data for Xeomin. She has specifically evaluated safety issues of concern with the botulinum class of products including distant spread of toxin effect, as well as other issues of special interest including changes in glucose and in alkaline phosphatase. She has not identified other safety concerns in this submission that have not been already identified for botulinum toxin products.
L1018: ## 2.3.18 Postmarketing Risk Management Plan
L1019: Merz submitted a REMS with the original BLA and subsequently submitted a revision. The REMS includes a MedGuide and a Communication plan, as described in Dr. Jones' review, and a timetable for assessments.
L1020: ## 2.3.19 Conclusions
L1021: I agree with Dr. Jones' assessment that the application for Xeomin (BLA 125360) allowed for an adequate safety review, and no safety concerns that would preclude approval were identified should NT 201 be found to be effective. I recommend considering the maximum dose that should be used for either cervical dystonia or blepharospasm due to limited experience with the highest
L1022: doses the Sponsor has proposed for use and because of dose-related adverse events observed in the ISS, particularly in cervical dystonia.
L1023: The safety review identified cases of dyspnea and dry mouth in the BEB program that could be due to spread of toxin effect. Other than that, Dr. Jones did not identify any instances of systemic spread of NT 201 effect. This was largely the case for several other previously approved botulinum toxin products in which systemic spread of effect cases were primarily identified following product approval. I agree with her recommendation to include the same Boxed Warning (and class labeling regarding spread of toxin effect) in the Xeomin labeling as required for other approved botulinum toxin products. I also agree that because different formulations of botulinum toxin are not interchangeable, to avoid unintentional overdose the Division should consider some mechanism for alerting clinicians to this issue. This could be in the format of an update to the drug safety information sheet4 that was last published in August 2009.
L1024: I agree with Dr. Jones' recommendations for the following postmarket evaluations:
L1025: - The sponsor should devise a plan to monitor for systemic spread when NT 201 is used in clinical practice and to forward in an expedited manner any case reports resembling spread of toxin effect.
- For the ongoing (or future) spasticity studies, the sponsor should be asked to utilize checklists of adverse events related to botulinum toxin to ascertain adverse events.
- In future studies, the sponsor should be asked to include vital signs at baseline and at specified time periods after NT 201 treatment (for example, at an hour after treatment, at a time point where maximum clinical effect is expected, and at follow-up).
L1026: There is limited experience with Xeomin for blepharospasm with doses of greater than 50-75 units, and minimal experience in patients without previous exposure to Botox. I recommend limiting the dose to 50-75 Units and limiting the indication to the population that was studied (patients pretreated with Botox), as there is no information on appropriate dosing for patients who are toxin naive. In addition, a PMC evaluating the appropriate dose as well as safety in that group of patients should be considered.
L1027: In addition, as for the other botulinum toxin products, PMRs should include studies of safety assessing distant spread of toxin effects after multiple administrations of Xeomin during a minimum period of 12 months in adult patients with spasticity and in pediatric patients with spasticity. The safety data could come from open-label extension of clinical studies that would be performed as PMCs or from long-term controlled safety and efficacy studies, with doses as
L1028: 4http://www.fda.gov/Drugs/DrugSafety/PostmarketDrugSafetyInformationforPatientsandProviders/ucm175011.htm
L1029: ## **Safety Team Leader Memo**
## **BLA 125360**
L1030: least as high as those shown effective in those studies or those doses commonly used to treat spasticity. In addition to spread of toxin effect, evaluation of other safety markers that may be of interest such as vital signs, glucose, alkaline phosphatase, and ECGs, should be performed at a time point when peak clinical effect is expected.

L1108: ## 1 RECOMMENDATIONS/RISK BENEFIT ASSESSMENT
L1109: ## 1.1 Recommendation on Regulatory Action
L1110: - The clinical trials data meets the regulatory standard for approval of effectiveness of IncobotulinumtoxinA (Xeomin) for the 120 unit dose for the treatment of cervical dystonia.
L1111: (b) (4)
L1112: The approved recommended dose of IncobotulinumtoxinA should be 120 units. The data from the pivotal CD efficacy trial (408/1) demonstrated no added benefit of 240 units over 120 units in the treatment of Cervical Dystonia. There has been no data to support injection of doses higher than 240 units.
L1113: ## 1.2 Risk Benefit Assessment.
L1114: The clinical trials data supports the approval of the 120 (b) (4) unit dose of IncobotulinumtoxinA for following reasons:
L1115: - IncobotulinumtoxinA has demonstrated efficacy in treating patients with cervical dystonia. In the pivotal study conducted in the United States among 37 centers (408/1), the primary efficacy endpoint of change in TWSTRS total score from baseline to week 4 after injections showed a difference of -9 points in the 240 U group and -7.5 points in the 120 U group. Comparison of each IncobotulinumtoxinA group to placebo was statistically significant at p &lt; 0.001. (A more negative score signifies improvement).
- The Patient Evaluation of Global Response also showed that 67% and 64% of patients who received 240 units and 120 units respectively documented slight improvement to complete abolishment of all signs and symptoms as opposed to 20% of placebo patients.
L1116: The safety data reviewed by Dr. Lisa Jones showed that the overall incidence of TEAE was higher in the treatment group. All adverse events, especially dysphagia occurred at a greater risk when patients were treated with 240 U compared to patients injected with 120 U. Neck pain, muscular weakness and musculoskeletal pain were expected findings based on the drug's mechanism of action and administration. Muscle stiffness was also predominant among patients (3.1% in NT 201 and 1.1% in placebo). Dysphagia is one of the more serious adverse events documented in the study. These side effects are consistent with other approved botulinum toxins and there have been no new or unexpected safety issues documented in the studies. Despite this limitation, the overall risk is therapeutically acceptable and the benefits outweigh the risks.
L1117: (b) (4), it is still recommended that patients who require botulinum toxin, whether or not they are treatment naïve, be started on 120 units because of the following reasons:
L1118: - There is a dose response effect resulting in greater risks of adverse events in patients who received 240 U.
L1119: (b) (4)
L1120: (b) (4)

The label of each container or package of IncobotulinumtoxinA will include prominent instructions to authorize dispensers to provide a Medication Guide to each patient to whom the drug is dispensed, and state how the Medication Guide is provided.

The DHCP letter will be distributed to neurologists, physiatrists and other specialties and healthcare professional staff who might prescribe or inject IncobotulinumtoxinA® or other licensed botulinum toxin products (i.e., physical medical and rehabilitation specialists, dermatologists, ophthalmologists and other specialists who have previously dispensed botulinum toxins).
L1125: **C. REMS Assessments** will be submitted to FDA at (b) (4), 3 years and 7 years from the date of initial approval of the REMS. The reporting interval covered by each assessment should conclude no earlier than 60 days before the submission date for that assessment.

L1137: ## 2 INTRODUCTION AND REGULATORY BACKGROUND
L1138: ## 2.1 Product Information
L1139: IncobotulinumtoxinA (trade name Xeomin®, reviewed and approved by DMEPA on March 25, 2010 and meeting the standards of USAN) is, according to the Sponsor, a formulation of highly purified Botulinum neurotoxin type A [150kDa], which the company distinguishes from the other botulinum toxins in the market as being "free from complexing proteins (b) (4)
L1140: The drug product is supplied as a powder solution for injection (lyophilizate) packed in a (b) (4) glass vial with 50 or 100 LD50 units strength. Its proposed indications upon submission are for the treatment of Cervical Dystonia (Spasmodic Torticollis) and Benign Essential Blepharospasm. The specific indication found in the label submitted for cervical dystonia is (b) (4)
L1141: Their
L1142: (b) (4)
L1143: recommended initial dose is 120 (b) (4) U
L1144: Similar to other botulinum neurotoxin type A, IncobotulinumtoxinA acts selectively on peripheral cholinergic nerve endings, inhibiting the release of neurotransmitter acetylcholine (Ach). It is applied through intramuscular injections as a therapeutic means in dystonia and spasticity which are characterized by pathologically overactive muscles. The company differentiates its drug from other botulinum toxin A by stating that it is "highly purified" (b) (4)
L1145: These complexing proteins do not contribute to the paralytic effect of the neurotoxin and their removal, the company surmises, may be relevant for chronic and long term treatment.
L1146: IncobotulinumtoxinA is currently available and approved for treatment of cervical dystonia, blepharospasm and upper limb spasticity in Europe, Mexico, and Argentina. It has also been approved in Canada on February 4, 2010 for treatment of post stroke spasticity and dystonia.

None of these treatments have been approved by the FDA for the treatment of CD.

L1154: **Myobloc® is a serotype B toxin that is available in the United States and acts on the outside of the synaptic vesicle, cleaving the vesicle-associated membrane protein (synaptobrevin, VAMP) in contrast to the type A which acts on the inner surface of the postsynaptic membrane (SNAP-25).
L1155: ## 2.4 Safety Issues With Common to All Botulium Toxin Products
L1156: Cervical dystonia presents with clonic or tonic muscle contractions resulting in abnormal movements and postures of the head and neck. Successful treatment with intramuscular injection of the botulinum toxin is dependent on the site and dose. Regional or local spread of the toxin's biologic activity to the areas around the neck musculature may lead to dysphagia which is the most commonly reported adverse events in both double blind and open label studies following injection of Onabotulinumtoxin (Botox®) (19%), Rimabotulinumtoxin (Myobloc®) (10-25%) and AbobotulinumtoxinA (Dysport®) (15 to 39%) as reported in the clinical trials section of each drug's label.
L1157: More importantly, spread from the initial site of injection may produce systemic side effects which may be due to retrograde axonal transport in the spinal motor neurons (Garner CG, Straube A. Witt TN, et al Mov Disord 1993; 8: 33-37) or spread via the circulatory system. Post marketing reports have documented asthenia, generalized muscle weakness, diplopia, blurred vision, ptosis, dysphagia, dyspnea, dysarthria, urinary incontinence and breathing difficulties reported from hours to weeks after injection. Swallowing and breathing problems may pose a life threatening risk and there have been reports of death. The risk of symptoms is greatest in children treated for spasticity but symptoms can also occur in adults treated for spasticity and other conditions. These adverse events led to the addition of a "Boxed Warning" included in the label for all botulinum products marketed in the US by August 3, 2009.
L1158: Patients with myasthenia gravis, swallowing difficulties and muscle weakness are at increased risk for adverse events especially aspiration. Increased risk for adverse events may occur with normal treatment levels or with a history of previously tolerated treatment from botulinum toxin. There may also be increased systemic or muscle sensitivity to the botulinum toxins in some patients.
L1159: The product contains a small amount of human albumin, thus carrying the risk for viral infection transmission. A theoretical concern for Creutzfeld Jakob disease remains although this has never been reported to date,
L1160: ## 2.5 Summary of Presubmission Regulatory Activity Related to Submission.
L1161: ## **Pre-Phase 3 Meeting: September 14, 2004:**
L1162: - FDA advised the sponsor to provide more details on the manufacturing process from cell bank characterization up to purification which should be included in the future IND.
- FDA recommended discussing the assay method of the drug with the Office of Biological Products prior to deciding on antibody testing.
- FDA suggested (in response to the sponsor's proposal to initiate a phase 3 study for CD in the US --after conducting a phase 3 trial in CD and Blepharospasm (BPS) in Europe), that proposed studies should assess efficacy of NT 201 against placebo and should consider two additional studies to establish efficacy, obtain dose ranging data against
L1163: placebo and to study Botox-naïve patients. However, FDA explained that one very robust and persuasive dose ranging study in CD and another in BPS could provide sufficient evidence of efficacy for both indications and that both studies could be mutually supportive for both indications.
L1164: - FDA recommended using the TWSTRS total (b) (4)f as the primary endpoint. The Agency also recommended that the placebo period be extended to 6 to 8 weeks (b) (4) to develop adequate placebo controlled safety data.
- FDA recommended that all adverse events observed during the double blind or open label period should be followed until the event resolves or stabilizes.
- FDA also required submission of evidence that efficacy is sustained over time with re-treatment as well as safety data over time.

L1235: ## 4.3 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L1236: ## Safety Pharmacology:
L1237: The sponsor has made the following claims:
L1238: - The active pharmaceutical ingredient in NT 201 is [REDACTED] (b) (4), biotechnologically derived from C. botulinum and purified [REDACTED] (b) (4). This ingredient has been used by the sponsor in some of the animal toxicity studies that they have conducted. The extremely high potency of BoNT/A is illustrated by the fact that 1 LDU corresponds to less than 10 pg of [REDACTED] (b) (4).
- The intended dose per injection is up to [REDACTED] (b) (4) which is a maximum clinical dose of approximately [REDACTED] (b) (4); for a 60 kg patient.
- One (1) LDU (abbreviated to Units, U) is the quantity of BoNT/A which kills 50% of male mice by intraperitoneal injection, i.e. the mouse LD50.
- Most of the nonclinical pharmacology and toxicology studies with [REDACTED] (b) (4) and NT 201 were conducted in compliance with national GLP regulations and/or OECD guidelines.
L1239: ## **REVIEWER'S COMMENTS:**
L1240: - There have been no data provided on how [REDACTED] (b) (4) compares with NT 201 although the Sponsor mentions that NT 201 is the active pharmaceutical ingredient in [REDACTED] (b) (4).
- The conclusions below have been obtained from the Sponsor's non-clinical reports of studies done using NT 201 except for hERG channel screening report on cardiovascular effects with which the Sponsor tested concentrations of [REDACTED] (b) (4) instead of NT 201.
- **FDA Pharm Tox Reviewer will provide details and comments on the Sponsor's data.**
L1241: ## **Single dose toxicity studies**
L1242: These were done in mouse and Sprague Dawley rats. The LD-50 ranged from 50 Units (given intravenously) to 100,000 Units given orally with the maximum lethal dose causing death within
L1243: Anne E. A. Constantino, MD
L1244: NDA 125360-1
L1245: Botulinum Toxin A, Xeomin® IncobotulinumtoxinA
L1246: 48 hours to 5 days. All animals at 150 LDU/kg died 3 to 8 days after injection and one treated with 50 LDU/kg died on day 5. There was also a decrease in body weight and body weight gain at doses of 50 and 150 LDU/kg. The calculated LD-50 of 41 and 87 LDU/kg was established for male and female mice respectively. The results indicated that 5 LDU/kg can be considered as the NOAEL for a single intramuscular injections of NT 201 in mice, however, there was no LD-50 determined for the IM administration. In male Sprague-Dawley rats, maximum paralysis score was reached by all animals from 48 to 96 hours after treatment at 32 and 64 LDU/kg.
L1247: ## Repeat dose toxicity studies
L1248: Repeat dose toxicity studies done in the cynomolgus monkeys who were given doses as high as 12 LDU/kg in 4 weeks for 10 injections resulted in atrophied gastrocnemius muscle fibers. There were no treatment related effects on ophthalmology, cardiovascular function, food consumption, hematology, serum chemistry and urinary parameters. Based on this dose, the NOAEL for systemic toxicity is &gt;4 times higher than the maximal recommended human dose. Doses given above 10 LDU/kg every 2 to 4 weeks caused death when given intramuscularly in the rabbits.
L1249: ## Local tolerance toxicity
L1250: Local tolerance of NT 201 has been assessed by histopathological examination of the injection sites in the repeat dose intramuscular study in mice, rabbits and monkeys. No clinically relevant local injection site reactions after repeated intramuscular injection up to 40 LDU/kg (in mice, rabbit and monkeys) or repeated intradermal administration up to 8.34 LDU/kg (in rabbits).
L1251: ## Locomotor activity
L1252: The results were dose dependent in mice and the reduction in motility parameters was observed starting at 20 LDU/kg. The NOAEL for motility parameters after a single IV injection is 9 LDU/kg which is approximately 1.3 fold greater than maximum clinical dose in humans (7.24 LDU/kg for 60 kg patient).
L1253: ## CNS Toxicity
L1254: Studies in the mouse at repeated doses with an approximate LD50 of 50 LDU/kg showed clinical signs of piloerection, ptosis, lacrimation, mydriasis, reduced motility, muscular tonus, ataxia, hypothermia and dyspnea.
L1255: ## GI toxicity
L1256: The NOAEL of a single intramuscular injection of NT 201 for effects on gastro-intestinal motility in rats is 32 LDU/kg which is 4.5 fold greater than the maximum human clinical dose.
L1257: ## Genotoxicity
L1258: No mutagenicity studies have been conducted by the sponsor. The sponsor's rational for not conducting mutagenicity studies is based on the ICH guidance (S6 1996, Module 4, Section 4.3), "The range and type of genotoxicity studies routinely conducted for pharmaceuticals are not applicable to biotechnology derived pharmaceuticals and therefore are not needed."
L1259: ## Carcinogenicity
L1260: No carcinogenicity studies have been conducted based on a similar rationale as stated for genotoxicity.
L1261: ## Reproductive and developmental toxicity
L1262: The studies which were performed in rabbits and rats did not demonstrate reproductive toxicity up to a dose level that was associated with maternal toxicity. Maternal NOAEL was 30 LDU/kg and embryotoxicity NOAEL was 90 LDU/kg. (b) (4)
L1263: (b) (4)
L1264: ## Cardiovascular effects in vivo
L1265: Based on studies done by the sponsor, repeated intramuscular administration of dose levels at least two times higher than therapeutically recommended human doses in cynomolgus monkeys, did not show any deleterious effects on atria and intraventricular conduction velocity or ventricular repolarization. The NOAEL for cardiovascular effects in monkeys after four IM injections was 16 LDU/kg. However, a decrease in heart rate and variations in respiratory rate and QT intervals were noted two to three hours after treatment but this was attributed to ketamine hydrochloride anesthesia. Interestingly, one week after treatment, heart rate was markedly higher than during the pre-test period or during the day of treatment and this was associated with an increase in QTc interval duration corrected with Fridericia's formula but no effect was observed on the QTc interval length corrected with Basset's formula. The sponsor did not consider this as biologically relevant because the increase was less than 20 ms and only visible after correction with Fridericia's formula.
L1266: **In vitro hERG channel screening:** At a concentration of 10,000 LDU/ml, (b) (4) had no effect hERG tail currents at -20mV, which according to the sponsor, this concentration exceeds achievable concentration in human blood concentration. The sponsor further comments that the tested concentration of (b) (4) in the hERG assay exceeds the maximal achievable concentration in human blood by a factor of at least 10,000. Since (b) (4) was used in this particular study, extrapolating results from this study to make conclusions about in vitro hERG channel screening of NT 201 may not be appropriate because of insufficient data comparing the chemical and pharmacologic characteristics between these two preparations.
L1267: The Sponsor has made the following comparisons with the other FDA approved botulinum toxins:
L1268: - Similar to the diffusion of Botox and Dysport, IncobotulinumtoxinA injection into the mouse tibialis anterior, gastrocnemius or quadriceps muscle was very low.
- Systemic distribution of NT 201 is similar to that of Botox and Dysport based on their effects in single dose comparative intravenous toxicity study in mice.
L1269: - NT 201 pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics are comparable to Botox based on time course of onset, degree of paralysis and systemic body weight loss induced by intramuscular hindlimb injections in mice.
L1270: ## 4.4 Clinical Pharmacology
L1271: ## Mechanism of Action
L1272: ## Pharmacodynamics
L1273: Three Phase 1 pharmacodynamics studies were performed by the sponsor in healthy subjects. The sponsor claims, that from these studies, IncobotulinumtoxinA and BOTOX® were similar in terms of time course, degree of muscle paralysis and local diffusion into adjacent muscles. In a sponsor's study on area diffusion, Dysport® produced a greater area of diffusion in the forehead than IncobotulinumtoxinA when given in comparable doses and volume.
L1274: The Botulinum toxins inhibit cholinergic transmission at the neuromuscular and autonomic postganglionic synapses reducing excessive muscular activity or glandular secretions.
L1275: The mode of action can be divided into extracellular and intracellular components. The heavy chains bind specifically to extracellular structures on cholinergic nerve terminals. Through endocytosis, the light chain of the toxin is then transported across the vesicular membrane into the cytosol. The 4 step sequential process includes:
L1276: - docking to the binding site at the plasma membrane
- internalization of the BoNT/A molecule
- translocation into the cytosol of the zinc dependent endopeptidase part of BoNT/A
- proteolysis of synaptosomal associated protein 25 (SNAP 25), a component of the vesicle fusion machinery, which finally stops exocytosis of acetylcholine.
L1277: At the neuromuscular junction, the desired target blockade leads within about a week to retraction of the endplate nerve terminal and compensatory sprouting, with re-innervations and partial recovery of function within a month. Within 2 months, the poisoned terminals begin to exocytose acetylcholine again, leading to true recovery of endplate organization and withdrawal of collaterals. Within 3 months, there is recovery both morphologically and functionally in motor endplate. Clinically, when injected into the target tissue (skeletal muscle or gland), clinical effects are observed in two to five days, reach therapeutic effect at 10 to 14 days and resolve within 16 to 24 weeks. The figure below illustrates the important steps in its mechanism of action at the target organ.
L1278: ## Figure 2. Mechanism of Action of Botulinum Toxin A
## **Copyright Material**
L1279: Source: Amon SS, et al. J.Amer.Med. Assoc. 285: 1059, 2001
L1280: ## Pharmacokinetics
L1281: ## Distribution
L1282: The high chemical specificity and extreme potency of Botulinum neurotoxin limits PK profile studies in humans.
L1283: ## Absorption
L1284: After oral ingestion, the neurotoxin is absorbed in the gastrointestinal tract and taken up by cholinergic terminals leading to the toxic effects seen after ingestion.
L1285: ## Metabolism
L1286: After systemic absorption, the neurotoxin is rapidly metabolized to smaller molecular structures. White blood cells play a role in the disposal of circulating toxin type A as shown for other proteins. The neurotoxin is then degraded intracellularly. Like all proteins, Botulinum toxin type A is rapidly metabolized by proteases and molecular components are recycled through normal metabolic pathways.
L1287: ## Drug interactions:
L1288: Direct interactions of other drugs with botulinum toxin at the receptor or effector level have not been found. However, botulinum toxin A can potentially interact with any drug which has a peripheral cholinergic mode of action or with drugs that affect neuromuscular transmission like aminoglycosides or curare like compounds.

L1293: Study ID
MRZ 60201-0408/2	Extension Phase: To Evaluate the Safety and Efficacy of repeated injections of 2 doses of NT 201 over a one year period
Study Design	Randomized, Double Blind, Parallel Group Multi-center design
Entry Characteristics	
Following Visit 4 of the Main Phase, the subject was to be questioned every two weeks, either by phone or at a visit, about the need for a new injection. The interval between two visits was not to exceed 4 weeks. If a subject expressed via phone the need for a new injection, a visit was scheduled immediately. If the physician did not see the need for a new injection (the Toronto Western Spasmodic Torticollis Rating Scale [TWSTRS] Total score was <20), the subject was to be seen every 2 weeks from that time. In addition, the subject could return at any time up to Week 20 after the first injection, if he/she felt the need for a new injection session.
If the Investigator agreed that a new injection was required (the assessment of the TWSTRS score revealed a TWSTRS-Total score > 20), this represented the end of the MP for this subject and all assessments of the Final Visit (Visit 5) of the MP were performed. The MP for this subject was terminated before any further injections of study medication was administered.

Dose	120 units, 240 units (there were patients who were given less than 120 units; the maximum dose was 240 units)
Duration of Study	68 weeks (Last week of injection at week 48 and 20 weeks of safety evaluation)
EP (Extension Phase): up to 5 injection sessions, last injection not later than 48 wks with 20 week safety observation phase.
Primary Endpoints	Change in TWSTRS total score from previous injection
Secondary Endpoints	TWSTRS Disability, Severity and Pain Scores and PEGR
