BLA 125360
Xeomin ®, NT 201 (Incobotulinumtoxin A)
L1743: ## 1. RECOMMENDATIONS/RISK-BENEFIT ASSESSMENT
L1744: ## 1.1 Recommendation on Regulatory Action
L1745: For the indications of Benign Essential Blepharospasm (BEB), Cervical Dystonia (CD) (b) (4) review of the safety data for NT 201 (Xeomin ®) demonstrated an adverse event profile similar to other botulinum toxin products. Due to the small number of adverse events, a full analysis of effect modifiers and less common adverse events was not possible. However, the application allowed for an adequate safety review, and no safety concerns that would preclude approval were identified. Should NT 201 be found to be effective, I would recommend its approval and its addition to the other botulinum toxin products currently marketed in the United States.
L1746: ## 1.2 Risk Benefit Assessment
L1747: The safety review of the BEB, CD (b) (4) indications of NT 201 did not reveal any significant unexpected safety signals compared to other botulinum toxin products currently approved in the United States. If NT 201 is judged to be efficacious, the risk-benefit profile is acceptable and similar to other members of the drug class.
L1748: ## 1.3 Recommendations for Postmarket Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies
L1749: The following standard REMS Elements were submitted by the sponsor1:
L1750: - **Medication Guide:** A Medication Guide will be dispensed with each vial of XEOMIN® in accordance with 21 CFR 208.24. Pursuant to 21 CFR 208.24, the Medication Guide will be available in sufficient numbers e.g. the Medication Guide will be provided with each single vial of XEOMIN®. The label of each container or package of XEOMIN will include a prominent instruction to authorized dispensers to provide a Medication Guide to each patient to whom the drug is dispensed, and state how the Medication Guide is provided.
- **Communication Plan:** MERZ will implement a communication plan consisting of a Dear Healthcare Professional (DHP) Letter disseminated at the time of drug approval in the United States. The DHP letter will be distributed to neurologists, physiatrists and other specialties and healthcare professional staff who prescribe or inject Xeomin ® or other licensed botulinum toxin products (i.e., physical medical and rehabilitation specialists, dermatologists, ophthalmologists and other specialists who have previously dispensed botulinum toxins).
L1751: 1 The following paragraphs were adapted from the NT 201 Efficacy Review for BEB completed by Dr. Ken Bergman.
L1752: 3. **REMS Assessments:** Merz will submit REMS Assessments to FDA at (b) (4) 3 years, and 7 years from the date of the initial approval of the REMS. To facilitate inclusion of as much information as possible while allowing reasonable time to prepare the submission, the reporting interval covered by each assessment should conclude no earlier than 60 days before the submission date for that assessment. Merz will submit each assessment so that it will be received by the FDA on or before the due date.
L1753: **Reviewer comment:** This reviewer would recommend the following additions to product labeling and the Medication Guide:
L1754: - Although the safety review did not identify any instances of systemic spread of NT 201 effect, this was also true in previously approved botulinum toxin products in which systemic spread of toxin effect cases occurred following product approval. Due to this and the fact that some clinicians will likely use doses higher than those used in the clinical trial, the NT 201 labeling should include the same Boxed Warning on systemic spread of toxin effect as other approved botulinum toxin products.
- Because different formulations of botulinum toxin are not interchangeable, to avoid unintentional overdose the Division should consider some mechanism for alerting clinicians to this issue.
L1755: ## 1.4 Recommendations for Postmarket Requirements and Commitments
L1756: The following postmarket requirements should be considered:
L1757: - The sponsor should devise a plan to monitor for systemic spread when NT 201 is used in clinical practice. This should include literature searches, regular monitoring of the sponsor's postmarketing safety database and other options as applicable. Any case reports submitted to the sponsor resembling systemic spread should be forwarded to the Division in an expedited manner.
- For the ongoing and future spasticity studies, the sponsor should be asked to utilize checklists of adverse events related to botulinum toxin to ascertain adverse events. Vital signs within the NT 201 development program were largely measured prior to treatment (see Section 7.4.3.1), and thereafter 12 weeks subsequent to NT 201 administration and at a time when NT 201's effect has waned. The sponsor should be asked to perform a study examining vital signs at specified time periods after NT 201 treatment (including the time of maximum clinical effect). These could be included as part of another PMR/PMC study, if possible.
L1758: ## 2 Introduction and Regulatory Background
L1759: ## 2.1 Product Information
L1760: NT 201 is a purified formulation of Botulinum neurotoxin type A which is stated by the sponsor to be free from complexing proteins. The registered trade name for NT 201 for neurological indications is Xeomin®.
L1761: NT 201 is synthesized as a single chain polypeptide with a molecular weight of approximately 150 kDa, made using an (b) (4) strain of the anaerobic bacterium *Clostridium botulinum*. The NT 201 neurotoxin is produced as part of a high molecular weight complex (approximately 900 kDa) consisting of NT 201 and (b) (4) complexing proteins. During the manufacturing process, the toxin is (b) (4) purification (b) (4). The active ingredient represents the pure neurotoxin type A, which the sponsor asserts should be considered a new drug substance (Clinical Summary, pg. 4).
L1762: *Reviewer comment: As noted elsewhere in the review, I defer to the pharmacology reviewers to assess the sponsor's assertion that NT 201 is "free of complexing proteins."*
L1763: ## 2.2 Tables of Currently Available Treatments for Proposed Indications
L1764: The botulinum toxin preparations currently approved in the United States (US) are Botox®, Dysport® and Myobloc®. Botox is approved in the US for the treatment of CD, BEB, strabismus, hyperhidrosis, and glabellar lines. Myobloc® is approved in the United States for the treatment of CD and Dysport® is approved for cervical dystonia and glabellar frown lines (Clinical Summary, pg. 25). These products and indications are summarized in the table below.
L1765: Table 1: Botulinum Toxin Products Approved for use in the United States and their Indications

L1767: ## 2.3 Availability of Proposed Active Ingredient in the United States
L1768: NT 201 has not been approved, and is not available, in the United States. Although other botulinum toxin products have been approved in the United States (specifically Botox ®, Myobloc ® and Dysport ®), Merz notes that NT 201 differs from other botulinum toxin products in that it is a more purified form of the toxin. Botulinum toxin products have been approved in the US since Botox ® was first approved in 1989.
L1769: NT 201 has been approved in a number of countries outside of the United States, and these are discussed in Section 8 of this review.
L1770: ## 2.4 Important Safety Issues With Consideration to Related Drugs
L1771: During the pre-BLA, Merz and the FDA developed a list of Adverse Events of Special Interest (AESI). The list was based upon drug-specific adverse events that had been observed during the pre- and post-marketing period of other botulinum toxin products. The results of the examination of AESIs are discussed in Section 7.2.6 of this review.
L1772: The review of Botox ®, Dysport ®, and Myobloc® revealed several important safety issues specific to these drugs. Primary among these is the concern of systemic spread of toxin effect from the site of local injection. A review of post-marketing cases performed by the FDA's Office of Surveillance and Epidemiology in July 2008, identified cases of severe systemic spread of toxin effect. Subsequent analysis found cases of spread of toxin effect in unapproved uses and in approved indications at doses comparable to those used to treat cervical dystonia and at lower doses. A second AESI of interest with botulinum toxins is the development of blocking antibodies which may reduce efficacy with repeated administration. The immunogenicity of NT 201 is reviewed in Section 7.4.5.
L1773: In the Dysport ® review for cervical dystonia, the safety reviewer2 identified a number of common adverse events. The points below (ordered approximately by descending frequency) provide more detail on these events.
L1774: - **Muscular Weakness:** Muscular weakness was thought to result from excess of therapeutic effect involving the muscles of the neck, face and jaw. It was the most common event, reported in 18.2% of Dysport-treated subjects and 4.4% of placebo-treated subjects in double-blind studies (Odds Ratio 5.42, 95% CI 2.42-12.14, p&lt;0.0005) and in 28.9% of all Dysport-treated subjects. A few of these events (1.9% of Dysport-treated double-blind subjects and 3.6% of all Dysport-treated subjects but no placebo treated subjects) reported muscle weakness remote from the injection site. Muscle weakness also appeared to be dose-dependent, occurring in 28% of subjects assigned to 1000 U and 11% and 12% of subjects assigned to 250 U and 500 U, respectively, in the fixed dose study.
L1775: - **Dysphagia:** This was the most common serious event in post-marketing reports for Dysport® and severe dysphagia was also one of the most common serious events reported in clinical studies. Dysphagia may have been involved in the development of pneumonia and a death. Dysphagia was reported in 17.4% of Dysport®-treated subjects and 3.8% of placebo-treated subjects in double-blind studies (Odds Ratio 5.29, 95% CI 2.29-12.18, p&lt;0.0005) with increasing incidence by dosage in the fixed dose study. Dysphagia was reported by 29.1% of all Dysport-treated subjects in the principal cervical dystonia studies.
- **Dyspnea:** Dyspnea was reported in 5.0% of all Dysport®-treated subjects and respiratory complaints were a common serious complaint among Dysport® post-marketing reports (8.7% of serious adverse reactions involved dyspnea). This event was attributed to a weakening of accessory muscles of respiration in the neck following injections for cervical dystonia, perhaps in conjunction with pre-existing lung disease.
- **Blood Glucose Increased:** Due to limited laboratory testing in the Dysport® development program, elevated blood glucose levels was recognized in only a few patients. Despite the small number of patients, Dysport® was assessed as having a small effect on glucose metabolism for which the impact on patients with diabetes is unknown. The following statement was included in labeling, monitoring for elevated glucose in the post-marketing period was recommended.
L1776: “Subjects treated with DYSPORT® exhibited a small increase from baseline (0.23 mol/L) in mean blood glucose relative to placebo-treated subjects. This was not clinically significant among subjects in the development program but could be a factor in patients whose diabetes is difficult to control.”
L1777: Injection site adverse events as well as fatigue and flu-like syndromes were relatively common complaints in the Dysport® development program, although Dr. Stone noted that these were relatively evenly distributed among Dysport® and placebo-treated patients.
L1778: **Reviewer comment:** The adverse events listed were examined closely in the sections of this review addressing adverse events with NT 201.
L1779: ## 2.5 Summary of Pre-submission Regulatory Activity Related to Submission
L1780: The sponsor submitted NT 201 IND 12,821 for the treatment of cervical dystonia in December 2005, for the treatment of blepharospasm in May 2006 (b) (4).
L1781: The DNP met with the sponsor during the pre-submission period to provide feedback and recommendations on safety assessments, the primary of which are summarized below:
L1782: - Pre-Phase 3 Meeting: September 14, 2004
L1783: (b) (4)
L1784: - Facility Review Meeting: March 19, 2007: Merz submitted an information package that detailed the intended design and general operational procedures for a new parenterals facility to be constructed by Merz in Dessau, Germany.
- Type C Meeting for Cervical Dystonia and Blepharospasm: June 11, 2007
- Type B Pre-BLA December 12, 2008: Discussion of the administrative, quality, preclinical and clinical questions related to the BLA for the proposed indications cervical dystonia (b) (4)
L1785: Notable safety-related issues discussed during the pre-BLA period for NT 201 are summarized below:
L1786: - Due to a small elevation in blood glucose in drug- versus placebo-treated patients within the Dysport ® development program, the FDA requested that the sponsor perform expanded analyses of blood glucose.
- The FDA and the sponsor developed a list of Adverse Events of Special Interest (AESIs) based on adverse events observed with previously developed botulinum toxin products, and these were the focus of additional analyses. These particularly included AEs associated with systemic spread such as dysphagia, speech problems, facial weakness, general body weakness and dyspnea or other respiratory problems.
- (b) (4) the FDA recommended that the long term safety data which is up to 1 year of exposure must cover a full dose range for each indication (up to 400 units per treatment). Merz agreed to delay filing in order to include data from 60 patients exposed for 1 year at 400 units, 30 patients exposed for 1 year at 300 to 400 units and 30 patients exposed for one year at 200 to 300 units. In addition, 85 patients with cervical dystonia will have been treated for one year at 240 units. It was also agreed upon that the pooling of datasets should be for each indication and all across all indications and to include a subgroup analyses by dose, indication and duration of exposure.
- The sponsor had proposed recruiting patients who had previously responded to Botox ® for the pivotal and supporting trials. The FDA responded that "Reliance on a select responder population generally does not adequately support a claim of safety and efficacy in a more heterogeneous clinical population of patients."
- The Division noted the sponsor's concern regarding limiting the placebo period in order to minimize subject dropouts and allow for early re-injection of patients. The DNP concurred that an extensive placebo period may be problematic for patients. "However, it is highly desirable to develop adequate placebo- controlled safety data for NT-201 (which
L1787: is not available from the previous clinical investigations) and to measure the NT-201 duration of effect...For these reasons, the Agency strongly recommends extending the placebo period (b) (4) in the second study synopsis, preferably on the order of 6-8 weeks."
L1788: (b) (4)
L1789: The FDA further recommended that Merz discuss their antibody assay with our Office of Biological Products for their input on the best assay method.
L1790: - Specific ECG cardiac and pulmonary evaluations were not considered to be required unless particular concerns arose. However, the FDA emphasized that be specific and frequent evaluation for adverse events related to collateral spread of NT201 in these and other areas of adjacent muscles:, and so forth.
- Adverse events were to be followed until they stabilize or resolve, and were to be elicited for up to 4 weeks following the last injection.
- The FDA emphasized the need to conduct pre and post natal development studies and Sponsor should provide written justification if they are unable to provide these studies.
L1791: The IND for study of blepharospasm (as well as cervical dystonia) had been put on clinical hold because of a deficit in the non-clinical information needed to support the human clinical trials. This information was submitted and discussed with the Division and the clinical hold ended in June 2006. At the time the hold was lifted, the DNP also requested that methods to elicit AEs among study sites be standardized.
L1792: (b) (4)
L1793: After the BEB and CD indications were filed, FDA reviewers noted CMC issues that were not adequately addressed by the sponsor. These resulted in a delayed submission of these requirements, which extended the review clock to July 31, 2010.
L1794: ## 2.6 Other Relevant Background Information
L1795: The DNP's review of Dysport®, approved in 2009, is referenced for comparison in various sections of the review.
L1796: For the purpose of the safety review, it is notable that different formulations of botulinum toxin are not interchangeable with respect to dosing.
L1797: ## 3 Ethics and Good Clinical Practices
L1798: ## 3.1 Submission Quality and Integrity
L1799: ## 3.1.1 Quality
L1800: Although the quality and organization of the NT 201 BLA documents was generally compliant with eCTD guidelines and allowed for an adequate safety assessment, the application was lacking in several areas. These included:
L1801: - Information regarding exposure was poorly presented to the point that multiple requests for clarification were required in order to determine whether pre-specified exposure criteria had been met.
- Some documents, including study reports, were scanned and not able to be searched electronically. Searchable versions of these documents were requested and the sponsor provided these.
- Datasets that were difficult to utilize and lacking in basic components. The primary reviewers provided the sponsor with guidance on how these datasets should be structured, and the sponsor was generally compliant with these instructions.
L1802: The sponsor's proposed labeling was also inadequate, and is discussed further in Section 9.2 of this review.
L1803: ## 3.1.2 Integrity
L1804: Two domestic sites were inspected by the FDA's Division of Scientific Investigation on the basis of having enrolled the largest number of patients.3
L1805: - Alberto Vasequez, MD of Suncoast Neurological Associates, Inc. in St. Petersburg, FL was inspected as he had a high number of enrollments. DSI found that informed consents were obtained from all subjects prior to screening and entry into the study. DSI report "found no evidence of under reporting of adverse events" in this site.
- Paul Cullis, MD of St. John's Hospital Medical Center, Detroit, MI was another site inspected because of the high number of enrollments. The inspection covered 100% review of informed consent forms for the total 15 subjects screened at that site and the medical records were reviewed in depth. There were no deaths and one SAE was reported. The DSI investigation "found no evidence of under reporting of adverse events."
L1806: These two sites were chosen by the Quality Assurance GCP contracted by the Sponsor and were given Clinical Investigator Audit Certificates. The choice of audit sites and results of the internal audits conducted by the company was similar to those of the DSI reports.
L1807: ## 3.2 Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
L1808: The sponsor attested that all of the clinical trials were conducted in accordance with the ethical principles that have their origin in the Declaration of Helsinki, and that are consistent with Good Clinical Practice [GCP] and the applicable regulatory requirements. This is in compliance with 21 CFR 312 Parts 50 and 56, and ICH E6. Merz asserted that the NT 201 development program adhered to national and international ethical principles for protection of human subjects in clinical research trials.4
L1809: **Reviewer comment:** During the course of the safety review, this reviewer checked coding, source tables in study reports and case report forms, and did not find any instances in which Good Clinical Practices were violated.
L1810: ## 3.3 Financial Disclosures
L1811: **BEB Indication:** In the BEB indication, Merz provided the required information regarding financial disclosure for the confirmatory blepharospasm trial MRZ 6020-0433. Form FDA 3455

L1814: was reviewed for the investigators from 33 sites (31 of which enrolled patients). Three investigators received payments above the threshold for reporting at sites (b) (4) for duties related to the execution of the trial (administrative and training). These sites enrolled (b) patient(s) respectively. Site (b) (4) was also chosen for inspection by the Office of (4) Compliance, Division of Scientific Investigations and was found to be in compliance with GCP. Merz cited the measures taken to avoid the potential for bias at the affected clinical sites. The numbers of subjects at these sites are insufficient to alter the outcome of the trial.5

Financial disclosures were obtained from all the site investigators in the study done in the United States, and reviewed by Dr. Anne Constantino who did not identify any conflicts of interest noted. The three highest paid Principal Investigators enrolled a total of (b) (4) patients with (b) (4) patients receiving NT 201. This small number of patients was not sufficient to influence either the efficacy or safety results.

L1817: ## 4 Significant Safety Issues Related to Other Review Disciplines
L1818: ## 4.1 Chemistry Manufacturing and Controls
L1819: NT 201 is supplied as a lyophilized powder for reconstitution for injection, packed (b) (4) in glass vials. NT 201 is administered as an intramuscular injection (Clinical Overview, pg. 13).
L1820: ## 4.2 Clinical Microbiology
L1821: Merz stated that no microbial growth was observed in NT 201 vials challenged with 50 cfu of eight different test organisms derived from human skins. During a 72-hour stability test on sterility and endotoxins of microbial parameters of reconstituted NT 201 vials, the drug product was unaffected over a three day period at 2-8°C.
L1822: ## 4.3 Preclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L1823: Merz explained that the active ingredient in NT 201 is (b) (4), biotechnologically derived from C. botulinum and purified (b) (4). The sponsor used (b) (4) in a number of animal toxicity studies.
L1824: 5 Information in this paragraph adapted from the FDA's Review of Clinical Efficacy for the NT 201 Development Program, prepared by Dr. Ken Bergman.
L1825: 6 This paragraph adapted from the CD Clinical Review by Dr. Anne Constantino.
L1826: Other safety-related aspects of the preclinical pharmacology within the NT 201 development program were:
L1827: - A study in mice (Carli et al.)7 compared Xeomin® with two other botulinum toxins (Dysport® and Botox®) with regard to diffusion from the injection site. The authors concluded that “the three formulations exhibit a very moderate diffusion in nearby muscles from the injection site and only Dysport® shows a slightly wider mobility than Botox® and Xeomin®.
- Systemic distribution of NT 201 is similar to that of Botox and Dysport based on their effects in single dose comparative intravenous toxicity study in mice (Leuschner 1998).
- NT 201 pharmacodynamics (PD) and pharmacokinetics (PK) are comparable to Botox based on time course of onset, degree of paralysis and systemic body weight loss induced by intramuscular hindlimb injections in mice (Leuschner 1999).
- In four nonclinical pharmacokinetics studies conducted in mice, oral LD 50 was estimated to be about 100,000 LDU/animal, intravenous LD 50 for [REDACTED] was estimated to be 50 LDU/kg and intraperitoneal LD 50 is 50 LDU/kg. Oral bioavailability of [REDACTED] is 1:100000 or 0.001%.
- The sponsor conducted preclinical hERG channel testing, and found no effect of NT 201 at a concentration of 10,000 LDU/m (in vitro) or on ECGs (in monkeys). The hERG channel studies are discussed in more detail in Section 7.4.4 of this review.
L1828: Merz also conducted preclinical studies to examine NT 201 diffusion from the injection site. The studies found that diffusion could be up to 5 cm in a rabbit.8 EMGs detected electrophysiological changes in the extensor digitalis brevis (EDB) muscle in the foot of subjects who received BoNT injection in the head/neck region (Garner et al: Mov Dis 8(1) 33-37, 1993).
L1829: **Reviewer comment:** Please see Section 7.2.3 of this review (Special Animal and In Vitro Testing) for a summary of additional preclinical studies.
L1830: In her review, Dr. Anne Constantino raised the following concerns regarding preclinical pharmacology and toxicology testing:
L1831: “The sponsor has done multiple single dose toxicology studies with NT 201 on different species. In the 2 intramuscular studies that were done, the LD 50 was not determined. This would have been important because it would mimic the administration of the toxin in humans. No specific animal studies were designed to evaluate the presence of toxin spread. With the other toxins reporting the presence of adverse events from toxin spread, the Sponsor should have looked further into causes of toxin spread. There were studies done on mice that had documented results on [REDACTED] and implied results into the effects of NT 201. There has
L1832: 7 Carli L et al. An histological of diffusion of different botulinum neurotoxin type A injection in mice leg. Presented at Toxincon 2008, Volume 51, Supplement 1.
8 Borodic, et al: Head and Neck 12: 392-398, 1990
L1833: been no data provided on how (b) (4) compares with NT 201 although the Sponsor mentions that NT 201 is the active pharmaceutical ingredient in (b) (4).
L1834: ## 4.4 Clinical Pharmacology
L1835: ## 4.4.1 Mechanism of Action
L1836: Botulinum toxin type A blocks cholinergic transmission at the neuromuscular junction by inhibiting the release of acetylcholine stored in vesicles within the nerve terminal. This results in neuromuscular paralysis which is manifested as persistent (but not permanent) muscle relaxation. This pharmacological effect is responsible for the therapeutic effect of NT 201 in CD, BEB, and (b) (4). In CD, this local muscle relaxation is manifested as decreased abnormal movement of the head and neck. In BEB, the muscle relaxation is revealed as decreased frequency and forcefulness of eyelid closure. (b) (4) Merz stated that impulse transmission is re-established by the formation of new nerve terminals and motor endplates, or by recovery of the previous ones (Clinical Overview, pg. 4, 13).
L1837: The mechanism of action for NT 201 is illustrated in the following figure:
L1838: Figure 1: Botulinum Toxin's Mechanism of Action
(Adapted from FDA Figure 2, Clinical Review prepared by Dr. Ken Bergman, pg. 23)
L1839: **Figure 1: BoNT Mechanism of Action**
L1840: **Copyright Material**
L1841: ## 4.4.2 Pharmacodynamics
L1842: Merz characterized native Botulinum toxin type A as a high molecular weight complex, which, in addition to the neurotoxin (150 kDa), as well as other proteins such as hemagglutinins and non-hemagglutinins (Clinical Overview, pg. 14). The sponsor has asserted that NT 201 is "free of other complexing proteins."
L1843: **Reviewer comment:** *I defer to the FDA's pharmacology reviewers to evaluate the validity of the sponsor's statement that NT 201 is "free from complexing proteins."*
L1844: In single dose toxicology studies in mice, the LD50 for oral NT 201 was "10,000-fold greater than by the intravenous route,"
L1845: Merz stated that Botulinum toxin type A undergoes retrograde axonal transport after intramuscular injection. However, the sponsor stated that "transsynaptic passage of active Botulinum toxin type A into the central nervous system has not been found after intramuscular injection (Clinical Overview, pg. 14)."
L1846: Merz stated that receptor-bound Botulinum toxin type A is endocytosed into the nerve terminal prior to reaching its target protein (SNAP 25) and is eventually degraded intracellularly. Free circulating Botulinum toxin type A molecules that have not bound to pre-synaptic cholinergic nerve terminal receptors will be phagocytosed/pinocytosed and degraded like any other free circulating protein. The sponsor noted that in human patients, recovery from paralysis after intramuscular injection normally occurs within three to four months as nerve terminals sprout and reconnect with the muscle endplate (Clinical Overview, pg. 14).
L1847: Three Phase 1 pharmacodynamics studies were performed by the sponsor in healthy subjects. Based on these studies, the time course and degree of paralysis between NT 201 and BOTOX® were similar. Dysport® was reported to have shown a significantly greater area of diffusion than NT 201. There were no studies done to compare systemic spread of toxin effects and there was a paucity of reports of adverse events in the NDA submission for Xeomin9.
L1848: ## 4.4.3 Pharmacokinetics
L1849: ## 4.4.3.1. Distribution
L1850: The high chemical specificity and extreme potency of Botulinum neurotoxin limits PK profile studies in humans. Animal studies suggest that the radiolabeled neurotoxin complex remains at the intramuscular injection site (DasGupta, et al. 1994). Extent of diffusion is dependent on local anatomy, volume injected and dose. In animal studies, diffusion distance can be up to 5 cm (Borodic, et al. 1990). Systemic effects are inevitable after intramuscular injection because of penetration into the circulation as demonstrated by subclinical electromyographic changes (jitter) in muscles distant from the site of injection (Garner et al 1993)10.
L1851: 9 This paragraph adapted from the CD Clinical Review by Dr. Anne Constantino.
L1852: 10 Information in the Pharmacokinetics section of this review were adapted from the FDA's Review of Clinical Efficacy for the NT 201 Development Program, prepared by Dr. Ken Bergman.
L1853: ## 4.4.3.2. Absorption
L1854: After oral ingestion, the botulinum neurotoxin is absorbed in the gastrointestinal tract and taken up by cholinergic terminals, thus the toxicologic effects seen after ingestion. Gastrointestinal tract absorption is dependent on the presence of bound non-toxin proteins and hemagglutinins that appear to protect the neurotoxin from proteolytic degradation in the gastrointestinal tract (Fujinaga et al 1997).
L1855: **Reviewer comment:** Although the sponsor repeatedly describes NT 201 as "free of complexing proteins," the sponsor notes that native botulinum toxin contains "hemagglutinins and non-hemagglutinins" (Clinical Overview, pg. 14).
L1856: ## 4.4.3.3. Metabolism
L1857: After systemic absorption (in other words, toxin that was not bound at the site of intramuscular injection), the neurotoxin is rapidly metabolized to smaller molecular structures. White blood cells play a role in the disposal of circulating toxin type A as shown for other proteins. The neurotoxin is then degraded intracellularly. Like all proteins, Botulinum toxin type A is rapidly metabolized by proteases and molecular components are recycled through normal metabolic pathways.
L1858: ## 4.4.3.4. Drug interactions
L1859: Both bioavailability and storage ability of BoNT/A are affected by accompanying complex proteins (Schantz and Johnson 1994). Exogenous albumin is typically added to (b) (4) the BoNT/A (b) (4). Pharmacokinetic interactions between proteins and NT 201 after intramuscular injections are not expected.
L1860: **Reviewer comment:** The ability to diffuse up to 5 cm (depending on the volume injected) could explain the occurrence of dysphagia in both the BEB and CD indications.
L1861: The sponsor stated that classic kinetic and distribution studies could not be conducted with Botulinum toxin type A because the active substance is applied in such small quantities (picograms per injection), and binds so rapidly and irreversibly to cholinergic nerve terminals that no circulating NT 201 can be detected (Clinical Overview, pg. 14).

L1887: ## 5.2 Review Strategy
L1888: This review addresses three indications for NT 201: blepharospasm (BEB), cervical dystonia (CD) and spasticity (SP). Due to variation in administration (both for dose and site) and significant differences in the patient populations, the safety data in this review is generally subdivided by indication within each section. However, due to the relatively small number of adverse events, even among common adverse events, in most sections another sub-section analyzing the pooled data is included as well. The limitations of combining such diverse populations should be kept in mind when examining these pooled data sub-sections. For the indication specific sections, the review is generally further divided into sub-sections addressing the placebo-controlled data and the open-label data.
L1889: This review will follow FDA-recommended guidelines for the safety review, with a particular focus on deaths and SAEs (Sections 7.3.1 and 7.3.2). Adverse events of special interest (AESIs) due to their occurrence with other forms of botulinum toxin, especially spread of toxin effect, are closely examined (See Section 7.3.2 and 7.4.1 for examination of adverse events which contained any symptom of spread of toxin). Other FDA-recommended aspects of the safety review included in this document are:
L1890: - Evaluation of dose-response relationships with AEs (Section 7.5.2),
- Examination of potential effect modifiers such as age or gender (Section 7.5.3)
- An audit of the appropriateness of AE coding (Section 7.1)
L1891: The data within the 120 Day Safety Update were incorporated in this review, and material from the 120 Day Safety Update is referenced as such.
L1892: ## 5.3 Discussion of Individual Studies/Clinical Trials
L1893: **Reviewer comment:** Due to the large number of Phase 1 and 2 studies within the three combined indications, only Phase 3 studies are summarized below. I searched all the Phase 1 and 2 studies for SAEs, and found no notable results.
L1894: ## 5.3.1 Summary of Studies: BEB
L1895: The pivotal studies in the BEB development program are summarized below:
L1896: - **Study MRZ 60201-0433/1 (Main Period) and 60201-0433/2 (OLEX):** Prospective, double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized, multi-center trial with an open-label extension period to investigate the efficacy and safety of NT 201 in the treatment of Blepharospasm (ISS, Section 2.7.6)
L1897: Study 0433/1 and 0433/2 were conducted in 34 study centers in the United States and Canada. This study actively recruited patients previously treated with Botox® (as did Study 003). In the Main period of the study (0433/1), a single injection of up to 50 U of NT 201 was given (dose determined by investigator assessment). Patients were followed for 6 to 20 weeks post-treatment, depending on duration of treatment effect. If a new injection was required, the patient was transitioned to the OLEX period of the study. In the OLEX period, the patient could receive up to 5 injection sessions, with the dosing based upon the patient's stable response to the last two BOTOX® treatment sessions prior to entry into the trial. The dose was approximate due to the lack of interchangeability of botulinum toxin products, and difficulties in calculating the corresponding NT 201 dose. The doses and periorbital injection locations then approximated the patient's prior treatment with BOTOX®. In the open extension trial (0433/2) the patient continued the same regimen from the main period of the study.
L1898: - **Study MRZ 60201-0003:** Safety and efficacy of NT 201 compared to Botox (purified Botulinum Neurotoxin A-complex) in blepharospasm (ISS, Section 2.7.6)
L1899: The sponsor stated that Study 60201-0003 was conducted to “assess the safety of NT 201 compared to Botox in patients with blepharospasm.” This was a prospective, randomized, double-blind, parallel group, active-controlled, multicenter, multinational study. Recruitment criteria for patients included a diagnosis of BEB and at least two prior treatments with Botox®. A total of 303 patients from 41 centers in Europe and Israel were enrolled. At baseline (day 0), patients received an intramuscular injection of NT 201 or Botox with a maximum dose of 35 units per eye. Patients were monitored for up to 16 weeks following the injection. A control visit took place three weeks after baseline (day 21±1 day) and a final visit between days 109 and 112, or before, at the patient's
L1900: request. Optional intermediate visits were carried out only at the request of the patients (ISS Section 2.7.6).
L1901: ## 5.3.2 Summary of Studies: CD
L1902: 1. **Study MRZ 60201-0408/1 (Main Period) 60201-0408/2 (OLEX):** *Prospective, double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized, multi-center trial with a double-blind parallel-group extension period to investigate the efficacy and safety of different doses of NT 201 in the treatment of cervical dystonia (ISS, Section 2.7.6).*
L1903: This Phase 3, double-blind, placebo-controlled study was conducted in 37 study centers in the United States in a total of 233 patients with CD. The study enrolled patients aged 18 to 75 years with a clinical diagnosis of cervical dystonia and a Toronto Western Spasmodic Torticollis Rating Scale [TWSTRS]-Total score &gt;20, TWSTRS-Severity score &gt;10, TWSTRS-Disability score &gt;3, and a TWSTRS-Pain score &gt;1. The study recruited patients with or without a history of treatment with Botox ®.
L1904: Patients were randomized (1:1:1) to either 120 U NT 201 (N=78), 240 U NT 201 (N=78) or placebo (N=74). In the Main Period of CD Study of 0408 (0408/1) a single injection was given followed by 8 to 20 weeks post-treatment phase, depending on duration of treatment effect. If a new injection was required, the patients were transitioned into the Extension Period (0408/2) for up to 5 injection sessions; dosing interval depending on duration of treatment effect, but not shorter than 6 weeks and with the last injection not later than 48 weeks after start of EP.
L1905: In the 0408/2 OLEX period of the study, patients continuing from 0408/1 received up to 5 injections at least 6 weeks apart over 48 weeks, with 20 weeks of follow-up. The doses in the study were 120 U and 240 U.
L1906: In the placebo-controlled period, 4.9% of the 240 U patients had SAEs, compared to no SAEs in the 120 U and placebo groups. Total common adverse events were experienced by 55 % of patients receiving 240U, 55% of patients receiving 120U and 41% of patients receiving placebo. The most frequent AEs which exceeded placebo were dysphagia (11.6%), neck pain (8.6%), muscular weakness (6.4%), and injection site pain (6.4%).
L1907: **Reviewer comment:** *See Section of this review for further discussion of the common adverse events with the CD trials. As noted within that section, although overall common AE risk is similar between the dose groups, the risk in the AEs which are most likely to be drug-related (based on past botulinum toxin studies difference from placebo) do show a dose-response pattern.*
L1908: 2. **Study MRZ 60201-0013 : Safety and efficacy of NT 201 (highly purified Botulinum Neurotoxin A) compared to BOTOX® (purified Botulinum Neurotoxin A-complex) in cervical dystonia (ISS, Section 2.7.6)**
L1909: Study 60201-0013 was a double-blind, parallel-group study comparing the safety and efficacy of NT 201 to Botox®. It was conducted at sites in 11 countries: Austria, Belgium, Czech Rep, France, Germany, Hungary, Israel, Poland, Russia, Slovakia, and Sweden. Inclusion criteria for patient recruitment were diagnosis of cervical dystonia (and a sufficient score on the TWSTRS testing), last Botox® at least 10 weeks prior, aged 18 to &lt;75 and on a stable dose of any other drugs to treat dystonia for three month prior to enrollment. The NT 201 dose in the study was 70-300 Units and patient follow-up was for a maximum of 16 weeks.
L1910: Overall, 110 adverse events occurred in 65/231 (28.1%) patients in the NT 201 group as compared to 87 adverse events in 56/232 (24.1%) pts. in the Botox® group. SAEs were observed in 4/231 (1.7%) patients treated with NT 201 (cerebral concussion, vertigo, hematoma, ovarian tumor, worsening of eczema, metrorrhagia) and in 5/232 (2.2%) patients treated with Botox. The most common AE was dysphagia (10.8% in the NT 201 group and 8.2% in the Botox® group)(Study 0013 Final Report, pg. ).
L1911: 3. **Study MRZ 60201-0605/1 and 0605/2:** Prospective, single-arm, multicenter study to investigate the efficacy and safety of NT 201 (Botulinum neurotoxin type A free of complexing proteins) and the duration of treatment effect after one injection session and in long-term treatment in subjects with cervical dystonia.
L1912: The main period of the study enrolled adults 18 to 76 years of age with CD of the predominantly rotational form (i.e., spasmodic torticollis). Botulinum toxin pre-treated and treatment-naïve subjects were eligible for the study. Pre-treated subjects must have had a stable response to the two most recent Botulinum toxin injections, with a maximum dose of 300 U Botox or 1200 U Dysport per injection, and must not have received an injection within 10 weeks of study Baseline. All subjects were required to have minimum scores on the Toronto Western Spasmodic Torticollis Rating Scale (TWSTRS).
L1913: For the OLEX period, patients who completed the Main Period of the study, had their last treatment with Botulinum toxin \ge10 weeks ago, and (for women only) were not pregnant and were using highly effective methods of birth control, were eligible for participation in the OLEX Period.
L1914: Treatment in the main period of the study consisted of a single intramuscular (IM) administration of 50 to 300 U of NT 201. The number and sites of the injections were to be determined by the Investigator. A control visit for assessment of the primary efficacy variable took place four weeks following injection. In the main part of the study, subjects were followed for up to a total of four weeks.
L1915: During the OLEX period, patients received up to four additional injections of up to 200 U of NT 201 (in exceptional cases up to 300 U), depending on their need for a new injection. The date of the injection sessions, as well as the number and sites the muscles
L1916: to be injected, were determined by the Investigator. Injections were required to be at least 10 weeks apart. The follow-up period for the Main and OLEX Periods together was up to 121 weeks.
L1917: The most frequently observed TEAEs were dysphagia (9.2%), nasopharyngitis (6.6%), neck pain (5.3%), and headache (3.9%). Two patients experienced SAEs. TEAEs of special interest were observed in 7 subjects, and included dysphagia (7 subjects) and dry mouth (1 subject) (120 Day Safety Update, Appendix Group, pg.4).
L1918: ## 5.3.3 Summary of Studies: SP
L1919: ## **1. Study MRZ 60201-0410/1: Safety and efficacy of NT 201 compared to Placebo in Post Stroke Spasticity of the Upper Limb (ISS, Section 2.7.6).**
L1920: Study 0410/1 was a prospective, double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized, multi-center trial to assess the safety and efficacy of NT 201 compared with placebo in the treatment of post-stroke spasticity of the upper limb. It was conducted in the Czech Republic, Poland and Hungary. Naïve and pre-treated patients with post-stroke spasticity of the upper limb were recruited. For the patients with prior botulinum toxin treatment, the sponsor set the following criteria:
L1921: - The most recent injection with Botulinum Toxin must have been maximal 50 Units BOTOX® or 200 Units Dysport® or 2000 Units Neurobloc® (type B preparation) per each of these flexors: carpi ulnaris, digitorum superficialis, digitorum profundus.
- The most recent injection with Botulinum Toxin must have been maximal 60 Units BOTOX® or 240 Units Dysport® or 2400 Units Neurobloc (type B preparation) for flexor carpi radialis.
L1922: Patients received a single injection between 70 and 400 units of NT 201 and were followed for a minimum of 13 weeks and a maximum of 16 weeks. A total of 303 patients were enrolled. Of these, 300 patients were included in the ITT population and 256 patients in the TPP population.
L1923: Merz stated that a similar numbers of patients in each treatment group experienced at least 1 AE (NT 201: N = 21, Placebo: N = 20), slightly more AEs were reported in the NT 201 (43) relative to the placebo group (36). An AE of Special Interest (AE SI) occurred in one patient in the NT 201 group (nasopharyngitis).
L1924: ## **2. Study MRZ 60201-0410/2: Prospective, double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized, multi-center trial with an open-label extension period to investigate the efficacy and safety of NT 201 in the treatment of post-stroke spasticity of the upper limb (ISS, Section 2.7.6)**
L1925: Study 0410/2 was a prospective, double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized, multi-center trial with an open-label extension period to investigate the efficacy and safety of NT 201 in the treatment of post-stroke spasticity of the upper limb. It was conducted in Poland, Hungary and the Czech Republic.
L1926: Merz stated that a total of 145 patients proceeded from the Main Period of this study to continued NT 201 treatment during the OLEX Period. The inclusion criteria were the same as in Study 0410/1 above. The Main Period had a minimum duration of 13 weeks and maximum of 21 weeks. The OLEX Period had a minimum duration of 48 weeks and maximum of 69 weeks (\pm 3 days), depending on the time point of the last injection of trial medication.
L1927: During the OLEX Period, a total of 145 patients received up to five injection sessions with the intended dosages of up to 400 U NT 201 each. The mean cumulative dose administered in the OLEX Period was 1197.0 \pm 450.8 U NT 201.
L1928: For the combined main and open label data, the most frequently occurring AEs by preferred term were muscle spasticity (15 patients [10.3%]); upper respiratory infection and depression (both in 7 patients [4.8%]) gamma-glutamyl transferase increased and hypertension (both in 6 patients [4.1%]); epilepsy and muscular weakness (both in 5 patients [3.4%]); respiratory tract infection, urinary tract infection, and injection site pain (each in 4 patients [2.8%]); influenza, pain in extremity, pyrexia, hyperglycemia, and benign prostatic hyperplasia (each in 3 patients [2.1%]).
L1929: **Reviewer comment:** A discussion of hyperglycemia with NT 201 treatment is contained in Section 7.3.4.3 of this review.
L1930: ## 3. **Study MRZ 60201-0607/1:** *Prospective, randomized, observer-blind, parallel-group, multi center trial to assess efficacy and safety of two different dilutions of NT 201 in patients with upper limb spasticity (ISS, Section 2.7.6)*
L1931: Study 0607/1 was a prospective, randomized, observer-blind, parallel-group, multi-center trial to assess efficacy and safety of two different dilutions of NT 201 in patients with upper limb spasticity. The study was conducted in multiple European countries, including Austria, France, Germany, Italy, Portugal, Spain, Switzerland and the United Kingdom.
L1932: A total of 192 patients were enrolled. Of these, 95 were randomized to the 50 U/mL dilution group and 97 to the 20 U/mL dilution group. A total of 89 subjects in the 50 U/mL dilution group and 91 subjects in the 20 U/mL dilution group completed the study. The inclusion criteria for patients enrolled included:
L1933: - Being over 18-years-old
L1934: - Having with upper limb spasticity caused by either stroke, brain injury, spinal cord injury, multiple sclerosis or cerebral palsy, who had either been pre-treated or were treatment naïve stable upper limb spasticity for 6 months after stroke, brain injury or spinal cord injury, no relapse of multiple sclerosis within 3 months prior to the screening or cerebral palsy
- Disability Assessment Scale (DAS) \ge 2 points for primary therapeutic target at both screening and baseline visits
- For pre-treated patients only, source documentation of the most recent injection session with Botulinum toxin and sufficient therapeutic response for flexed wrist or flexed wrist and elbow. The total dose of most recent injection with Botulinum toxin must have been not more than 400 units BOTOX® (1600 units Dysport® or 16.000 units Neurobloc® [type B]) for the affected flexors of wrist or affected flexors of wrist and elbow.
L1935: Dosing in the study was determined by the Worldwide Education and Awareness of Movement Disorders [WE MOVE] for the corresponding clinical pattern of spasticity, with a maximum dose of 400 units. The patient received a single injection at baseline and efficacy and safety assessments were conducted over a period of 12 weeks. There was an additional follow-up of 8 weeks. If a new injection of non-trial botulinum toxin was administered between Week 12 and Week 20 the follow-up was performed at the time of administration.
L1936: In total, Merz stated 73 patients (38.0%) experienced treatment emergent adverse events (TEAEs), with a slightly higher rate 50 U/mL group (41 subjects [42.7%]) compared with the 20 U/mL group (32 subjects [33.3%]). SAEs occurred in 12 patients (6.3%): 6 patients in the 20 U/L group and 6 patients in the 50 U/L group. Injection hematoma was the most common adverse event.
L1937: ## 6 Review of Efficacy
L1938: The efficacy of treatment with NT 201 is evaluated in separate reviews by Dr. Ken Bergman (for blepharospasm), and Dr. Anne Constantino (for cervical dystonia). Additional information can be found in the Team Leader Memo prepared by Dr. G. Dave Podskalny.
L1939: This document examines NT 201 safety only.
L1940: ## 7 Review of Safety
L1941: ## Safety Summary
L1942: A summary of the safety review for NT 201 is presented below:
L1943: - **Deaths:** There were a total of six deaths in patients within the NT 201 development program for all indications (including the non-neurologic indications): two in patients treated with placebo, one in a patient treated with Botox® and three in patients treated with NT 201. The three deaths in the NT 201-treated patients all occurred within the spasticity (b) (4) studies, and all three were during the OLEX period. The causes of death were described as cardiac arrest, CVA and unknown. However, the deaths described as cardiac arrest and CVA both occurred suddenly so that there were no laboratory or imaging results to support this designation. The determination was apparently based on the patient's medical history.
- **Serious Adverse Events:** The SAEs in the NT 201 development program were generally those expected for the background population of the same age group. There were two SAEs of respiratory failure and dyspnea, but neither fit the clinical presentation of systemic botulinum toxin spread and both cases had clear alternate causes (such as post-surgery blood loss). There were 6 cases of epilepsy and 1 case of convulsion among the SAEs, all within the spasticity (b) (4). Some of these patients were known to have pre-existing epilepsy, but in others there was no history of epilepsy. CVA's may lead to subsequent seizures, and all of the patients in the SP (b) (4) had experienced a prior CVA. There has been a post-marketing report of anaphylaxis.
- **Common Adverse Events:****BEB:** Adverse events occurring in \ge 3\% of NT 201-treated subjects compared to placebo in Study 0433/1 Main Period were eyelid ptosis (19% vs. 9%), dry eye (16% vs. 12%), dry mouth (16% vs. 3%), diarrhea (8% vs. 0%), headache (7% vs. 3%), visual disturbance (7% vs. 6%), dyspnea (5% vs. 3%) and nasopharyngitis (5% vs. 3%). In the BEB open-label studies, the most common AEs in the repeated dose BEB studies were eyelid ptosis (18.9%), dry eye (16.2%), dry mouth (16.2%), and visual disturbance (6.8%).
- **CD:** In the placebo-controlled CD studies, the most frequent AEs compared to placebo were neck pain (10.7% NT 201 vs. 4.1% placebo), muscular weakness 14 (8.8% NT 201 vs. 1.4% placebo), musculoskeletal pain 8 (5.0% vs. 1.4%) and musculoskeletal stiffness (3.1% NT 201 vs. 1.4%). In the open-label studies, the most frequent events were dysphagia (34 of 214 subjects, 15.9%), sinus infection (7.5%), "common cold" (6.1%), headache and neck weakness (4.2%, each).
- **SP:** In the placebo-controlled SP studies, the AEs in the NT 201-treated group with the largest increase compared to placebo-treated patients were: headache (2.7% NT 201 versus 1.3% placebo), epilepsy (2.7% NT 201 versus 1.3% placebo) and hyperglycemia (4.1% NT 201 versus 0% placebo). In the open-label SP studies, the most frequent AEs were muscle spasticity (8.5%), depression (7.0%), upper respiratory tract infection (5.6%) and muscular weakness (4.2%).
L1944: - **Discontinuations:** Although the number of discontinuations due to AE is low in the NT 201 development program, it is consistent with that seen in other botulinum toxin development programs. The number of discontinuations due to adverse event was (0.5% (6/1313 patients) in the NT 201 treatment group, 0.3% (1/396 patients) Botox-treated subjects and 0.3% (1/346 patient) among placebo-treated patients. During the course of the safety review, I did not notice a pattern among the patients discontinuing due to adverse events. I audited the narratives of patients who discontinued for reasons other than adverse events (such as withdrawal of consent and loss to follow-up). See also “Evidence of Systemic Spread of Toxin” below.
- **Evidence of Systemic Spread of Toxin Effect:** There were no events containing multiple symptoms (such as respiratory failure, paralysis, the need for intensive inpatient care, etc.) of broad systemic botulinum toxin poisoning within the NT 201 development program for BEB, CD and SP.11 It should be noted, however, that there was also no signal for systemic spread of effect in the development program for Botox® and Dysport®, and cases only emerged during the postmarketing period.
L1945: One case that raised concern of systemic spread occurred in an SP patient (Patient 1142) who received NT 201 injection in the upper extremity, who twice experienced dysphagia following NT 201 treatment (310 U and 320 U for the first and second adverse events, respectively). Further, the event appeared temporally related to NT 201 administration, occurring nine days after treatment in the first event and one day after treatment in the second event. Time of resolution of the event was not reported in either case.
L1946: This reviewer recommends that the Boxed Warning describing systemic spread contained in the label of other botulinum toxin class members also be included in the NT 201 labeling.
L1947: - **Hyperglycemia:** Blood glucose was examined closely due to a small elevation of blood glucose in treated versus placebo patients in the Dysport® development program. The data linking NT 201 to an effect on glucose levels is mixed. There were cases of elevated blood glucose in NT 201-treated patients, including one in the spasticity (b) (4) that was classified as an SAE due to the need for hospitalization. Within the placebo-controlled trials, there were also more clinically significant elevated glucose values in the NT 201 compared to the placebo treated patients, but the overall number of patients with events in each group was small (For example, in the spasticity (b) (4), 3 cases in the NT 201 group compared to none in the placebo group). When mean glucose value was compared from the initial study visit to the primary endpoint visit and the final visit with the placebo-controlled trials, the values for NT 201, placebo and Botox® were similar (both over time and between groups).
- **Vital Signs:** Although one indication (BEB) showed a slight decrease in heart rate from baseline to final visits, this was not replicated in (b) (4) (CD and SP). The
L1948: 11 There were a number of adverse events relating to muscular weakness and dyspnea, but none fit the pattern of systemic botulinum toxin spread.
L1949: potential signal for decreased heart rate observed in the Dysport® development program does not appear to be replicated in the NT 201 development program.
L1950: - **Analysis for Effect Modifiers: Study Location:** The sponsor performed multiple analyses examining the association of age, gender, baseline disease severity and pre-treatment status with adverse events. Although variations in AE risk were observed for some of these factors, the number of events per subgroup was small and the effects were not replicated across indications.
L1951: As observed in other drug development programs, a higher number of reported adverse events were observed in the North American studies compared to those conducted outside North America. In many instances the risk of the common adverse events (overall and for specific indications) was approximately two- to three-fold higher in the North American countries compared to the non-North American countries. Consistent with the pattern seen in other drug development programs, the effect is more pronounced for the common adverse events than for serious adverse events.
L1952: ## 7.1 Methods
L1953: With the exception of the deaths and SAEs, in which the review is integrated across all indications (including non-neurologic), adverse events will be examined by indication. This is due to the differences in product administration and underlying patient population.
L1954: ## 7.1.1 Studies/Clinical Trials Used to Evaluate Safety
L1955: A table of all the studies which contributed safety data to the NT 201 development program is contained in Section 5.1 of this review. A more detailed text summary of the Phase 3 studies in the NT 201 development program is supplied in Section 5.2.
L1956: The manner in which adverse events are solicited in the clinical trials (open-ended questions, checklists, etc.) can affect the number of adverse events recorded. The sponsor was therefore requested to prepare a table summarizing the methods used to elicit adverse events in each study. The resulting table is shown below:

All AEs observed during the course of the trial were described and commented on in the CRF.

L1961: **Reviewer comment:** The methods used to elicit adverse events (as described in the table above), are adequate but not ideal. In Study 60201-9801 investigators were instructed to ask patients
L1962: *"Has your state of health worsened since you last visit?" This method of questioning could miss smaller adverse events if misinterpreted by the patient (For example, a patient may respond "no" to this question if his injection site pain and/or bruising has resolved but he now has a dry mouth, thinking that his state of health has not changed). The majority of studies, with the exception of BEB study 0433, appeared not to have specifically used checklists, to ask about known adverse events associated with botulinum toxin. Universal use of such checklists would have likely increased capture of adverse events (for instance, some patients may not mention certain adverse events associated with botulinum toxin to investigators believing that because this is already a known event it does not need to be tracked further).*
L1963: ## 7.1.2 Categorization of Adverse Events
L1964: Merz stated that treatment-emergent adverse events were defined as events with onset at the time of, or following, the first dose of study treatment. In open-label Study 0410/2 (SP), Merz noted that any untoward medical occurrence appearing beyond 20 weeks following the last injection was not considered a TEAE (ISS, pg. 13).
L1965: Sponsor definitions of “common” and “very common” adverse event are contained in Section 7.4.1.1 of this review.
L1966: The FDA and Merz developed a list of Adverse Events of Special Interest (AESIs) based on adverse events that were observed in the development programs or postmarketing period of other botulinum toxin products. These are described in more detail in Section 7.2.6 of this review.
L1967: An issue related to AE calculations is whether AEs are presented as a risk or a rate. In the BLA, Merz presents adverse events as risks (percent) only, and did not calculate time-adjusted rates. This could be a potential source of bias if NT 201-treated patients discontinued from the study more frequently than placebo patients, as the latter group would have a longer observation period in which AEs may occur. However, as shown in Section 7.3.3 (Drop-outs and/or Discontinuations) the percent of patients in the drug and placebo group were similar, and therefore the use of risk in the safety analysis is acceptable.
L1968: ## 7.1.2.1 Coding of Verbatim to Preferred Terms
L1969: **Reviewer comment:** In assessing the quality of the safety data within the NT 201 BLA, I audited the coding of verbatim terms to preferred terms (PTs) within the Xeomin BLA. I paid particular attention to the coding of adverse events of interest, including dysphagia and muscular paralysis. Due to the relatively small number of SAEs, I was able to review all SAE coding.
L1970: The review of coding highlighted the significant number of adverse events of hypertension. However, a comparison of hypertension in drug-treated and placebo patients showed equivalent values over time, and hypertension is a common event in an older age group such as in this database where the mean age was 50-52 years.
L1971: *I did not find any systemic problems in the AE coding, although there were some minor issues with the coding of some adverse events. The verbatim term “cold symptoms” or “head cold” was sometimes coded to “flu-like symptoms” although there are more specific, non-influenza preferred terms available. It was unclear if these were due to coding problems or due to poor translation to English. This splitting could obscure a number of similar adverse events, but I do not believe re-coding is necessary as this is a non-critical AE and in the pooled placebo-controlled trials the rates of upper respiratory infection, influenza, etc. were essentially equivalent between the placebo and NT 201 groups.*
L1972: Table 6: Coding Irregularities within the Xeomin BLA
L1973: (Adapted from Sponsor Analysis Dataset 5.3.5.1.25.3.1, File coding.xpt; BTC 60201-9801 for CD)
L1974: Verbatim Term/Terms	Preferred Term	Coding Issue
“Cat hair and NI – contact allergy”	“Split”	Unclear preferred term
“Systremia”	“Cramp in Limb”	Unclear verbatim term
“Hysterectomy due to excessive menhorragia”	“Excessive Menstruation”	Multi-term verbatim issue which ideally should be coded as both “menhorragia” and “hysterectomy”
“Head cold,” “cold,” “upper respiratory track infection”	“Influenza,” “nasopharyngitis,” “upper respiratory track infection”	Although most of the preferred terms which referred to “head cold” were coded to “nasopharyngitis,” others were coding to a variety of different verbatim terms.
L1975: An area of focus for the audit of AE coding was the issue of dry mouth and dysphagia in the Blepharospasm indication. Although dry mouth has been previously reported, and is therefore somewhat expected, with botulinum toxin use for blepharospasm, true dysphagia would not be expected with botulinum toxin administration for blepharospasm, and would be evidence of spread of toxin outside the area of administration. I therefore examined the verbatim terms related to dysphagia, dry mouth, and swallowing difficulties to ascertain whether these events were coded properly. I found no instances in which the preferred term did not adequately characterize the reported verbatim term.
L1976: ## 7.1.3 Pooling of Data Across Studies/Clinical Trials to Estimate and Compare Incidence
L1977: **Reviewer comment:** As noted elsewhere in the review, the patient demographic profiles for the NT 201 studies varied considerably by indications. Patients in the spasticity indication were older and were more likely to be male (60%) than in the other indications. The differences in the demographic profiles between studies are discussed in further detail in Section 7.21 of this review.
L1978: Due to these differences in patient population, as well as large differences in dose and site of injection between indications, pooling all NT 201 studies for analysis is problematic. However, to adequately capture all deaths and SAEs, and to generally increase the number of patients for subgroup analyses, this review does include analyses of pooled studies. The limitations previously noted should be considered when interpreting these analyses.
L1979: Merz documented the studies which were pooled to create the pooled safety database. For placebo-controlled studies, the six studies included were (ISS, pg. 6).
L1980: - Study 0408/1 (CD);
- Study 0410/1 (SP);
- Study 0433/1 (BEB);
- Study 0520/1 (Glabellar Frown Lines, GFL);
- Study 0617 (Crow's Feet, CF);
- Study 0527 (GFL).
L1981: For the controlled studies (both placebo and active) the 10 studies pooled were: (ISS, pg. 12)
L1982: - The 6 placebo-controlled studies in the list above
- Study 0607 (SP); control: different dilutions of NT 201
- Study 9801 (CD); control: Botox
- Study 0013 (CD); control: Botox
- Study 0003 (BEB); control: Botox
L1983: ## 7.2 Adequacy of Safety Assessments
L1984: As discussed in more detail in Section 7.2.6 of this review, because other botulinum toxins have been previously marketed in the United States, the NT 201 safety review benefitted from their prior pre- and post-marketing experience through the creation of an Adverse Event of Special Interest (AESI) list. The sponsor monitored for these events in the NT 201 development program, which aided the safety assessment.
L1985: **Reviewer comment:** To assess the adequacy of the sponsor's safety assessment, I examined the clinical testing and safety monitoring methods within the NT 201 studies. These are discussed in detail in Section 7.2.4 and Section 7.1.2 of this review, respectively.
L1986: An important element of the adequacy of safety assessments is that an adequate number of patients were exposed at the therapeutic dose range in order to be able to detect and interpret adverse events. The exposures in the three indications (BEB, CD and SP) are discussed in Section 7.2.1 of this review.
L1987: (b) (4)
L1988: (b) (4)
L1989: Section 3.1.1 of this review also summarizes some of the deficits in the sponsor's safety analysis. None of these deficits were sufficient to prevent an adequate safety analysis. Other minor deficiencies were addressed through requests for additional information to the sponsor, and are discussed throughout the review.
L1990: ## 7.2.1 Overall Exposure at Appropriate Doses/Durations and Demographics of Target Populations
L1991: **Reviewer comment:** For chronically administered drugs, ICH guidelines recommend that 1500 patients should be treated overall in a drug development program, 300 to 600 patients be treated for six months, and 100 patients be treated for one year. These exposures should occur at the dose or dose range believed to be efficacious.
L1992: It is notable that none of the three neurologic indications reviewed in this document (BEB, CD, and SP) met these ICH guidelines for adequate exposure. In fact, the NT 201 development program does not meet these guidelines even when all of the indications, including non-neurologic indications, are combined (there are a total of 1313 NT 201-exposed patients in the pooled studies for the NT 201 development program). NT 201 is not chronically administered in the sense that it is an intermittent injection, and so exposure guidelines other than the ICH guidelines for chronically administered drugs may apply. During the pre-BLA period, the FDA discussed appropriate exposure guidelines with Merz. The Division's communication with Merz regarding the FDA's expectations for drug exposure
L1993: (b) (4)

L2046: **Reviewer comment:** The sponsor's table above does not account for time between injections. For instance, if the patient receives one dose and is followed for a year, this should not be considered a year of exposure.
L2047: The sponsor reported that in the "Main and OLEX Periods of Study 0433 combined over half of the subjects had received 5 or 6 doses of study medication, and were followed for 48 weeks or more. (BEB ISS)." As of the time of this review, information from the sponsor is pending regarding the number of patients receiving multiple consecutive doses at specific dose levels.
L2048: **Reviewer comment:** Using study data, the primary reviewer for the BEB indication, Dr. Ken Bergman, prepared the following summary of cumulative NT 201 exposure.
L2049: "Using the datasets for Trials 0433/1 and 0433/2, the intervals between sequentially administered treatments (numbers of days from one injection to the next injection) were summed. The interval of exposure to active drug in the double blind trial was carried over into the open label extension as these patients were rolled over without interruption in treatments.
L2050: This summed exposure for each subject was converted to weeks to get total duration of exposure. The rounding was downward in direction, e.g. 7 weeks and 6 days was considered 7 weeks. If a subject was lost to follow-up and had no final visit for that treatment session, that injection dose and its interval were dropped from the exposure data set.
L2051: Subjects with at least 24 weeks and 48 weeks continuous observation were taken to be supportive of exposure to the lowest dose in that interval. I looked at all combinations to see if a given dose exposure might be supported at some middle interval over the course of a subject's treatment. For example if a subject received 4 injections of 90, 100, 100, and 90 units at intervals over a year, it might also be supportive of 100 units at 6 months
L2052: exposure, but also 90 units at 48 weeks exposure. (The doses are expressed as deciles, e.g.: a dose between 90 and 99 units supported a 90 unit dose).”

L2114: ## 7.2.2 Explorations for Dose Response
L2115: **Reviewer comment:** Dose-response analyses should ideally be performed in fixed dose studies, due to confounding by disease severity in flexible dose studies. This limitation should be kept in mind with respect to dose-response analyses in the NT 201 development program.
L2116: ## 7.2.2.1 Dose Response: Deaths
L2117: Due to the small number of deaths (only three in NT 201-treated patients), a credible dose-response analysis could not be performed.

L2126: The pooled, controlled studies were: 0498/1 (CD) Main Period, 0013 (CD), 9801 (CD), 0410/1 (SP) Main Period, 0607 (SP), 0433/1 (BEB) Main Period, 0003 (BEB), 0520/1 (GFL) Main Period, 0527 (GFL), and 0617 (CF). L2127: Source: Table 3.3.1.1.1 and Table 3.3.9.1.1. Section 3.3.5.3. Appendix H.
L2128: Merz cautioned that because the dose of NT 201 varied widely across indications, with oldest (i.e., the BEB population) and sickest (i.e., the post-stroke SP population) subjects represented primarily in the lowest and highest dose groups, it is difficult to assess the incidence of SAEs by total dose group in the pooled, controlled studies. However, when SAEs are examined by total NT 201 dose at the SOC and PT levels, the sponsor stated that the number of individual SAEs was low (most events occurred in only one subject), and no pattern of increased incidence of certain events with higher NT 201 dose could be discerned (see Table 1, ISS Appendix D).
L2129: **Reviewer comment:** I concur with the sponsor's assessment that there are too few events to support a dose-response analysis for SAEs, and that general review of the SAEs does not demonstrate any particular pattern.
L2130: ## 7.2.2.2.2 Dose Response: SAEs: BEB
L2131: In the Study 0433/1 Main Period and 0433/2 OLEX Period, Merz reported that the incidence of SAEs was higher in subjects who received 10 to 40 U of NT 201, compared with those who received &gt;40 U of NT 201. Six of 27 subjects in the 10 to 40 U group (22.2%), compared with 4 of 79 subjects in the &gt;40 U group (5.1%), experienced SAEs. Although it appears that the incidence of SAEs does not increase with higher NT 201 doses in the treatment of BEB, because
L2132: of the small number of subjects with SAEs, it is not possible to draw reliable conclusions based on these data (BEB ISS 120 Day Safety Update, pg. 23).
L2133: ## 7.2.2.2.3 Dose Response: SAEs: CD
L2134: In the Study 0408/1 Main Period and 0408/2 Extension Period (analyzed together), Merz stated that there were 15 SAEs. These 15 included:
L2135: - 2 of 48 subjects (4.2%) in the \le 120 U treatment group
- 10 of 118 subjects (8.5%) in the &gt;120 to 240 U group
- 3 of 61 subjects (4.9%) in the group whose dose in Study 0408/2 Extension Period remains blinded (Table 2.2.1.1, ISS Appendix J).
L2136: From the limited dose data available, the sponsor concluded that SAEs do not increase with higher NT 201 dose (CD ISS, pg 33).
L2137: **Reviewer comment:** As noted previously, the number of SAEs in any single indication is insufficient to support a dose-response analysis. An analysis of pooled data by SOC, rather than individual SAEs is presented in Section 7.2.2.2.1 of this review.
L2138: ## 7.2.2.2.4 Dose Response: SAEs: SP
L2139: In the pooled SP studies (the Study 0410/1 Main Period and Study 0607), in the NT 201 treatment group 1 of 16 subjects (6.3%) receiving \le 120 U, 3 of 67 subjects (4.5%) receiving &gt;120 to 240 U, and 7 of 121 subjects (5.8%) receiving &gt;240 U experienced SAEs (SP ISS, pg. 33).
L2140: **Reviewer comment:** The risk of SAEs in SP patients treated with NT 201 did not demonstrate a dose-response pattern, but the analysis was based on a small number of patients and events.
L2141: ## 7.2.2.3 Dose Response: Common Adverse Events
L2142: ## 7.2.2.3.1 Dose Response: Common Adverse Events: Pooled Indications
L2143: **Reviewer comment:** Due to the differences in dosing for the various indications, pooled data should not be used in a dose-response analysis. The effect of dose on common adverse events is therefore discussed in the individual indications below.

L2154: ## 7.2.2.3.3 Dose Response: Common Adverse Events: CD
L2155: Merz reported that common treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) in any NT 201 treatment group) observed in patients who received NT 201 (120 U; 240 U) or placebo in the CD indication included dysphagia (13%; 19%, 3%, respectively), neck pain (6%; 15%, 4%, respectively), muscle weakness (6%; 11%, 1%, respectively), injection site pain (9%; 4%, 7%, respectively), musculoskeletal pain (6%; 4%, 1%, respectively), nausea (3%; 5%, 0%, respectively), headache (3%; 5%, 4%, respectively), and musculoskeletal stiffness (1%; 5%, 1%, respectively)(Clinical Overview, pg. 28).
L2156: **Reviewer comment:** In the list of adverse events above, with the exception of injection site pain, all of the adverse events show a greater risk when patients are treated with 240 U compared to 120 U, and in all cases the risk with 240 U substantially exceeds placebo.
L2157: *Of note is the relatively large number of patients who experience dysphagia when treated with 240 U, nearly 20%, or one in five. The risk of nausea in patients treated with 120 U (3%) and 240 U (5%) was also elevated compared to placebo (0%). These two events raise the question of whether they represent evidence of systemic spread beyond the injection site. Dysphagia is commonly observed after treatment for CD in other development programs. Dysphagia was the most frequently reported adverse reaction in clinical trials for Botox® and one of the most common in the double blind placebo controlled trials for Dysport®, according to the labeling for those drugs. However, as shown in the table below, the number of patients experiencing these events was small (only 6 NT 201-treated CD patients reported nausea), and the risk estimations are therefore not very robust. For further consideration of whether a dose-response effect is apparent for NT 201 CD development program with respect to adverse events, please see also Section 7.2.2 of this review*

L2163: **Reviewer comment:** In the table above, the overall risk of common adverse event for CD patients is similar across doses: 57% in the 120 U group, 55% in the 240 U group and 42% in placebo. However, when restricted to common adverse events most likely to be caused by the drug (based on the frequency of events, the difference from placebo and past experience with other botulinum toxin products), these events appear to demonstrate a dose-response (For example dysphagia (13% 120U; 19% 240 U, 3% placebo), neck pain (6% 120 U, 15% 240 U, 4% placebo), muscle weakness (6% 120 U; 11% 240 U, 1% placebo).
L2164: I believe that there is a dose-response effect demonstrated within this study, with the 240 U dose having a greater risk of adverse events. I believe the reason that the overall risk is similar for the 120 U and 240 U groups is that the table above includes many incidental adverse events unlikely to be drug related (somnolence, sinusitis, syncope, etc) and these obscure the dose-response in the adverse events that are most likely to be drug related.
